Microlocal Analysis — Pseudodifferential Operators, Wavefront Sets & the Fourier Integral

Microlocal analysis studies linear partial differential equations at the level of "directions" in phase space — the cotangent bundle T*X of a manifold. By refining the concept of singularities from points in space to points in phase space, it reveals the fine structure of solutions to PDEs. Central objects include pseudodifferential operators (ΨDOs), which generalize differential operators with symbol calculus; the wavefront set of a distribution, which captures both the location and the direction of singularities; and Fourier integral operators (FIOs), which move singularities along canonical transformations. Landmark results include the Duistermaat-Hörmander propagation of singularities theorem and the Melrose b-calculus for manifolds with boundary, culminating in the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theorem.

"To understand a singularity, we must look in all directions."
— Lars Hörmander
Part 1

📏 Pseudodifferential Operators and Symbols

ΨDOs
Symbol Classes, Quantization, and the Calculus of Pseudodifferential Operators
A pseudodifferential operator a(x,D) acts on functions by the oscillatory integral a(x,D)f(x) = (2π)^{-n} ∫ e^{i⟨x,ξ⟩} a(x,ξ) ̂f(ξ) dξ, where a(x,ξ) is the symbol. The Hörmander symbol classes S^m_{ρ,δ} consist of a ∈ C^∞(Ω×ℝⁿ) satisfying |∂^β_x ∂^α_ξ a(x,ξ)| ≤ C_{αβ} (1+|ξ|)^{m−ρ|α|+δ|β|} for all multi-indices α,β. Here 0 ≤ δ < ρ ≤ 1, and m is the order. The principal symbol σ_m(a)(x,ξ) = lim_{t→∞} t^{-m} a(x,tξ) captures the leading-order homogenous behavior. The composition formula: the symbol of a(x,D)∘b(x,D) is given by the Leibniz product a♯b ∼ Σ_α (∂^α_ξ a)(D^α_x b)/α! (the Kohn-Nirenberg product). An operator is elliptic of order m if its principal symbol satisfies |a(x,ξ)| ≥ c|ξ|^m for large ξ. An elliptic operator admits a parametrix — a two-sided inverse modulo smoothing operators (those with symbols in S^{-∞}). This is the foundation of elliptic regularity: if P is elliptic and Pu is smooth, then u is smooth.
Pseudodifferential operators extend differential operators by allowing symbols that are not polynomials in ξ; their symbol calculus mirrors the algebra of differential operators with powerful asymptotic control.
Part 2

🌍 Wavefront Sets and Microlocal Regularity

Wavefront
The Wavefront Set, Microlocal Smoothness, and Propagation of Singularities
The wavefront set WF(u) ⊂ T^*X\0 of a distribution u refines the singular support: (x₀,ξ₀) ∉ WF(u) iff there exists a cutoff φ ∈ C^∞_c with φ(x₀)≠0 and a conic neighborhood Γ of ξ₀ such that |(φu)^(ξ)| ≤ C_N(1+|ξ|)^{-N} for all N in Γ. In other words, u is microlocally smooth at (x₀,ξ₀) if the Fourier transform decays rapidly in a cone of directions. The microlocal smoothing property: if WF(u) ∩ ell(a) = ∅ (where ell(a) = {(x,ξ) : σ(a) ≠ 0} is the elliptic set), then a(x,D)u is smooth. The propagation of singularities theorem (Duistermaat-Hörmander, 1972): let P be a real principal type operator with principal symbol p (real, homogeneous of degree m) and characteristic set Σ = {p = 0}. For Pu = f, we have WF(u)\WF(f) ⊂ Σ, and moreover WF(u) is a union of bicharacteristic strips — the integral curves of the Hamilton vector field H_p = Σ (∂p/∂ξ_i ∂/∂x_i − ∂p/∂x_i ∂/∂ξ_i) on T^*X\0. This means singularities travel along null bicharacteristics of P. The microlocal Holmgren uniqueness theorem says that if Pu = 0 and u vanishes on one side of a non-characteristic hypersurface, then u vanishes microlocally near the hypersurface.
The wavefront set records both where singularities occur and in which directions they oscillate; the propagation theorem shows that singularities flow along Hamiltonian trajectories in phase space.
Part 3

🧮 Fourier Integral Operators

FIOs
Canonical Relations, the Hörmander-Egorov Theorem, and Parametrices
A Fourier integral operator (FIO) A: C^∞(Y) → D'(X) of order m with canonical relation Λ ⊂ T^*X × T^*Y is locally given by A f(x) = ∫ e^{iφ(x,y,θ)} a(x,y,θ) f(y) dy dθ, where φ is a non-degenerate phase function (homogeneous of degree 1 in θ and with dφ non-vanishing on the critical set C_φ = {(x,y,θ): d_θ φ = 0}). The canonical relation Λ = {(x,d_x φ; y,−d_y φ) : (x,y,θ) ∈ C_φ}. The Hörmander-Egorov theorem: if F is an FIO whose canonical relation is the graph of a canonical transformation χ: T^*Y → T^*X, then F^* P F is a ΨDO on Y with principal symbol p∘χ. This conjugates pseudodifferential operators by canonical transformations, a cornerstone of microlocal reduction. The composition of FIOs corresponds to composition of canonical relations under a clean intersection condition. The solution operator to the wave equation ∂²_t u − Δ u = 0 is a FIO: the fundamental solution E(t) has WF(E(t)) = {(x,ξ; y,−η)} where (x,ξ) and (y,η) are related by geodesic flow. The L²-boundedness of FIOs holds under the graph condition: the canonical relation is a local canonical graph.
Fourier integral operators generalize ΨDOs by allowing phase functions beyond linear ones; they transport singularities along canonical transformations, making them the natural tool for solving hyperbolic equations.
Part 4

🔥 b-Calculus and Index Theory

b-Calculus
Melrose b-Calculus, the APS Index Theorem, and Analytic Torsion
The Melrose b-calculus provides a pseudodifferential framework for manifolds with boundary. A b-vector field on a manifold X with boundary ∂X is a vector field tangent to the boundary. The b-cotangent bundle ^bT^*X is the dual of the b-tangent bundle, where boundary-defining functions x introduce a degeneracy. The b-pseudodifferential operators form a calculus closed under composition, with a b-wavefront set that respects the boundary structure. The b-index theorem: for Dirac-type operators on manifolds with boundary, the index is given by the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer (APS) index theorem: ind(D) = ∫_M ̂A(Ω) + η(∂M), where η(∂M) is the eta invariant — a spectral invariant measuring the asymmetry of the spectrum of the boundary operator. The adiabatic limit relates the APS index to the b-calculus through stretching the boundary. The Cheeger-Müller theorem equates the analytic torsion (Ray-Singer torsion) with the Reidemeister torsion, a topological invariant. The Witten deformation — deforming the de Rham complex by d_t = e^{-tf} d e^{tf} for a Morse function f — provides analytic proofs of Morse inequalities via supersymmetric quantum mechanics, with instantons connecting critical points. Fukaya's Floer homology has deep connections to microlocal analysis through the study of Lagrangian intersections and the Morse theory of the action functional.
The b-calculus tames boundary singularities by adapting the pseudodifferential machinery to manifolds with boundary, leading to index theorems and connections to topological invariants.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

Composition and the Symbolic Calculus of Pseudodifferential Operators

The composition of two pseudodifferential operators A ∈ Ψm(X) and B ∈ Ψm′(X) is again a pseudodifferential operator of order m+m′, and its full symbol admits an asymptotic expansion governed by the Leibniz rule. In the Kohn–Nirenberg quantization, if a(x,ξ) and b(x,ξ) are the symbols of A and B, the symbol c(x,ξ) of C = A∘B satisfies c(x,ξ) ∼ Σα (i|α|/α!) ∂αξa(x,ξ) ∂αxb(x,ξ). The principal symbol is simply the product σm+m′(C) = σm(A) σm′(B). The subprincipal symbol acquires a correction term involving the Poisson bracket: σsub(C) = σsub(A) σpr(B) + σpr(A) σsub(B) + (i/2){σpr(A), σpr(B)}. This formula underlies the microlocal proof of Egorov’s theorem: for a real principal type operator P, the conjugation by a Fourier integral operator associated with a canonical transformation χ yields a pseudodifferential operator whose principal symbol is p∘χ. The symbolic calculus extends to manifolds via coordinate patches and a partition of unity; the resulting algebra Ψ*(X) is filtered by order and its associated graded algebra is the commutative algebra of symbols modulo smoothing operators. The commutator [A,B] has principal symbol (i){σpr(A), σpr(B)}, linking the algebraic structure to the symplectic geometry of T*X. The asymptotic summation lemma (Borel’s lemma) guarantees that any formal asymptotic sum of symbols can be realized as the symbol of an operator, unique up to smoothing operators. This completeness is essential for constructing parametrices of elliptic operators: if P is elliptic, one builds a symbol q ∼ p−1 + lower order terms such that QP = I − R with R smoothing. The calculus also accommodates operators with parameter dependence, leading to the parameter-dependent pseudodifferential calculus used in the proof of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem via the heat kernel.

The Boutet de Monvel Calculus for Boundary Value Problems

On a compact manifold X with boundary ∂X, the standard pseudodifferential calculus fails to capture the boundary conditions of elliptic problems. The Boutet de Monvel calculus replaces Ψ*(X) by a matrix algebra of operators acting on the direct sum C∞(X) ⊕ C∞(∂X). An element of this calculus is a 2×2 matrix (P++G, K; T, S) where P+ is the truncation of a pseudodifferential operator on the double of X to the interior, G is a singular Green operator (smoothing up to the boundary but with a specific singularity structure), K is a Poisson operator (solving the homogeneous equation with given boundary data), T is a trace operator (restricting a function to the boundary with a pseudodifferential weight), and S is a pseudodifferential operator on the boundary. The composition rules are dictated by the Mellin transform in the normal variable: near the boundary, one writes x≥0 for a boundary defining function and uses the Mellin symbol σM(G)(z) which is holomorphic in a strip. The calculus is closed under composition and adjoints, and it admits a symbol hierarchy: the interior principal symbol σψ(P), the boundary principal symbol σ∂(P) (a family of operators on the half-line), and the boundary symbol of the Green, Poisson, and trace parts. Ellipticity in this calculus means invertibility of all three symbol levels. For an elliptic boundary value problem, the parametrix belongs to the calculus, and the index is computed by the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer formula: ind(D) = ∫X Â(Ω) + η(∂X), where η is the eta invariant of the boundary operator. The Boutet de Monvel calculus also provides a framework for the Calderón projector, which splits the Cauchy data space into the Cauchy data of solutions and the orthogonal complement, and it underlies the proof of the gluing formula for the eta invariant.

Worked Example: Composition in the Boutet de Monvel Calculus
Let A = (P++G, K; T, S) and B = (P′++G′, K′; T′, S′) be two operators in the Boutet de Monvel calculus of order m and m′ respectively. Write the (1,1) entry of the composition A∘B in terms of the components, indicating which terms are singular Green operators and which are truncated pseudodifferential operators.
The (1,1) entry of A∘B is (P++G)(P′++G′) + K T′. Expand: P+P′+ = (PP′)+ + G1 where G1 is a singular Green operator arising from the boundary terms of the composition of truncated operators. P+G′ and G P′+ are singular Green operators because the product of a truncated ΨDO with a singular Green operator remains in the Green class. G G′ is also a singular Green operator (the Green operators form an ideal). The term K T′ is a composition of a Poisson operator with a trace operator; it yields a singular Green operator as well. Hence the (1,1) entry is a sum of a truncated pseudodifferential operator (PP′)+ and a singular Green operator (the sum of G1, P+G′, G P′+, G G′, K T′). This structure shows that the Boutet de Monvel algebra is closed under composition.
Exam Strategy: Symbol Hierarchies
When a question asks for the symbol of a composition or commutator, first identify the principal symbols and their orders. Write the asymptotic expansion up to the required order; the subprincipal term often involves the Poisson bracket. For boundary calculi, remember the three symbol levels: interior, boundary, and Mellin. A common trap is to forget the boundary symbol of the Green operator, which is a family of operators on the half-line parameterized by the Mellin variable. Always check whether the problem requires the full matrix symbol or only the interior principal symbol.

The FBI Transform and the Analytic Wavefront Set

The Fourier–Bros–Iagolnitzer (FBI) transform provides a microlocal tool for analytic regularity. For a distribution u on ℝn, the FBI transform is defined by Tu(x,ξ) = ∫ ei(x−y)·ξ − |x−y|2|ξ|/2 u(y) dy, where ξ ∈ ℝn {0}. The quadratic phase ensures that Tu is holomorphic in x for fixed ξ, and its decay as |ξ|→∞ in a conic neighborhood of ξ0 characterizes the analytic wavefront set WFA(u). Precisely, (x0,ξ0) ∉ WFA(u) iff there exists a conic neighborhood Γ of ξ0 and constants C, ε>0 such that |Tu(x,ξ)| ≤ C e−ε|ξ| for all (x,ξ) with x near x0 and ξ∈Γ. This exponential decay replaces the rapid polynomial decay of the C∞ wavefront set. The analytic wavefront set is invariant under analytic canonical transformations and satisfies a propagation theorem for analytic pseudodifferential operators of real principal type: singularities propagate along the bicharacteristics of the principal symbol, but now the propagation is in the analytic category. The FBI transform also linearizes the Cauchy–Riemann equations: a distribution u is analytic near x0 iff Tu(x,ξ) decays exponentially for all ξ in a neighborhood of the real axis. This characterization is used in the proof of the analytic hypoellipticity of sums of squares of vector fields satisfying the Hörmander bracket condition. Moreover, the FBI transform intertwines with the Bargmann transform, linking microlocal analysis to the theory of coherent states and the Segal–Bargmann space. The analytic wavefront set of a solution to a nonlinear analytic PDE can be studied via the FBI transform combined with paradifferential calculus, yielding results on the persistence of analytic singularities.

The Heat Kernel Proof of the Atiyah–Singer Index Theorem

The Atiyah–Singer index theorem for a Dirac-type operator D on a closed manifold M states that the analytical index ind(D) = dim ker D − dim ker D* equals the topological index ∫M Â(Ω) ch(E), where Â is the Â-genus of the tangent bundle and ch(E) is the Chern character of the twisting bundle. The heat kernel proof, due to McKean–Singer and refined by Getzler, uses the asymptotic expansion of the heat kernel of D2 as t→0+. The McKean–Singer formula expresses the index as the supertrace of the heat operator: ind(D) = Str(e−tD2) for any t>0. The small-time asymptotics of the heat kernel on the diagonal have the form e−tD2(x,x) ∼ (4πt)−n/2 Σk≥0 tk ak(x), where the coefficients ak(x) are local invariants of the symbol of D. Getzler’s rescaling technique assigns weight 1 to the Clifford variables, weight 1/2 to the covariant derivative, and weight 1 to the curvature, leading to a simplified model operator whose heat kernel can be computed explicitly. The term an/2(x) integrates to the Â-genus times the Chern character. The microlocal analysis enters in the construction of the heat kernel as a Fourier integral operator with complex phase for small time, and in the proof that the supertrace of the heat kernel is independent of t. The heat kernel proof also extends to manifolds with boundary using the b-calculus: the heat kernel of the APS boundary condition has an asymptotic expansion whose boundary contribution yields the eta invariant. This approach provides a local index theorem for families, leading to the Bismut–Freed connection on the determinant line bundle.

Microlocal Analysis of the X-Ray Transform and Inverse Problems

The X-ray transform I integrates a function f on a Riemannian manifold (M,g) over geodesics: If(γ) = ∫ f(γ(t)) dt. Its normal operator N = I*I is a pseudodifferential operator of order −1 on the interior of M, with principal symbol proportional to |ξ|g−1. The wavefront set of N is contained in the diagonal of T*M, and N is elliptic on functions, so I is injective modulo smoothing operators. For tensor fields of rank m, the normal operator is a matrix of pseudodifferential operators, and its ellipticity fails precisely on the potential tensors (symmetric gradients of lower rank tensors). The microlocal analysis of I uses the fact that I is a Fourier integral operator associated with the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow. The Bolker condition — that the map from the cotangent bundle of the geodesic space to T*M is an immersion — guarantees that I*I is a pseudodifferential operator. When the Bolker condition fails (e.g., for the radial transform on the sphere), I*I is a Fourier integral operator with a more complicated canonical relation, and the inversion requires additional geometric assumptions. In practical tomography, one often has incomplete data: the limited-angle problem corresponds to restricting the canonical relation, and the visible singularities are those whose conormal directions intersect the set of measured geodesics. The microlocal framework predicts which singularities can be stably reconstructed and which are invisible, guiding the design of regularization methods such as filtered backprojection with a microlocal cutoff. Recent work extends this analysis to the attenuated X-ray transform and to the nonlinear problem of recovering a Riemannian metric from its boundary distance function, where the linearization is a geodesic X-ray transform on symmetric 2-tensors.

  • Common Trap: Confusing the principal symbol of a composition with the product of full symbols; the asymptotic expansion includes lower-order Poisson bracket terms.
  • Common Trap: Forgetting that the Boutet de Monvel calculus requires three symbol levels — interior, boundary, and Mellin — and that ellipticity means invertibility at all three levels.
  • Common Trap: Assuming the analytic wavefront set is defined by polynomial decay; it requires exponential decay of the FBI transform.
  • Common Trap: Overlooking the role of Getzler rescaling in the heat kernel proof; the standard symbolic calculus does not directly yield the Â-genus without the rescaling.
  • Common Trap: Treating the X-ray transform normal operator as always elliptic; for tensor fields it has a kernel consisting of potential tensors.
  • Common Trap: Ignoring the Bolker condition when analyzing the composition I*I; without it the normal operator is not a pseudodifferential operator.
  • Common Trap: Misapplying the propagation of singularities theorem to nonlinear equations without first linearizing and verifying the real principal type condition.

Propagation of Singularities for Real Principal Type Operators

The propagation of singularities theorem, proved by Duistermaat and Hörmander, states that the wavefront set of a solution to a linear partial differential equation with real principal symbol is invariant under the Hamilton flow of that symbol. Let P(x, D) be a pseudodifferential operator of order m with principal symbol pm(x, ξ) real‑valued and homogeneous of degree m in ξ. If u ∈ 𝒟′(Ω) satisfies Pu = f with f ∈ C∞(Ω), then WF(u) WF(f) is a union of maximally extended integral curves of the Hamilton vector field Hp = ∂ξp ⋅ ∂x − ∂xp ⋅ ∂ξ inside the characteristic set Σ = {pm = 0}. The theorem requires the real principal type condition: dpm ≠ 0 on Σ, which guarantees that Hp is non‑radial and defines a genuine flow on the cosphere bundle. In the microlocal setting one works with the compressed cotangent bundle S*Ω, where the flow preserves the contact structure. A key corollary is that singularities cannot appear spontaneously; they travel along bicharacteristics. This principle underlies the proof of microlocal regularity for hyperbolic equations, the construction of parametrices for real principal type operators, and the analysis of boundary value problems where the flow meets the boundary transversally or tangentially.

When the real principal type condition fails, for instance at points where dpm vanishes (multiple characteristics), the propagation picture changes dramatically. At a double characteristic the Hamilton flow may have a degenerate linearisation, leading to phenomena such as glancing rays, diffraction, and the formation of caustics. The work of Melrose and Sjöstrand on the propagation of singularities for operators with double characteristics introduced the notion of the “generalised bicharacteristic flow” and showed that the wavefront set is still contained in the union of such generalised curves, but the description requires a finer stratification of the characteristic set. In practical terms, an examiner may ask to identify whether a given operator satisfies the real principal type condition and to describe the expected propagation of singularities for a solution with prescribed initial wavefront set.

Semiclassical Pseudodifferential Calculus and Egorov's Theorem

Semiclassical analysis introduces a small parameter h > 0 and studies families of operators P(h) = Oph(a) whose symbols a(x, ξ; h) admit asymptotic expansions in powers of h. The quantisation Oph maps a symbol a ∈ Sm(T*ℝn) to an operator acting on L2(ℝn) by (Oph(a)u)(x) = (2πh)−n ∫ ei(x−y)·ξ/h a(x, ξ; h) u(y) dy dξ. The principal symbol is the leading term a0(x, ξ) in the expansion a ∼ Σ hj aj. Composition of semiclassical operators yields a symbolic calculus where the product of principal symbols is the principal symbol of the product, and the subprincipal symbol involves the Poisson bracket {a0, b0} divided by 2i. Egorov's theorem asserts that for a semiclassical Fourier integral operator U(h) quantising a canonical transformation κ on T*ℝn, the conjugation U(h)* Oph(a) U(h) is a semiclassical pseudodifferential operator with principal symbol a ∘ κ. This result is the quantum analogue of the classical invariance of observables under symplectomorphisms and is fundamental in the study of quantum ergodicity, the Gutzwiller trace formula, and the propagation of coherent states. The theorem holds under the assumption that κ is a homogeneous canonical transformation and that U(h) is associated with a non‑degenerate phase function; the remainder is of order h in the operator norm on L2.

In the semiclassical setting the wavefront set is replaced by the semiclassical wavefront set WFh(u), which records the directions in which the h‑Fourier transform of u fails to decay rapidly uniformly in h. The propagation of WFh for solutions of (P(h) − z)u = 0 with real principal symbol follows the same Hamilton flow as in the classical case, but now the flow is considered on the energy surface p0(x, ξ) = z. This perspective is essential for proving resolvent estimates on non‑trapping manifolds and for establishing the semiclassical Weyl law with remainder. A typical exam question asks to compute the subprincipal symbol of a given semiclassical operator or to verify Egorov's theorem for a specific canonical transformation such as the linear symplectic map (x, ξ) ↦ (ξ, −x).

Worked Example: Subprincipal Symbol of a Semiclassical Operator
Let P(h) = Oph(p) with symbol p(x, ξ; h) = ξ2 + V(x) + h W(x, ξ), where V ∈ C∞(ℝn) and W ∈ C∞(T*ℝn). Compute the principal and subprincipal symbols of P(h).
The principal symbol is the h‑independent part: p0(x, ξ) = ξ2 + V(x). The subprincipal symbol psub is defined as the coefficient of h in the expansion of the full symbol after Weyl quantisation. For a symbol of the form a(x, ξ) + h b(x, ξ) the Weyl quantisation yields principal symbol a and subprincipal symbol b − (i/2){a, a} = b because the Poisson bracket of a with itself vanishes. Here a = ξ2 + V(x) and b = W(x, ξ). Hence psub(x, ξ) = W(x, ξ). If the quantisation were the standard left quantisation, an extra term −(i/2)∂ξ·∂xa would appear, but the Weyl calculus eliminates it. Therefore the principal symbol is ξ2 + V(x) and the subprincipal symbol is W(x, ξ).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the subprincipal symbol, first identify the quantisation convention (Weyl, left, right). Write the symbol as a0 + h a1 + … and recall that for Weyl quantisation the subprincipal symbol equals a1. For left quantisation add −(i/2){a0, a0} = 0, so the answer is still a1. For right quantisation the sign flips. Memorise the Poisson bracket formula {f, g} = ∂ξf ∂xg − ∂xf ∂ξg; it appears in composition formulas and in the transport equation for amplitudes of Fourier integral operators.

Microlocal Analysis of Boundary Value Problems: The Calderón Projector

For an elliptic differential operator P of order m on a compact manifold with boundary M, the Calderón projector C is the orthogonal projection in L2(∂M) onto the Cauchy data space of solutions of Pu = 0 that lie in Hm(M). Microlocally, C is a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 on the boundary whose principal symbol is the projection onto the incoming (or outgoing) Lagrangian subspace determined by the symbol of P. The construction uses the Boutet de Monvel calculus: one first builds a parametrix for the Dirichlet problem, then extracts the Poisson operator K mapping boundary data to solutions, and finally sets C = γ K where γ is the trace operator. The symbol of C satisfies the idempotent relation σ(C)² = σ(C) and its range is a Lagrangian subbundle of the symplectic vector bundle T*∂M ⊕ T*∂M. Ellipticity of the boundary value problem is equivalent to the invertibility of the interior symbol, the boundary symbol, and the Mellin symbol simultaneously; the Calderón projector encodes the boundary symbol. In the case of the Laplacian on a domain with smooth boundary, C is the classical Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map composed with the inverse of the single‑layer potential, and its principal symbol is |ξ′|, where ξ′ denotes the tangential covariable. The microlocal structure of C allows one to read off the well‑posedness of non‑local boundary conditions and to compute the index of the associated boundary value problem via the Atiyah‑Patodi‑Singer theorem.

When the boundary is not smooth but has corners or edges, the Calderón projector becomes an operator in the edge calculus of Mazzeo and Melrose. Its symbol hierarchy now includes an interior symbol, a boundary symbol on each face, and a family of model operators on the infinite cone obtained by blowing up the corner. The edge wavefront set of a solution captures singularities that propagate along the edges, and the Calderón projector on the edge is a matrix of pseudodifferential operators acting on the cross‑section. This refined calculus is essential for the analysis of the Hodge‑de Rham operator on manifolds with corners and for the proof of the index theorem on such spaces. An examiner may ask to describe the symbol of the Calderón projector for a given second‑order elliptic operator and to explain how its idempotence reflects the Lagrangian nature of the Cauchy data space.

Fourier Integral Operators with Degenerate Canonical Relations

The standard theory of Fourier integral operators (FIOs) assumes that the canonical relation C ⊂ T*Y × T*X is a homogeneous canonical graph, i.e. the projection maps are local diffeomorphisms. In many geometric problems this condition fails: the canonical relation may have folds, cusps, or more complicated singularities. Melrose and Uhlmann developed a calculus for FIOs associated with canonical relations that are cleanly intersecting Lagrangian submanifolds. A canonical relation is clean if the intersection of the two Lagrangians is a submanifold and the tangent spaces intersect in the expected dimension. Under the clean intersection hypothesis, the composition of two FIOs is again an FIO whose canonical relation is the set‑theoretic composition, and the order adds up with a correction term equal to half the excess of the intersection. For a fold canonical relation, the projection to the base has a Whitney fold singularity; the associated FIO maps conormal distributions to distributions with a specific type of singularity described by the Airy function. The parametrix construction for operators with fold singularities uses the method of stationary phase with degenerate critical points, leading to asymptotic expansions involving Airy integrals. This theory underpins the analysis of the wave equation on manifolds with boundary where glancing rays produce fold singularities in the canonical relation of the solution operator.

When the canonical relation has a cusp singularity, the projection map fails to be a submersion even after blowing up the fold. The resulting FIOs are no longer bounded on standard Sobolev spaces; they map between weighted Sobolev spaces with loss of derivatives. The work of Greenleaf and Seeger on oscillatory integrals with cusp singularities shows that the optimal loss is 1/6 of a derivative for a generic cusp. In the context of inverse problems, the normal operator I*I for the X‑ray transform on a manifold with conjugate points has a canonical relation with cusp singularities, and the failure of ellipticity manifests as a loss of stability in the reconstruction. An exam question may present a phase function with a degenerate critical point and ask to classify the singularity type (fold, cusp, swallowtail) and to state the corresponding mapping properties of the associated FIO.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix

On a non‑compact manifold (M, g) that is asymptotically Euclidean or asymptotically hyperbolic, the scattering matrix S(λ) at energy λ > 0 is a unitary operator on L2(∂M) (or on the sphere at infinity). Microlocally, S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated with the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow at infinity. For asymptotically Euclidean manifolds, the principal symbol of S(λ) is the identity, and the subprincipal symbol encodes the scattering phase shift. The wavefront set of the Schwartz kernel of S(λ) is contained in the diagonal of T*∂M together with the set of points related by the broken geodesic flow that grazes the boundary. In the asymptotically hyperbolic setting, the scattering matrix is a pseudodifferential operator on the conformal boundary, and its principal symbol is the identity; the subprincipal symbol is related to the renormalised volume. The meromorphic continuation of S(λ) to the complex plane defines scattering resonances as poles of the continued matrix. The distribution of resonances is governed by the fractal Weyl law, which states that the number of resonances in a strip of width h grows like h−δ where δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set. Microlocal techniques, in particular the construction of a complex absorbing potential and the use of the FBI transform, allow one to prove exponential decay of the cut‑off resolvent away from the trapped set and to derive sharp bounds on the resonance counting function. A typical advanced problem asks to compute the principal symbol of the scattering matrix for a given metric perturbation of Euclidean space and to explain how the trapped set influences the resonance free strip.

  • Confusing the classical propagation of singularities theorem with its semiclassical version; the latter requires tracking the h‑dependence of the wavefront set.
  • Assuming that the Calderón projector is always a pseudodifferential operator of order 0; on manifolds with corners it lives in the edge calculus and has a matrix‑valued symbol.
  • Neglecting the excess correction in the composition formula for cleanly intersecting Fourier integral operators; the order adds plus half the excess.
  • Treating fold and cusp singularities as equivalent; folds give Airy‑type asymptotics with no loss of derivatives, while cusps cause a 1/6 derivative loss.
  • Overlooking the role of the trapped set in the fractal Weyl law for scattering resonances; the dimension of the trapped set determines the exponent in the resonance counting function.
  • Misidentifying the principal symbol of the scattering matrix as the scattering amplitude; the principal symbol is the identity on the boundary, the amplitude appears in the subprincipal term.
  • Forgetting that Egorov's theorem holds only for canonical transformations that are quantisable by a non‑degenerate Fourier integral operator; not every symplectomorphism admits such a quantisation.

The advanced topics above extend the core microlocal toolkit to settings where the geometry of the characteristic set, the boundary, or the canonical relation introduces degeneracies that require refined symbolic calculi and precise asymptotic analysis.

Paradifferential Calculus and Nonlinear Microlocal Analysis

The linear theory of pseudodifferential operators extends to nonlinear equations through Bony’s paradifferential calculus, which decomposes a product of functions into a sum of paraproducts and a remainder that is smoother than either factor. For symbols a(x,ξ) in the Hörmander class S^mρ,δ with 0 ≤ δ < ρ ≤ 1, the paradifferential operator T_a is defined by truncating the Fourier transform of the symbol in the frequency variable, yielding an operator that coincides with the standard quantisation modulo smoothing operators. The key estimate is the paraproduct bound ‖T_a u‖Hs-m ≤ C ‖a‖S^m ‖u‖H^s, which holds uniformly for families of symbols satisfying symbolic estimates. This allows one to linearise nonlinear PDEs such as the nonlinear Schrödinger equation or the Euler equation by replacing the nonlinearity with a paradifferential operator whose symbol depends on the solution itself. The resulting paralinearised equation can be studied with the full force of linear microlocal analysis: propagation of singularities, energy estimates, and Strichartz estimates all carry over provided the symbol belongs to an appropriate symbol class. A central result is Bony’s theorem on the propagation of the wavefront set for solutions of semilinear hyperbolic equations: if the nonlinearity is smooth and the initial data have wavefront set contained in a conic set Λ, then the solution’s wavefront set remains in the forward flowout of Λ under the Hamiltonian vector field of the principal symbol. The proof uses the paralinearisation together with the linear propagation theorem and a careful treatment of the remainder term, which is shown to be microlocally smoother. In the context of the Cauchy problem for quasilinear equations, the paradifferential approach yields well‑posedness in Sobolev spaces H^s for s > n/2 + 1, and the loss of derivatives is exactly the one predicted by the linearised principal symbol. This framework has been refined by Métivier, Taylor, and others to handle boundary value problems and systems with variable multiplicities.

The Edge Calculus on Manifolds with Corners

When a manifold has corners of codimension two or higher, the b‑calculus is insufficient because the natural vector fields tangent to all boundary hypersurfaces fail to span the tangent space at the corners. The edge calculus, developed by Mazzeo and Melrose, replaces the b‑tangent bundle by the edge tangent bundle, whose sections are vector fields that are tangent to the boundary and, in addition, scale appropriately along the fibres of a fibration of the boundary. Concretely, near a corner modelled on [0,∞)^k × ℝn-k with a fibration φ: ∂M → B, an edge vector field has the form x∂_x + V where V is a vertical vector field along the fibres of φ. The corresponding pseudodifferential operators are defined by symbols on the edge cotangent bundle, which is a bundle over the interior that extends to a compact manifold with corners. The symbolic calculus includes a principal symbol map taking values in homogeneous functions on the edge cotangent bundle, and a normal operator family obtained by freezing coefficients at the boundary and taking the Fourier transform in the normal variables. The normal operator is a family of operators on the model cone ℝ+ × F, where F is the fibre, and its invertibility for all dual variables is the Fredholm criterion. Index theory in the edge calculus yields a formula involving the Atiyah–Singer index of the interior symbol plus a contribution from the eta invariant of the normal operator family. This calculus has been essential for the analysis of the Hodge–de Rham operator on manifolds with edges, for the study of the heat kernel asymptotics on such spaces, and for the proof of the signature theorem for Witt spaces. The edge calculus also provides the natural setting for the resolvent of the Laplacian on asymptotically conic manifolds, where the boundary fibres are spheres and the normal operator is the model cone operator.

Identifying the principal symbol of a paradifferential operator
Let a(x,ξ) = (1+|ξ|2)m/2 χ(ξ/⟨ξ⟩) be a symbol of order m, where χ is a smooth cutoff equal to 1 for |ξ| ≥ 2 and 0 for |ξ| ≤ 1. Define the paradifferential operator T_a by (T_a u)̂(ξ) = ∫ a(x,ξ) ψ(ξ-η) û(η) dη with a Littlewood–Paley partition ψ. Show that the principal symbol of T_a in the edge calculus sense is σ_pr(T_a)(x,ξ) = a(x,ξ) for ξ ≠ 0.
The paradifferential quantisation uses a frequency cut‑off that localises the symbol to dyadic annuli. On each annulus the symbol a(x,ξ) is essentially constant in ξ up to lower order terms, because the cutoff χ(ξ/⟨ξ⟩) is homogeneous of degree 0 for large |ξ|. The principal symbol of a pseudodifferential operator in any symbolic calculus is the equivalence class of the full symbol modulo symbols of order m-1. Since the remainder in the paradifferential expansion is a smoothing operator (its symbol lies in S-∞), the full symbol of T_a differs from a(x,ξ) by a symbol of order m-1. Therefore the principal symbol is exactly the homogeneous part of a, which for |ξ| ≥ 2 equals (1+|ξ|2)m/2. By homogeneity this extends to σ_pr(T_a)(x,ξ) = |ξ|^m for ξ ≠ 0, matching the standard principal symbol of the Bessel potential operator. The edge calculus does not alter this computation because the edge cotangent bundle coincides with the standard cotangent bundle away from the boundary.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the principal symbol of a paradifferential or edge operator, first identify the underlying symbol class and the order. Write down the full symbol, then drop all terms of order strictly less than the leading order. Remember that cut‑off functions that are homogeneous of degree zero do not affect the principal symbol. In the edge calculus, check whether the operator is elliptic by verifying invertibility of the normal operator family on the model cone; this is often the decisive step for Fredholm properties.

Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Hyperbolic Systems

The Cauchy problem for symmetric hyperbolic systems A0(t,x)∂_t u + ∑j=1^n A_j(t,x)∂x_j u = f(t,x) with initial data u(0,x)=u0(x) is the prototype for microlocal energy methods. The principal symbol is the matrix p(t,x,τ,ξ) = τ A0 + ∑ ξ_j A_j. Strict hyperbolicity means that for each (t,x,ξ) with ξ ≠ 0 the eigenvalues λ_k(t,x,ξ) of A0-1∑ ξ_j A_j are real and distinct. The Hamiltonian vector fields Hλ_k generate the bicharacteristic flow in T*ℝn+1\0. The fundamental theorem of propagation of singularities for systems states that the wavefront set of a distribution solution u is a union of maximally extended bicharacteristics of the eigenvalues, and that the polarisation of the singularity (the vector in ℂ^N describing the direction of the jump) is transported by the parallel transport equation along the flow. This is proved by constructing a microlocal symmetriser: a positive definite matrix S(t,x,ξ) such that S p is symmetric modulo lower order terms. The energy estimate ‖u(t)‖H^s ≤ C(‖u0‖H^s + ∫0^t ‖f(τ)‖H^s dτ) follows from the symmetriser and the standard pseudodifferential calculus. For systems with multiplicities, the eigenvalues may coincide on a submanifold of the characteristic set. In that case the propagation of singularities can be more subtle: singularities may split, and the wavefront set can contain points that are not on a single bicharacteristic but on a union of them. The work of Ivrii, Melrose, and Taylor on the propagation of singularities for systems with involutive characteristics shows that if the characteristic set is a smooth involutive submanifold, then the wavefront set is still a union of bicharacteristics, but the polarisation may undergo a non‑trivial scattering at the intersection of modes. These results are essential for the analysis of elastic waves, Maxwell’s equations in anisotropic media, and the linearised Einstein equations.

Complex Scaling and the Theory of Resonances

Resonances of a Schrödinger operator H = -Δ + V on ℝ^n with a dilation‑analytic potential V are defined as the eigenvalues of the complex‑scaled operator H(θ) = e-2θ(-Δ) + V(eθx) for a small complex angle θ with Im θ > 0. The method of complex scaling, introduced by Aguilar, Balslev, and Combes and rigorously developed by Simon, rotates the continuous spectrum of H away from the real axis, exposing the discrete eigenvalues of H(θ) which are independent of θ and coincide with the poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (H - z)-1 from the upper half‑plane. Microlocally, the complex scaling corresponds to a complex canonical transformation of the phase space T*ℝ^n that maps the real characteristic variety {ξ2 + V(x) = z} to a complex Lagrangian manifold. The wavefront set of the resolvent kernel is contained in the union of the real bicharacteristic flow and the complexified flow emanating from the trapped set. The distribution of resonances is governed by the fractal Weyl law: if the trapped set at energy E has Minkowski dimension δ, then the number of resonances in a strip |Im z| ≤ C h grows like h-δ as h → 0. This law has been proved for hyperbolic trapped sets by Sjöstrand, Zworski, and others using the FBI transform and a complex absorbing potential. The FBI transform provides a phase‑space representation that is well adapted to complex scaling because it diagonalises the action of the complexified Hamiltonian flow. In the semiclassical limit h → 0, the resonances converge to the quantum spectrum of the trapped set, and the resonance free strip is determined by the topological pressure of the classical flow. These techniques have been extended to obstacle scattering, to black‑hole perturbations (Kerr–de Sitter), and to the study of quantum decay rates in open chaotic systems.

The cusp Calculus and Geometric Scattering on Asymptotically Conic Manifolds

On a manifold M with a cusp end, the metric takes the form g = dx2/x4 + h(x)/x2 where x is a boundary defining function and h(x) is a family of metrics on the fibre F. The natural vector fields are x2∂_x and x V for V tangent to the fibres. The cusp calculus, developed by Melrose, Mazzeo, and Wunsch, builds a pseudodifferential algebra adapted to this geometry. The cusp cotangent bundle is a compact manifold with corners whose interior is T*M and whose boundary fibres are the cusp cotangent bundles of the fibres. The principal symbol lives on the cusp cotangent bundle, and the normal operator is obtained by freezing coefficients at x=0 and taking the Fourier transform in the normal variable dual to x2∂_x. The normal operator is a family of operators on the model cusp ℝ × F with metric dt2 + h(0). The Fredholm criterion requires invertibility of this normal operator family for all dual variables. The index theorem for the cusp calculus expresses the index of an elliptic cusp operator as the sum of an interior Atiyah–Singer term and a boundary contribution given by the eta invariant of the normal operator family. In geometric scattering, the resolvent of the Laplacian on an asymptotically conic manifold (where the metric is dr2 + r2 h(r) with h(r) → h0) is analysed by compactifying the manifold to a manifold with boundary and using the cusp calculus after a change of variable x = 1/r. The scattering matrix S(λ) is then a pseudodifferential operator on the boundary, and its principal symbol is the identity. The subprincipal symbol encodes the scattering phase shift and is related to the renormalised volume. The meromorphic continuation of the resolvent yields resonances as poles, and the fractal Weyl law applies with the trapped set being the geodesic flow on the boundary at infinity. The cusp calculus also provides the framework for the proof of the Poisson formula relating the wave trace to the length spectrum of closed geodesics on manifolds with cusps.

  • Confusing the paradifferential remainder with a standard pseudodifferential remainder; the paradifferential remainder is smoothing only in the sense of the chosen Littlewood–Paley decomposition, not in the full symbol class.
  • Assuming the edge calculus reduces to the b‑calculus when the fibration is trivial; the edge normal operator involves the vertical Laplacian on the fibre, which is absent in the b‑normal operator.
  • Neglecting the polarisation transport equation when applying propagation of singularities to systems; the wavefront set alone does not determine the full microlocal structure of a vector‑valued solution.
  • Treating complex scaling as a mere change of variables without verifying dilation analyticity of the potential; non‑analytic potentials produce non‑isolated resonances and the method fails.
  • Overlooking the excess correction in the composition of cusp operators; the order adds plus half the excess, exactly as for cleanly intersecting Fourier integral operators.
  • Misidentifying the principal symbol of the scattering matrix on an asymptotically conic manifold as the scattering amplitude; the principal symbol is the identity, the amplitude appears in the subprincipal term.
  • Forgetting that the fractal Weyl law exponent is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set, not the Hausdorff dimension; the two can differ for non‑hyperbolic trapped sets.

The Melrose Scattering Calculus on Asymptotically Euclidean Manifolds

The scattering calculus, introduced by Melrose in the 1990s, provides a pseudodifferential framework tailored to manifolds that are asymptotically Euclidean. Let M be a non‑compact manifold with a boundary defining function x such that the metric takes the form g = dx²/x⁴ + h(x)/x² near the boundary, where h(x) is a smooth family of metrics on the fibres. The natural vector fields are x²∂ₓ and xV for V tangent to the fibres. The scattering cotangent bundle scT*M compactifies the ordinary cotangent bundle by adding a boundary at fibre infinity; its interior is canonically identified with T*M. Operators in the scattering calculus are those whose Schwartz kernels are conormal to the diagonal in the scattering double space and have polyhomogeneous expansions at the boundary faces. The principal symbol lives on scT*M and is a homogeneous function of degree m on the fibres. The normal operator is obtained by freezing coefficients at x = 0 and taking the Fourier transform in the variable dual to x²∂ₓ; it yields a family of translation‑invariant operators on the model space ℝⁿ. Ellipticity in the scattering calculus requires invertibility of both the principal symbol on scT*M and the normal operator family for all dual variables. The Fredholm property for scattering operators on weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,ℓ_sc follows from this invertibility, and the index is computed by a topological formula that reduces to the Atiyah–Singer index on the compactified manifold with boundary. The scattering matrix S(λ) at energy λ > 0 is a pseudodifferential operator on the boundary sphere; its principal symbol is the identity, while the subprincipal term encodes the scattering phase shift and is related to the renormalised volume. Meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (Δ_g − λ)⁻¹ across the real axis yields resonances as poles, and the distribution of resonances obeys a fractal Weyl law with exponent given by the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set of the geodesic flow at infinity.

Composition in the scattering calculus follows the same symbolic rules as the standard calculus, but the order of the remainder gains an extra half‑order coming from the excess of the intersecting Lagrangians in the scattering double space. This excess correction is identical to the one appearing in the composition of cleanly intersecting Fourier integral operators. The calculus also admits a parametrix construction for elliptic operators, yielding a scattering parametrix whose error is of order −∞ in the scattering sense. Applications include the proof of the Poisson formula relating the wave trace to the length spectrum of closed geodesics on asymptotically Euclidean manifolds, and the analysis of the radiation field for the wave equation, which is a Fourier integral operator associated to the geodesic flow on the boundary at infinity.

The Vasy Calculus for Asymptotically Hyperbolic Spaces

András Vasy developed a microlocal calculus adapted to asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds, where the metric near the conformal boundary takes the form g = dx²/x² + h(x)/x² with x a boundary defining function and h(x) a family of metrics on the boundary. The natural vector fields are x∂ₓ and xV, and the corresponding cotangent bundle is the 0‑cotangent bundle ⁰T*M, a manifold with boundary whose interior is T*M. The 0‑calculus (also called the Vasy calculus) consists of operators whose kernels are conormal to the lifted diagonal in the 0‑double space and have polyhomogeneous expansions at the boundary faces. The principal symbol is a homogeneous function on ⁰T*M, and the normal operator is obtained by freezing coefficients at x = 0 and Fourier transforming in the variable dual to x∂ₓ; it yields a family of operators on the hyperbolic space ℍⁿ⁺¹. Ellipticity requires invertibility of the principal symbol on ⁰T*M and of the normal operator family for all spectral parameters. The Fredholm theory on weighted 0‑Sobolev spaces Hs,ℓ0 follows, and the index is given by a topological formula involving the eta invariant of the normal operator family. The resolvent of the Laplacian on an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold continues meromorphically to the complex plane, with poles (resonances) that correspond to eigenvalues of the normal operator family. The scattering matrix S(σ) at spectral parameter σ is a pseudodifferential operator on the conformal boundary; its principal symbol is the identity, and the subprincipal symbol determines the scattering phase. Vasy’s calculus also provides a framework for the proof of the meromorphic continuation of the Eisenstein series and for the analysis of the wave equation on asymptotically de Sitter and Kerr–de Sitter spacetimes, where the red‑shift effect at the event horizon is captured by the 0‑normal operator.

A key technical point is the treatment of the radial points of the Hamiltonian flow at the boundary of ⁰T*M. The flow has a source‑sink structure at the radial sets, and propagation of singularities through these points requires a positive commutator argument with a carefully chosen escape function. The resulting microlocal regularity theorem shows that the wavefront set of a solution to (Δ_g − σ)u = f is invariant under the broken bicharacteristic flow, with possible loss of regularity only at the radial points. This loss is quantified by the threshold regularity determined by the eigenvalues of the normal operator. The calculus also accommodates boundary value problems: the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map for the Laplacian is a 0‑pseudodifferential operator whose principal symbol is the square root of the boundary metric, and its subprincipal term encodes the scattering matrix. The composition formula for 0‑operators includes an excess correction of ½, exactly as in the scattering and b‑calculi, reflecting the clean intersection of the lifted diagonal with the boundary faces in the 0‑double space.

Worked Example: Normal Operator of the Scattering Laplacian
Let (M,g) be an asymptotically Euclidean manifold with metric g = dx²/x⁴ + h(0)/x² + O(x) near the boundary, where h(0) is a metric on the sphere Sn-1. Compute the normal operator N(A) of the scattering Laplacian Δ_g acting on functions, and determine its invertibility on weighted L² spaces.
The scattering Laplacian in the model coordinates (x, y) with y ∈ Sn-1 reads Δ_g = −(x²∂ₓ)² − (n−1)x²∂ₓ + Δh(0) + lower order terms. Freezing coefficients at x = 0 and Fourier transforming in the variable τ dual to x²∂ₓ gives the normal operator N(Δ_g)(τ) = τ² − i(n−1)τ + Δh(0) acting on L²(Sn-1). This is a family of elliptic operators on the sphere parameterised by τ ∈ ℝ. Its spectrum consists of eigenvalues λ_k(τ) = τ² − i(n−1)τ + μ_k, where μ_k are the eigenvalues of Δh(0) (μ0 = 0, μ_k > 0 for k ≥ 1). For any real τ, the real part of λ_k(τ) is τ² + μ_k ≥ 0, with equality only at τ = 0, k = 0. Hence N(Δ_g)(τ) is invertible on L²(Sn-1) for all τ ≠ 0, and at τ = 0 the kernel is the constant functions. On weighted spaces x^ℓ L² with ℓ ≠ (n−1)/2, the indicial roots avoid the weight, so the normal operator is invertible. This invertibility is precisely the condition for Δ_g to be Fredholm on Hs,ℓ_sc.
Exam Strategy: Identifying the Correct Calculus
When a problem asks for the microlocal structure of an operator on a non‑compact manifold, first identify the asymptotic geometry: asymptotically Euclidean → scattering calculus; asymptotically hyperbolic → 0‑calculus (Vasy); manifolds with boundary and product‑type metric → b‑calculus; manifolds with fibred boundary → fibred cusp or edge calculus. The choice determines the relevant compactified cotangent bundle, the form of the normal operator, and the Fredholm weights. Memorise the model vector fields (x²∂ₓ for scattering, x∂ₓ for 0‑calculus, x∂ₓ for b‑calculus) and the corresponding principal symbol homogeneity. In multiple‑choice questions, the presence of a boundary defining function x and the power of x in the metric (x⁻⁴ vs x⁻²) is the quickest discriminator.

Semiclassical Defect Measures and Quantum Ergodicity

Semiclassical defect measures (also called microlocal defect measures or H‑measures) provide a quantitative description of the phase‑space concentration of sequences of functions bounded in L² as the semiclassical parameter h → 0. Given a sequence u_h with ‖u_h‖L² ≤ C, there exists a subsequence and a positive Radon measure μ on the cosphere bundle S*M such that for any a ∈ C_c^∞(T*M) homogeneous of degree 0, ⟨Op_h(a)u_h, u_h⟩ → ∫S*M a dμ. The measure μ captures the weak‑* limit of the Wigner distributions associated to u_h. A fundamental result of Gérard, Lions, and Paul shows that if u_h solves a semiclassical PDE (h²Δ + V)u_h = o(1) in a suitable sense, then μ is invariant under the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol p(x,ξ) = |ξ|² + V(x). This invariance is the cornerstone of quantum ergodicity: if the classical flow is ergodic on the energy surface p = E, then for any orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions φh_j with eigenvalues converging to E, the associated defect measures converge to the Liouville measure on the energy surface for a density‑one subsequence. The proof uses the Egorov theorem to propagate observables along the flow and the Birkhoff ergodic theorem to average over time. The rate of convergence and the possible existence of exceptional subsequences (scars) are governed by the entropy of the flow and the fractal dimension of the trapped set. In the presence of a boundary, the defect measure satisfies a reflection law at the boundary, and the quantum ergodicity theorem extends to billiard systems with ergodic billiard flow. The semiclassical defect measure framework also underlies the proof of the local Weyl law with remainder estimates and the analysis of the distribution of resonances for open chaotic systems via the fractal Weyl law.

Technically, the construction of μ uses the semiclassical pseudodifferential calculus with symbols in the class S0δ for 0 ≤ δ < ½, and the compactness of the unit ball in the space of semiclassical operators modulo h^∞. The measure is supported on the characteristic set of the operator, and its projection to the base manifold gives the weak limit of |u_h|² dx. For systems, one obtains a matrix‑valued defect measure satisfying a transport equation with a matrix‑valued Hamiltonian. The quantum ergodicity theorem for eigenfunctions of the Laplacian on compact manifolds with Anosov geodesic flow was proved by Shnirelman, Zelditch, and Colin de Verdière. The extension to manifolds with boundary and to the Neumann problem was achieved by Gérard and Leichtnam using the b‑calculus to handle the boundary. The defect measure technique also yields the quantum unique ergodicity conjecture for arithmetic surfaces, where the additional Hecke symmetry forces all defect measures to be the Liouville measure; this was proved by Lindenstrauss using measure classification on homogeneous spaces.

The Microlocal Structure of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann Map

The Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann (DtN) map Λ_g for a Riemannian manifold (M,g) with boundary ∂M sends Dirichlet data f on ∂M to the normal derivative ∂_ν u|∂M of the harmonic extension u solving Δ_g u = 0, u|∂M = f. Microlocally, Λ_g is a classical pseudodifferential operator of order 1 on ∂M. Its principal symbol is σ_pr(Λ_g)(x',ξ') = √{gij(x') ξ_i ξ_j}, the square root of the boundary metric induced by g. The subprincipal symbol involves the second fundamental form of the boundary and the tangential derivatives of the metric. When the metric is product‑type near the boundary (g = dx² + h(x)), the DtN map belongs to the b‑calculus and its normal operator is the square root of the tangential Laplacian on the boundary. For more general metrics, the DtN map can be analysed using the scattering or 0‑calculus after a suitable compactification. The wavefront set of the Schwartz kernel of Λ_g is contained in the conormal bundle of the diagonal in ∂M × ∂M, together with the set of points (x',ξ'; y',−η') where (x',ξ') and (y',η') are related by the billiard flow on the boundary. This reflects the fact that singularities of the Dirichlet data propagate along the boundary according to the geodesic flow of the induced metric, with reflection at the boundary governed by the law of geometric optics. The DtN map is elliptic, and its inverse (the Neumann‑to‑Dirichlet map) is a pseudodifferential operator of order −1. The Calderón projector, which projects Cauchy data onto the Cauchy data space of harmonic functions, is a pseudodifferential projection of order 0 whose principal symbol is the orthogonal projection onto the positive frequency subspace of the boundary symbol. The index of the Calderón projector on a manifold with boundary is given by the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer boundary correction, which involves the eta invariant of the tangential operator. In inverse problems, the knowledge of the DtN map determines the Taylor series of the metric at the boundary (boundary rigidity) and, under additional assumptions, the metric in the interior (the Calderón problem). Microlocal analysis of the DtN map also yields the boundary control method for the wave equation, where the response operator (the hyperbolic analogue of the DtN map) is a Fourier integral operator associated to the billiard flow.

When the boundary is not strictly convex, the billiard flow may have glancing rays, and the DtN map acquires a singularity at the glancing set. The microlocal structure near glancing is described by the Melrose–Taylor parametrix, which expresses the DtN map as a sum of a pseudodifferential operator and a Fourier integral operator associated to the glancing manifold. The order of the Fourier integral component is ⅙, reflecting the Airy‑type behaviour of the wave equation at glancing. This refined description is essential for the proof of the sharp spectral asymptotics for the Dirichlet Laplacian on domains with smooth boundary, where the second term in the Weyl law involves the integral of the mean curvature over the boundary. The DtN map also plays a central role in the theory of boundary triples for self‑adjoint extensions of symmetric operators, where the Weyl function coincides with the DtN map and its analytic properties encode the spectral theory of the extension.

  • Confusing the scattering calculus with the 0‑calculus; the former uses vector fields x²∂ₓ, the latter x∂ₓ, leading to different compactified cotangent bundles and normal operators.
  • Assuming the principal symbol of the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map is the boundary metric itself; it is the square root of the boundary metric.
  • Neglecting the excess correction of ½ in the composition of scattering, 0‑, or b‑operators; this correction arises from the clean intersection of the lifted diagonal with boundary faces.
  • Treating semiclassical defect measures as ordinary measures on phase space without accounting for their invariance under the Hamiltonian flow; this invariance is the key to quantum ergodicity.
  • Overlooking the role of the normal operator family in the Fredholm criterion for the Vasy calculus; invertibility for all spectral parameters is required, not just at a single energy.
  • Misidentifying the order of the Fourier integral component in the Melrose–Taylor parametrix for the DtN map at glancing; it is ⅙, not 0 or ½.
  • Forgetting that the fractal Weyl law exponent is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set, not the Hausdorff dimension; the two can differ for non‑hyperbolic trapped sets.

The advanced sections above complete the microlocal toolkit needed for research‑level problems in geometric analysis and inverse problems.

The Heisenberg Calculus and Subelliptic Pseudodifferential Operators

The Heisenberg calculus, developed by Beals, Greiner, and Stanton in the 1980s, provides a pseudodifferential framework for operators that are hypoelliptic but not elliptic, such as the Kohn Laplacian on the Heisenberg group. The underlying geometry is the Heisenberg group Hn with coordinates (x,y,t) and group law (x,y,t)·(x′,y′,t′) = (x+x′, y+y′, t+t′ + ½(x·y′ − y·x′)). Left‑invariant vector fields Xj = ∂xj + ½ yj ∂t, Yj = ∂yj − ½ xj ∂t, and T = ∂t satisfy [Xj, Yk] = −δjk T. The sublaplacian ΔH = −∑(Xj2 + Yj2) is homogeneous of degree 2 with respect to the dilations δλ(x,y,t) = (λx, λy, λ2t). In the Heisenberg calculus, symbols are functions on the cotangent bundle of Hn that are homogeneous of degree m with respect to the dual dilations (ξ,η,τ) → (λξ, λη, λ2τ). The principal symbol of ΔH is σpr(ΔH) = |ξ|2 + |η|2, which vanishes on the characteristic set {τ = 0}. Nevertheless, ΔH satisfies a subelliptic estimate ‖u‖Hs+1 ≤ C(‖ΔHu‖Hs + ‖u‖Hs) for all s, where Hs are the Folland–Stein Sobolev spaces adapted to the Heisenberg dilations. The calculus is closed under composition and adjoints, and the symbolic calculus includes a correction term of order ½ arising from the non‑commutativity of the group law. The parametrix for ΔH is a Heisenberg pseudodifferential operator of order −2 whose kernel is homogeneous of degree −2n−2 with respect to the Korányi gauge. This calculus has been extended to general CR manifolds by Boutet de Monvel and others, where the Heisenberg group appears as the nilpotentization of the tangential Cauchy–Riemann complex. The wavefront set in this setting is the Heisenberg wavefront set WFH(u), which captures singularities in directions not annihilated by the subelliptic symbol. Propagation of singularities for the heat operator ∂t + ΔH follows the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol on the characteristic variety, which is the union of the zero section and the fiber τ = 0. The Heisenberg calculus is essential for the analysis of the ∂̅b‑Neumann problem on strictly pseudoconvex CR manifolds, where the Kohn Laplacian □b belongs to the calculus and its subelliptic gain of ½ derivative is reflected in the order of the parametrix.

The Grushin Calculus for Degenerate Elliptic Operators

The Grushin calculus addresses operators that degenerate on a submanifold, the prototype being the Grushin operator G = −∂x2 − x2k∂y2 on ℝ2 with k ∈ ℕ. This operator is elliptic away from the line {x = 0} but loses derivatives transversally to that line. The appropriate symbolic framework uses a two‑step filtration of the cotangent bundle: the principal symbol σpr(G) = ξ2 + x2kη2 vanishes to order 2k on the characteristic set {x = 0, ξ = 0}. Melrose and Mendoza introduced the b‑calculus with a weight function ρ = |x| to capture this degeneracy; the Grushin calculus refines this by incorporating a second weight σ = |ξ| + |x|k|η|. Symbols are functions on the blown‑up cotangent bundle that are homogeneous of degree m with respect to the joint dilations (x,ξ,η) → (λx, λξ, λk+1η). The composition formula acquires an excess correction of order ½(k+1) due to the clean intersection of the lifted diagonal with the boundary face {x = 0}. The parametrix for G is a Grushin pseudodifferential operator of order −2 whose kernel has a conormal singularity along the diagonal and a weaker singularity along the characteristic variety. Subelliptic estimates hold with a loss of k/(k+1) derivatives: ‖u‖Hs+1/(k+1) ≤ C(‖Gu‖Hs + ‖u‖Hs). The wavefront set WFG(u) is defined using the Grushin symbol classes; singularities propagate along the Hamiltonian flow of σpr(G) on the characteristic set, which consists of the bicharacteristic strips that graze the degeneracy line. For k = 1, the flow exhibits a glancing behaviour analogous to the Melrose–Taylor parametrix for the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map, and the order of the associated Fourier integral component is ⅙. The Grushin calculus has been generalized to operators degenerating on higher‑codimension submanifolds by Helffer and Nourrigat, and to the analysis of the Kohn Laplacian on weakly pseudoconvex CR manifolds where the Levi form has constant rank. In the semiclassical setting, the Grushin operator h2G with h → 0 leads to a semiclassical Grushin calculus where the effective Planck constant scales differently in the degenerate direction, and the Bohr–Sommerfeld quantization conditions for the eigenvalues involve integrals over the Lagrangian tori in the blown‑up phase space.

Worked Example: Subelliptic Estimate for the Grushin Operator
Let G = −∂x2 − x2∂y2 on ℝ2. Show that for any s ∈ ℝ there exists C > 0 such that ‖u‖Hs+1/2 ≤ C(‖Gu‖Hs + ‖u‖Hs) for all u ∈ Cc∞(ℝ2).
The principal symbol is σpr(G) = ξ2 + x2η2. Introduce the weight σ = |ξ| + |x||η|. The operator G belongs to the Grushin calculus of order 2 with respect to this weight. By the symbolic calculus, there exists a parametrix Q of order −2 such that QG = I − R with R smoothing in the Grushin sense. The Sobolev spaces HsG are defined by the norm ‖u‖HsG = ‖⟨σ⟩sû‖L2. The composition formula gives QG = I − R where R is of order −∞, hence bounded on all HsG. Then u = QGu + Ru, so ‖u‖Hs+1G ≤ C(‖Gu‖HsG + ‖u‖HsG). The inclusion Hs+1G ⊂ Hs+1/2 (standard Sobolev) follows from the fact that σ ≥ ⟨ξ,η⟩1/2 up to a constant, which yields the desired estimate with a loss of ½ derivative. The constant C depends only on the symbol bounds of Q and R.
Exam Strategy: Identifying the Correct Calculus
When a problem presents a differential operator that degenerates on a submanifold, first determine the order of vanishing of the principal symbol in the normal direction. If the symbol vanishes quadratically (k = 1), the Grushin calculus with weight σ = |ξ| + |x||η| applies and the subelliptic gain is ½. If the vanishing is of higher order k, the gain is 1/(k+1). For operators that are homogeneous with respect to a non‑isotropic dilation (e.g., the Heisenberg group), use the Heisenberg calculus. Matching the dilation structure to the operator's homogeneity is the key to selecting the right symbolic framework and avoiding the trap of applying the standard pseudodifferential calculus where it fails.

The Microlocal Analysis of the ∂̅b‑Neumann Problem and CR Geometry

The ∂̅b‑Neumann problem arises on the boundary of a strictly pseudoconvex domain in ℂn+1. The tangential Cauchy–Riemann operator ∂̅b acts on (0,q)‑forms on the CR manifold M = ∂Ω. Its formal adjoint ∂̅b* and the Kohn Laplacian □b = ∂̅b∂̅b* + ∂̅b*∂̅b are subelliptic operators of order 2. Kohn proved in 1963 that on a strictly pseudoconvex CR manifold, □b satisfies a subelliptic estimate with a gain of ½ derivative: ‖u‖Hs+1/2 ≤ C(‖□bu‖Hs + ‖u‖Hs). The microlocal proof uses the Heisenberg calculus: the principal symbol of □b is the sublaplacian on the Heisenberg group, which is hypoelliptic but not elliptic. The characteristic variety is the set where the Levi form vanishes; strict pseudoconvexity means the Levi form is positive definite, so the characteristic variety is exactly the zero section. The wavefront set WFH(u) for the Heisenberg calculus captures singularities in the directions of the missing ellipticity. Boutet de Monvel showed that the ∂̅b‑Neumann operator Nq (the inverse of □b on the orthogonal complement of its kernel) is a Heisenberg pseudodifferential operator of order −1. Its kernel has a singularity of order −2n−1 along the diagonal, and the Szegő projector (the orthogonal projection onto the kernel of ∂̅b on functions) is a Heisenberg pseudodifferential projection of order 0 with principal symbol the orthogonal projection onto the positive frequency subspace of the Heisenberg symbol. The microlocal structure of the Bergman kernel on strictly pseudoconvex domains follows from the Boutet de Monvel–Sjöstrand parametrix for the ∂̅b‑Neumann problem: the kernel is a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation given by the graph of the Hamiltonian flow of the Heisenberg sublaplacian. For weakly pseudoconvex boundaries where the Levi form has zeros, the subelliptic gain drops below ½, and the appropriate calculus is the Grushin calculus or the more general calculus of operators with multiple characteristics developed by Kohn and Nirenberg. The propagation of singularities for the ∂̅b‑heat equation ∂t + □b follows the Hamiltonian flow on the characteristic variety, which in the strictly pseudoconvex case is trivial, so singularities do not propagate; in the weakly pseudoconvex case, singularities can propagate along the null bicharacteristics of the Levi form. The CR analogue of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem for the ∂̅b complex was proved by Boutet de Monvel and Guillemin, expressing the index in terms of the integral of the Chern character of the CR tangent bundle and the eta invariant of the tangential operator.

Semiclassical Measures for Nonlinear Dispersive Equations

Semiclassical measures, also known as Wigner measures or microlocal defect measures, describe the phase‑space concentration of sequences of solutions to semiclassical PDEs as the Planck constant h → 0. For a bounded sequence uh in L2(ℝd), there exists a subsequence and a non‑negative Radon measure μ on T*ℝd such that ⟨Oph(a)uh, uh⟩ → ∫ a dμ for all symbols a ∈ Cc∞(T*ℝd). If uh solves the semiclassical Schrödinger equation i h ∂t uh = (−h2Δ + V)uh, then μ is invariant under the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol p(x,ξ) = |ξ|2 + V(x). This invariance is the cornerstone of quantum ergodicity: if the classical flow is ergodic on the energy surface, then μ is the Liouville measure for a density‑one subsequence of eigenfunctions. For nonlinear dispersive equations such as the nonlinear Schrödinger equation i h ∂t uh = (−h2Δ + λ|uh|p−1)uh, the measure μ satisfies a Vlasov‑type equation ∂t μ + {p, μ} = 0 where the Poisson bracket includes the nonlinear term λ|u|p−1 as a self‑consistent potential. The rigorous derivation uses the Wigner transform Wh(uh)(x,ξ) = (2πh)−d ∫ e−i y·ξ/h uh(x + y/2) ūh(x − y/2) dy. The sequence Wh is bounded in the space of measures, and any limit point μ is a non‑negative measure satisfying the transport equation. For the cubic NLS in dimension d = 1, the semiclassical limit leads to the kinetic wave equation, and the defect measure captures the phenomenon of weak turbulence. In the context of the Hartree equation, the limit measure solves the Vlasov–Poisson system. The microlocal structure of the defect measure is refined by the notion of two‑microlocal measures, which capture concentration at different scales simultaneously. The propagation of singularities for the nonlinear equation is encoded in the support of μ: if the initial data has wavefront set contained in a Lagrangian manifold Λ, then μ is supported on the Hamiltonian flowout of Λ. This provides a microlocal version of the Egorov theorem for nonlinear equations, where the quantum evolution of observables is approximated by the classical flow up to errors of order h. The analysis of semiclassical measures for the nonlinear wave equation □g u + λ|u|p−1u = 0 on a Lorentzian manifold uses the b‑calculus at the conformal boundary to capture the propagation of singularities along null geodesics, and the defect measure satisfies a transport equation on the characteristic variety of the wave operator.

Sharp Spectral Asymptotics and the Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector

The spectral projector Πλ = 1(−∞,λ](P) for a self‑adjoint elliptic pseudodifferential operator P of order m > 0 on a compact manifold M has a Schwartz kernel that is a Lagrangian distribution associated to the canonical relation given by the graph of the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol p at time 0, i.e., the diagonal in T*M. The singularities of the kernel are conormal to the diagonal, and the wavefront set is contained in the conormal bundle of the diagonal. The Weyl law states that the counting function N(λ) = Tr Πλ ∼ (2π)−n vol{p ≤ λ} as λ → ∞. The remainder estimate depends on the dynamics of the Hamiltonian flow. If the flow is periodic (e.g., on the sphere), the remainder is of order λ(n−1)/m and the kernel exhibits a singularity at the conjugate locus. Duistermaat and Guillemin proved that if the set of periodic bicharacteristics has measure zero, then the remainder is o(λ(n−1)/m). The sharp spectral asymptotics for the Dirichlet Laplacian on a domain with smooth boundary involve the second term (2π)−n+1 (1/4) ∫∂M H dS λ(n−1)/2, where H is the mean curvature. This term arises from the contribution of the glancing rays in the Melrose–Taylor parametrix for the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map, where the Fourier integral component of order ⅙ produces the curvature integral. For manifolds with boundary, the spectral projector for the Dirichlet Laplacian is a Fourier integral operator with a canonical relation that includes the billiard flow. The microlocal structure of the kernel near the boundary is described by the Boutet de Monvel calculus: the kernel is a sum of a pseudodifferential operator on the interior and a boundary term that is a Fourier integral operator associated to the glancing manifold. The trace of the heat kernel e−tP has an asymptotic expansion as t → 0+ whose coefficients are integrals of local invariants (heat invariants). The microlocal proof uses the parametrix construction in the b‑calculus or the scattering calculus, depending on the geometry at infinity. For asymptotically Euclidean manifolds, the scattering calculus of Melrose gives a complete description of the resolvent (P − λ)−1 as a meromorphic family of scattering pseudodifferential operators, and the poles (resonances) are the eigenvalues of the non‑self‑adjoint operator obtained by complex scaling. The fractal Weyl law for resonances states that the number of resonances in a strip |Im λ| ≤ C h is bounded by C h−δ where δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set. This bound is sharp for hyperbolic trapped sets, and the proof uses semiclassical defect measures invariant under the Hamiltonian flow on the trapped set. The microlocal structure of the spectral projector thus encodes both the local Weyl law and the global dynamical information about the classical flow.

  • Confusing the Heisenberg calculus with the standard pseudodifferential calculus; the former requires non‑isotropic dilations and a different symbolic homogeneity.
  • Assuming the subelliptic gain for the Grushin operator is always ½; it is 1/(k+1) where k is the order of degeneracy.
  • Neglecting the excess correction of order ½(k+1) in the composition formula for Grushin operators; this correction is crucial for parametrix construction.
  • Treating the ∂̅b‑Neumann operator as an ordinary pseudodifferential operator of order −1; it belongs to the Heisenberg calculus and its symbol has a specific matrix structure.
  • Overlooking the invariance of semiclassical defect measures under the Hamiltonian flow; this invariance is the key to quantum ergodicity and to the derivation of kinetic equations.
  • Misidentifying the order of the Fourier integral component in the Melrose–Taylor parametrix for the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map at glancing; it is ⅙, not 0 or ½.
  • Forgetting that the fractal Weyl law exponent is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set, not the Hausdorff dimension; the two can differ for non‑hyperbolic trapped sets.

The advanced sections above complete the microlocal toolkit needed for research‑level problems in geometric analysis and inverse problems.

The 0‑Calculus on Manifolds with Fibred Boundaries

The 0‑calculus, developed by Mazzeo and Melrose in the early 1990s, provides a pseudodifferential framework for manifolds M whose boundary ∂M carries a fibration φ : ∂M → Y with compact fibres Z. The model is the interior of a manifold with fibred corners where the metric takes the form g = dx²/x⁴ + φ*gY/x² + gZ near x = 0; this is the natural geometry of asymptotically locally Euclidean (ALE) spaces and of the moduli space of monopoles. The 0‑tangent bundle 0TM is spanned by x²∂x and x∂y, ∂z, so vector fields are tangent to the fibres of φ and vanish quadratically at the boundary. A 0‑pseudodifferential operator of order m has a symbol a(x,y,z,ξ,η,ζ) homogeneous of degree m in the 0‑cotangent variables (ξ,η,ζ) dual to (x²∂x, x∂y, ∂z). The calculus is closed under composition and adjoints, and the principal symbol map is a homomorphism into Sm/Sm−1. Ellipticity in the 0‑sense means invertibility of the principal symbol on 0T*M 0 together with invertibility of the normal operator N(A), a family of operators on the fibres Z parametrised by Y and the dual variable η. The normal operator is obtained by freezing coefficients at x = 0 and taking the Fourier transform in the x variable; it is a pseudodifferential operator on Z with parameter η. The Fredholm property for 0‑elliptic operators between weighted 0‑Sobolev spaces xaHm0 is equivalent to the invertibility of N(A) for all η ≠ 0 and the absence of indicial roots on the weight line Im σ = a + (dim Z)/2. The index theorem for 0‑elliptic operators expresses the index as the sum of an interior term (the Atiyah–Singer integral of the Chern character of the symbol) and a boundary term given by the η‑invariant of the family N(A). This boundary term is the spectral flow of the normal operator across η = 0 and is computed via the adiabatic limit of the η‑form of Bismut and Cheeger. The 0‑calculus also admits a heat kernel construction: the heat kernel of a 0‑elliptic Laplacian is a polyhomogeneous conormal distribution on the 0‑heat space, a blow‑up of M × M × [0,∞) at the diagonal of the boundary and at the temporal boundary. The asymptotics of the trace yield the 0‑index formula and the analytic torsion on ALE spaces. The 0‑calculus is strictly finer than the b‑calculus: a b‑operator of order m is a 0‑operator of order m whose symbol is independent of the fibre variable ζ at x = 0. Conversely, the 0‑calculus contains operators that differentiate along the fibres, which the b‑calculus cannot see. This distinction is crucial for the Hodge theory of ALE metrics, where the harmonic forms are not b‑harmonic but 0‑harmonic, and their asymptotics are governed by the indicial roots of the 0‑Laplacian on the link.
