The Iterated Edge Calculus and the Resolution of Singularities

The iterated edge calculus, systematised by Mazzeo, Vertman, and collaborators, extends the edge calculus to manifolds with iterated fibred corners — spaces obtained by iteratively blowing up strata in a stratified pseudomanifold. The basic building block is an edge manifold M with boundary ∂M fibred over a base B with fibre Z, where B itself may be an edge manifold. The iterated edge tangent bundle ieTM is defined inductively: at the deepest stratum the vector fields are the usual tangent fields; at each higher level they are spanned by x∂x, ∂y (tangent to the base), and the lift of ieTB. The corresponding pseudodifferential operators have symbols homogeneous in the iterated edge cotangent variables. The calculus is closed under composition, and the principal symbol sequence is exact. The normal operator at a stratum is a family of operators on the fibre Z parametrised by the iterated edge cotangent bundle of the base. Ellipticity requires invertibility of the principal symbol and of all normal operators. The Fredholm theory is developed on iterated edge Sobolev spaces xaHmie with weights adapted to each stratum. The index theorem for iterated edge operators is proved by induction on the depth of the stratification, using the excision property of the index and the fact that the normal operator at the deepest stratum is a family of b‑operators on the fibre. The boundary contribution to the index is a sum of η‑invariants of the normal operators, each of which is itself an index of a lower‑depth operator. This recursive structure mirrors the resolution of singularities in algebraic geometry: the iterated blow‑up of a singular algebraic variety produces a manifold with iterated fibred corners, and the intersection cohomology of the variety is isomorphic to the L²‑cohomology of the resolved space with respect to an iterated edge metric. The Hodge theorem for iterated edge metrics states that the space of L² harmonic forms is finite‑dimensional and isomorphic to the intersection cohomology groups. The heat kernel of the iterated edge Laplacian is constructed on the iterated edge heat space, a sequence of blow‑ups of M × M × [0,∞) at the diagonals of all strata. The small‑time asymptotics of the trace yield the local index density and the Cheeger–Müller theorem for analytic torsion on stratified spaces. The iterated edge calculus also provides the natural framework for the microlocal analysis of the wave equation on spacetimes with conical singularities, where the propagation of singularities is described by the iterated edge bicharacteristic flow, which can diffract at the strata according to the normal operator. The diffraction coefficient is a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation given by the scattering matrix of the normal operator on the link.

Worked Problem: Indicial Roots in the 0‑Calculus
Let M be a manifold with fibred boundary ∂M = S¹ × S¹ where the fibration φ : ∂M → S¹ is projection onto the first factor, so the fibre is Z = S¹. Consider the 0‑Laplacian Δ0 = −(x²∂x)² − (x∂y)² − ∂z² on M near x = 0. Find the indicial roots of Δ0 and determine for which weights a the operator Δ0 : xaH²0 → xaL²0 is Fredholm.
The normal operator is obtained by setting x = 0 and Fourier transforming in x: N(Δ0) = σ² + η² − ∂z² acting on L²(S¹z) with parameter (σ,η) ∈ ℝ². The indicial family is I(Δ0, λ) = λ² + η² − ∂z² where λ is the dual variable to x (so x²∂x → iλ). On the fibre S¹, −∂z² has eigenvalues k² for k ∈ ℤ. Thus I(Δ0, λ) is invertible iff λ² + η² + k² ≠ 0 for all k. For fixed η ≠ 0, the roots are λ = ±i√(η² + k²). The weight line for the mapping xaH²0 → xaL²0 is Im λ = a + (dim Z)/2 = a + ½. Fredholmness requires no indicial root on this line. Since η² + k² > 0 for all η ≠ 0 and all k, the roots are purely imaginary with |Im λ| = √(η² + k²) ≥ |η| > 0. Therefore the operator is Fredholm for all a such that a + ½ ≠ ±√(η² + k²) for any η ≠ 0, k ∈ ℤ. Since the right‑hand side can be arbitrarily close to 0 as η → 0 and k = 0, the only excluded weights are those with a + ½ = 0, i.e., a = −½. For all other a, Δ0 is Fredholm between the indicated spaces.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Calculi by Tangent Bundles
When a question asks which calculus applies to a given geometry, identify the tangent vector fields that are allowed to degenerate at the boundary. b‑calculus: x∂x, ∂y (simple boundary). 0‑calculus: x²∂x, x∂y, ∂z (fibred boundary, quadratic degeneracy in normal direction). Edge calculus: x∂x, ∂y, ∂z (fibred boundary, linear degeneracy). Scattering calculus: x²∂x, x∂y (asymptotically conic, no fibration). Iterated edge: recursive version of edge. Match the metric asymptotics to the correct tangent bundle; the symbol homogeneity and normal operator structure follow automatically.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Wave Equation on Kerr Black Hole Spacetimes

The Kerr family of rotating black hole solutions to the Einstein vacuum equations provides the most physically relevant testbed for microlocal methods in general relativity. The metric in Boyer–Lindquist coordinates (t,r,θ,φ) has a non‑degenerate event horizon at r = r+ and a Cauchy horizon at r = r−; the ergoregion r+ < r < rergo(θ) is where the stationary Killing field ∂t becomes spacelike. The wave equation □gu = 0 on Kerr is a real principal type operator with a characteristic variety that is a smooth conic hypersurface in T*M 0 except at the trapped set, which consists of null geodesics that neither escape to infinity nor fall into the horizon. The trapped set is a normally hyperbolic symplectic submanifold of the characteristic variety, diffeomorphic to S² × ℝ, and its projection to the (r,θ) plane is the photon sphere. The microlocal analysis of the resolvent R(λ) = (□g − λ²)⁻¹ for Im λ > 0 uses the Mellin transform in t and the b‑calculus at the horizon (after a suitable conformal compactification). The resolvent is a meromorphic family of b‑pseudodifferential operators with poles at the quasinormal modes. The high‑frequency asymptotics of the resolvent are governed by the semiclassical defect measures on the trapped set. The work of Dyatlov, Vasy, and others shows that the resolvent satisfies the estimate ‖R(λ)‖H¹→H¹ ≤ C|λ| for |λ| ≫ 1 in a strip Im λ ≥ −γ, where γ > 0 is the spectral gap determined by the topological pressure of the trapped set. The proof uses the radial point estimates at the horizon and at infinity (in the scattering calculus) together with a propagation of singularities argument through the trapped set using the normally hyperbolic structure. The key microlocal tool is the escape function, a symbol G such that the Poisson bracket HpG is positive on the trapped set, which yields a positive commutator estimate. The quasinormal modes are the poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent to Im λ < 0; their distribution obeys a fractal Weyl law with exponent equal to the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set, which for Kerr is δ = 2 (the trapped set is a smooth 3‑manifold in the 5‑dimensional characteristic variety). The late‑time asymptotics of solutions to the wave equation are governed by the quasinormal mode with the smallest imaginary part (the fundamental mode), leading to exponential decay of local energy for subextremal black holes (a < M). For extremal Kerr (a = M), the horizon is degenerate and the decay is polynomial t⁻² due to the Aretakis instability on the horizon, which is captured by the b‑calculus with a different weight. The microlocal structure of the Cauchy horizon is more delicate: the solution extends continuously but not differentiably across the Cauchy horizon, and the wavefront set of the solution contains the incoming null cone from the Cauchy horizon. This is proved using the edge calculus at the Cauchy horizon (which is a null hypersurface with a fibred structure) and the propagation of singularities for real principal type operators with a degenerate boundary.

The Microlocal Structure of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem and the Eta Invariant

The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer (APS) index theorem for a Dirac‑type operator D on a compact manifold M with boundary ∂M expresses the index of D with APS boundary conditions as indAPS(D) = ∫M α(x) − (η(D∂) + h)/2, where α(x) is the local index density (the Â‑genus for the spin Dirac operator), D∂ is the induced operator on the boundary, h = dim ker D∂, and η(D∂) is the eta invariant η(s) = ∑λ≠0 sign(λ)|λ|⁻ˢ analytically continued to s = 0. The microlocal proof uses the b‑calculus of Melrose. The APS boundary condition is the projection onto the non‑negative eigenspaces of D∂; this projection is a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 on ∂M whose symbol is the Heaviside function of the principal symbol of D∂. The Calderón projector for D is the orthogonal projection onto the Cauchy data space of solutions of Du = 0 near the boundary; it is a b‑pseudodifferential operator of order 0 whose indicial operator is the APS projection. The heat kernel of D² with APS boundary conditions is constructed on the b‑heat space M²b,heat, a blow‑up of M × M × [0,∞) at the diagonal of the boundary and at the temporal boundary. The kernel is polyhomogeneous conormal with leading term at the temporal boundary given by the local index density and at the boundary diagonal by the eta form. The small‑time asymptotics of the supertrace Trs(e−tD²) yield the index formula. The eta invariant itself has a microlocal interpretation: it is the regularised value at s = 0 of the zeta function of the boundary operator, which can be expressed as the integral of the symbol of the parametrix of D∂ over the cosphere bundle. The variation of the eta invariant under a deformation of the metric is given by the integral of a local form (the Chern–Simons form) plus a boundary term involving the symbol of the deformation. This variational formula is the basis for the proof of the APS index theorem for families, where the eta invariant becomes a differential form on the parameter space (the Bismut–Cheeger eta form). The microlocal structure of the eta invariant also appears in the spectral flow formula: the spectral flow of a family of self‑adjoint elliptic operators is equal to the integral of the eta form plus the index of a certain Toeplitz operator on the cylinder. In the context of the b‑calculus, the APS index theorem is a special case of the index theorem for b‑elliptic operators with boundary conditions given by a Lagrangian submanifold of the boundary symbol space. The general b‑index theorem states that the index of a b‑elliptic operator with a well‑posed boundary condition is the sum of the interior Atiyah–Singer integral and a boundary term given by the index of the normal operator family, which is a family of operators on the half‑line. For the Dirac operator, the normal operator is the translation‑invariant operator on ℝ⁺ with boundary condition at 0, and its index is precisely −(η + h)/2. This microlocal perspective clarifies why the APS boundary condition is the natural one for the index problem: it is the unique boundary condition that makes the operator Fredholm in the b‑calculus and whose normal operator has index equal to the eta invariant.

The Microlocal Structure of the Wave Trace and the Poisson Relation

The wave trace Tr cos(t√Δ) for the Laplace–Beltrami operator on a compact Riemannian manifold M is a distribution on ℝ whose singular support is contained in the length spectrum Lsp(M) — the set of lengths of closed geodesics. The Poisson relation, proved by Duistermaat and Guillemin, states that the singular support of the wave trace is exactly Lsp(M) ∪ {0}, and the leading singularity at a non‑degenerate closed geodesic γ of length L is a Lagrangian distribution associated to the conormal bundle of {L} in ℝ, with principal symbol determined by the linearised Poincaré map of γ. Microlocally, the wave kernel cos(t√Δ)(x,y) is a Fourier integral operator of order −(n−1)/2 associated to the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow on T*M. The trace is obtained by pulling back to the diagonal x = y and integrating; this is a clean intersection of Lagrangian submanifolds precisely when the fixed point set of the geodesic flow at time t is a clean submanifold of T*M. For a non‑degenerate closed geodesic, the fixed point set is a circle (the geodesic itself), and the clean intersection calculus gives the singularity as a conormal distribution of order −(n−1)/2 with a phase function whose Hessian is the Poincaré map minus the identity. The amplitude involves the Maslov index of the geodesic. For degenerate closed geodesics (e.g., on the sphere where all geodesics are closed and have the same length), the fixed point set is the whole cosphere bundle, and the singularity is stronger: the wave trace has a singularity of order −n at t = π on Sⁿ, reflecting the high multiplicity of the spectrum. The Poisson relation extends to manifolds with boundary: for the Dirichlet or Neumann Laplacian, the singular support of the wave trace includes the lengths of billiard trajectories (broken geodesics reflecting at the boundary according to the law of reflection). The microlocal analysis of the billiard wave trace uses the Melrose–Taylor parametrix for the wave equation with boundary, where the reflection at the boundary is a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation given by the billiard map. The singularities at glancing trajectories (where the geodesic is tangent to the boundary) are more subtle: the canonical relation has a fold singularity, and the wave trace has a singularity of order −(n+1)/3 at the glancing length, with an amplitude involving the Airy function. This is the microlocal origin of the ⅙ order Fourier integral operator in the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map. The wave trace also encodes the spectrum of the Laplacian via the trace formula: the distributional trace of the wave group is ∑j cos(t√λj). The Poisson relation is the dual statement to the Gutzwiller trace formula in quantum chaos, which expresses the density of states as a sum over periodic orbits. The microlocal proof of the Gutzwiller formula uses the stationary phase approximation on the wave trace, where the critical points are the periodic orbits, and the Hessian is the monodromy matrix. The remainder estimates in the trace formula depend on the dynamics: for Anosov flows, the remainder is O(λ−∞) in a suitable sense, while for integrable systems the remainder is larger. The wave trace invariants (the coefficients of the singularities at the lengths of closed geodesics) are spectral invariants; they determine the length spectrum and the linearised Poincaré maps. The question of whether the wave trace determines the metric up to isometry is the spectral rigidity problem; microlocal methods show that the wave trace determines the jet of the metric at a non‑degenerate closed geodesic, and under generic conditions the length spectrum determines the metric locally.

  • Confusing the 0‑calculus with the scattering calculus: both have x²∂x but the 0‑calculus includes a fibration ∂M → Y and the vector field x∂y, while the scattering calculus has no fibration and uses x∂y only for the tangential variables on a boundary that is a smooth manifold without fibration.
  • Assuming the normal operator in the 0‑calculus is a b‑operator on the fibre; it is a pseudodifferential operator on the fibre with parameter η, and its symbol is the restriction of the 0‑symbol to x = 0 after Fourier transform in x.
  • Treating the iterated edge calculus as a simple iteration of the edge calculus without checking the compatibility of the weight vectors across strata; the Fredholm theory requires a consistent choice of weights that satisfy the indicial root conditions at every stratum simultaneously.
  • Overlooking the role of the escape function in the Kerr resolvent estimate: the positive commutator argument requires a symbol G with HpG > 0 on the trapped set, and such a function exists only because the trapped set is normally hyperbolic; for degenerate trapped sets (e.g., extremal Kerr) the construction fails and the spectral gap closes.
  • Misidentifying the order of the singularity in the wave trace at a glancing trajectory: it is −(n+1)/3, not −(n−1)/2 (the non‑degenerate order) or −n (the completely degenerate order). The exponent ⅓ comes from the Airy function asymptotics of the fold canonical relation.
  • Confusing the eta invariant with the spectral asymmetry of the boundary operator; the eta invariant is the regularised value at s = 0 of ∑ sign(λ)|λ|⁻ˢ, while the spectral asymmetry is the limit limt→0 Tr(D∂e−tD∂²), which equals the eta invariant only when the kernel is zero.
  • Assuming the APS boundary condition is the only one that gives a Fredholm Dirac operator on a manifold with boundary; any boundary condition given by a Lagrangian submanifold of the boundary symbol space that is transverse to the Cauchy data space yields a Fredholm operator, but the index formula then involves the Maslov index of the Lagrangian path.

The Geometric Optics Construction and the WKB Method for Hyperbolic Equations

The geometric optics approximation constructs high-frequency asymptotic solutions to linear hyperbolic equations by concentrating energy along bicharacteristic curves in phase space. For a strictly hyperbolic operator P(x, D) of order m with real principal symbol pm(x, ξ), one seeks solutions of the form uλ(x) = eiλφ(x) ∑k=0∞ λ−k ak(x) where λ ≫ 1 is a large frequency parameter. The phase φ must satisfy the eikonal equation pm(x, dφ) = 0, which means the graph of dφ is a Lagrangian submanifold of the characteristic set Σ = {pm = 0}. The transport equations for the amplitudes ak are obtained by substituting the ansatz into Puλ and equating powers of λ. The leading transport equation is a first-order linear PDE along the Hamiltonian flow of pm on Σ: Hpma0 + ca0 = 0 where c involves the subprincipal symbol. This construction breaks down at caustics where the projection of the Lagrangian manifold to the base space develops singularities (folds, cusps). The Maslov canonical operator resolves this by using different phase functions on overlapping charts of the Lagrangian manifold, introducing the Maslov index — a topological invariant counting the number of conjugate points along the bicharacteristic. For systems with multiple characteristics (e.g., elasticity, Maxwell), the geometric optics ansatz must be generalized to include polarization vectors satisfying the algebraic eigenvalue problem pm(x, ξ)v = 0, and the transport equations couple different modes through the subprincipal symbol. The WKB method extends this to semiclassical operators P(h) = ∑ hj Pj where h = λ−1 is the semiclassical parameter. The Bohr-Sommerfeld quantization condition for completely integrable systems emerges from the requirement that the geometric optics solution be single-valued on the Lagrangian torus: ∮γ ξ·dx = 2πh(k + μ/4) where μ is the Maslov index of the cycle γ. This microlocal construction underlies the proof of the Duistermaat-Guillemin trace formula and the analysis of quantum ergodicity.

The Duistermaat-Guillemin Trace Formula for Clean Intersection and Degenerate Periodic Orbits

The Duistermaat-Guillemin trace formula expresses the singularities of the distributional trace of the wave group U(t) = cos(t√P) for an elliptic pseudodifferential operator P of order 2 on a compact manifold M in terms of the geometry of the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol p on the cosphere bundle S*M. The wave trace is the distribution w(t) = Tr U(t) = ∑j cos(t√λj) where λj are the eigenvalues of P. Its singular support is contained in {0} ∪ Lsp(M) where Lsp(M) is the length spectrum — the set of lengths of closed geodesics. For a non-degenerate closed geodesic γ of length L (meaning the Poincaré map Pγ has no eigenvalue 1), the wave trace has a singularity at t = L of the form w(t) ∼ Cγ |t − L|−(n−1)/2 eiπσγ/4 where σγ is the Morse index and Cγ involves |det(I − Pγ)|−1/2. When the fixed point set of the geodesic flow at time L is a clean submanifold C ⊂ S*M of dimension d (clean intersection), the singularity order becomes −(n−1−d)/2. For example, on the sphere Sn all geodesics are closed with the same length π, the fixed point set is the whole cosphere bundle (d = 2n−2), and the singularity order is −n. The amplitude involves the integral over C of a half-density determined by the linearized Poincaré map on the normal bundle. For degenerate periodic orbits where the fixed point set is not clean (e.g., elliptic orbits with resonances, or bifurcations), the stationary phase method must be replaced by more sophisticated techniques: the method of steepest descent for complex phase, or the theory of Fourier integral operators with degenerate canonical relations. The Gutzwiller trace formula in quantum chaos is the semiclassical version: the density of states d(E) = ∑ δ(E − Ej) has an asymptotic expansion as h → 0 given by a sum over periodic orbits of the classical Hamiltonian, with amplitudes involving the stability determinant and the Maslov index. The remainder estimates depend critically on the dynamical properties: for Anosov flows, the remainder is O(h∞) in a suitable sense, while for integrable systems the remainder is larger due to the high multiplicity of periodic orbits. The wave trace invariants — the coefficients of the singularities at the lengths of closed geodesics — are spectral invariants; they determine the length spectrum and the linearized Poincaré maps. The spectral rigidity problem asks whether the wave trace determines the metric up to isometry; microlocal methods show that the wave trace determines the jet of the metric at a non-degenerate closed geodesic, and under generic conditions the length spectrum determines the metric locally.

Worked Example: Wave Trace Singularity Order
Consider the wave trace for the Laplacian on the flat 2-torus T2 = R2/Z2. The eigenvalues are λm,n = 4π2(m2 + n2) for (m, n) ∈ Z2. The length spectrum consists of lengths Lm,n = 2π√(m2 + n2). For a primitive closed geodesic corresponding to a coprime pair (m, n), determine the order of the singularity of the wave trace at t = Lm,n and explain why it differs from the non-degenerate formula −(n−1)/2 = −1/2.
On T2, the geodesic flow is completely integrable. The fixed point set of the geodesic flow at time Lm,n consists of all points on the invariant tori corresponding to the direction (m, n). This fixed point set is a 2-dimensional torus (the cosphere bundle over the closed geodesic), so d = 2. The base manifold dimension is n = 2. The clean intersection formula gives singularity order −(n−1−d)/2 = −(2−1−2)/2 = 1/2. Thus the wave trace has a singularity of order +1/2 (i.e., a continuous but not differentiable function) at each length Lm,n. This is less singular than the non-degenerate order −1/2 because the high multiplicity of periodic orbits (a whole family parameterized by the torus) smears out the singularity. The amplitude involves the volume of the fixed point set. For the flat torus, the wave trace can be computed explicitly via Poisson summation: w(t) = ∑k∈Z2 δ(t − 2π|k|), confirming that the singularities are delta functions (order −1) at t = 0 and jumps (order 0) at other lengths, but the distributional derivative has order 1/2 singularities. The discrepancy with the clean intersection formula arises because the flat torus has a non-generic metric (zero curvature); for a generic perturbation breaking integrability, the clean fixed point set breaks into isolated non-degenerate orbits, and the singularity order becomes −1/2 for each.
Exam Strategy: Trace Formula Singularity Orders
Memorize the three key singularity orders: (1) Non-degenerate closed geodesic: −(n−1)/2. (2) Clean intersection with fixed point set dimension d: −(n−1−d)/2. (3) Completely degenerate (all geodesics closed, e.g., sphere Sn): −n. The glancing trajectory order −(n+1)/3 is distinct and comes from the fold canonical relation in the billiard problem. For the Gutzwiller trace formula in quantum chaos, the amplitude is |det(I − Mγ)|−1/2 where Mγ is the monodromy matrix. Always check whether the question asks for the order of the wave trace itself or its derivative — the order shifts by 1.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Bergman Kernel and the Tian-Yau-Zelditch Expansion

The Bergman kernel Bk(x, y) is the integral kernel of the orthogonal projection onto the space of holomorphic sections H0(X, Lk) of the k-th tensor power of a positive holomorphic line bundle L over a compact Kähler manifold X. Its diagonal asymptotics as k → ∞ encode deep geometric information. The Tian-Yau-Zelditch expansion states that on the diagonal, Bk(x, x) = kn (1 + a1(x)k−1 + a2(x)k−2 + ⋯) where n = dimC X, and the coefficients aj are polynomials in the curvature tensor and its covariant derivatives. The leading coefficient is 1, reflecting the fact that the Bergman kernel approximates the volume form. The first coefficient is a1 = S/2 where S is the scalar curvature of the Kähler metric. This expansion is proved by microlocal methods: the Bergman projection is a Fourier integral operator with complex phase associated to the graph of the identity in X × X, or equivalently, a semiclassical pseudodifferential operator on the circle bundle S ⊂ L* with the Toeplitz structure. The Boutet de Monvel calculus for Toeplitz operators provides a symbolic calculus where the principal symbol of the Bergman projection is the characteristic function of the moment map image (the unit disk in the fiber). The off-diagonal asymptotics exhibit exponential decay away from the diagonal: Bk(x, y) = O(e−ck dist(x,y)2) for x ≠ y, which follows from the fact that the Bergman projection is a complex Fourier integral operator with a non-degenerate complex phase whose imaginary part is positive definite off the diagonal. The microlocal structure of the Bergman kernel is intimately related to the Szegő kernel on the boundary of a strictly pseudoconvex domain in Cn. The Szegő projection is the orthogonal projection onto the Hardy space of boundary values of holomorphic functions. Its kernel has a singularity on the diagonal of the form S(z, w) ∼ cn |ρ(z, w)|−n where ρ is a defining function for the boundary. The Boutet de Monvel calculus for the ∂̅-Neumann problem shows that the Szegő projection is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated to the characteristic variety of the ∂̅-Neumann Laplacian. The asymptotic expansion of the Bergman kernel on a Kähler manifold is the interior analogue of the Fefferman expansion for the Bergman kernel on strictly pseudoconvex domains, where the coefficients involve the CR invariants (Webster curvature, Chern-Moser tensor). The Tian-Yau-Zelditch expansion has applications to Kähler-Einstein metrics: the condition that the Bergman metric ωk = i∂∂̅ log Bk approximates the original Kähler metric ω with error O(k−2) is equivalent to the vanishing of the first two coefficients a1 and a2, which characterizes Kähler-Einstein metrics. The microlocal proof uses the parametrix construction for the ∂̅-Neumann problem on the circle bundle, where the subelliptic estimates of Kohn and Hörmander play a crucial role.

The Semiclassical Pseudodifferential Calculus for Systems and the Diagonalization of Matrix-Valued Symbols

For systems of PDEs, the principal symbol is a matrix-valued function p(x, ξ) ∈ Mat(N × N, C). The semiclassical pseudodifferential calculus for systems requires a symbolic calculus where composition corresponds to matrix multiplication of symbols plus correction terms involving derivatives. The key difficulty is that the eigenvalues of p(x, ξ) may cross, leading to non-diagonalizable symbols and mode conversion. When the eigenvalues are distinct and the eigenprojections are smooth, one can construct a microlocal diagonalization: there exists a semiclassical Fourier integral operator U (a microlocal unitary) such that U* P U is a diagonal matrix of scalar pseudodifferential operators modulo O(h∞). This is the quantum analogue of the classical diagonalization of the Hamiltonian matrix. The construction proceeds by solving the homological equation for the symbol of U order by order in h. At leading order, one needs a unitary matrix u0(x, ξ) that diagonalizes p0(x, ξ). The subprincipal symbol of the diagonalized operator acquires a geometric term: the Berry connection Aj = i⟨ψj | dψj⟩ where ψj are the eigenvectors. This connection governs the adiabatic transport of the polarization vector along the bicharacteristics of the j-th mode. When eigenvalues cross (e.g., at conical intersections in molecular dynamics, or at the Dirac points in graphene), the diagonalization breaks down. Near a generic crossing of two eigenvalues, the symbol can be put in the normal form p(x, ξ) = ξ1 σ1 + ξ2 σ2 + ξ3 σ3 where σj are Pauli matrices. This is the symbol of the Dirac operator, and the crossing is a conical singularity of the eigenvalue surfaces. The microlocal analysis near such crossings uses the theory of Fourier integral operators with singular canonical relations (the crossing set is a Lagrangian submanifold with a conical singularity). The Landau-Zener formula for transition probabilities between crossing modes is derived by solving the semiclassical system near the crossing using parabolic cylinder functions. For systems with symmetries (e.g., time-reversal, particle-hole), the symbol satisfies additional constraints (e.g., p(x, −ξ) = p(x, ξ)* for time-reversal invariant systems), which force eigenvalue crossings to occur in pairs (Kramers degeneracy). The topological classification of such systems (topological insulators) is expressed in terms of the Berry curvature integrated over the Brillouin zone, which is a microlocal invariant of the symbol. The semiclassical calculus for systems also underlies the analysis of the Born-Oppenheimer approximation in molecular quantum mechanics, where the electronic Hamiltonian has eigenvalues that are functions of the nuclear coordinates, and the nuclear dynamics is governed by an effective Hamiltonian with Berry connection terms.

The Microlocal Structure of the Cauchy Problem for Nonlinear Hyperbolic Equations and the Formation of Singularities

The microlocal analysis of nonlinear hyperbolic equations focuses on how singularities propagate and interact, and how new singularities are generated through nonlinear effects. For a semilinear equation □g u = F(u, ∂u) with smooth nonlinearity F, the wavefront set of the solution satisfies WF(u) ⊂ WF(u0) ∪ WF(u1) ∪ N where N is the set of points reached by the nonlinear interaction of singularities. The fundamental result of Bony and Meyer states that for semilinear equations, singularities propagate along the bicharacteristics of the linearized operator, and new singularities can only be created at the intersection of existing singularities. The interaction of two conormal singularities along transversely intersecting hypersurfaces produces a new singularity along the outgoing hypersurface determined by the sum of the conormal vectors (the "sum law" for wavefront sets). This is analyzed using the paradifferential calculus of Bony, which decomposes the product uv into paraproducts Tuv + Tvu + R(u, v) where T is a paraproduct operator (a pseudodifferential operator with symbol depending on one factor) and R is a smoothing remainder. The wavefront set of the product is contained in the union of the wavefront sets of the factors, plus the set of sums of covectors from each factor. For quasilinear equations where the principal symbol depends on the solution, the bicharacteristics themselves depend on the solution, leading to the possibility of shock formation. The microlocal analysis of shock formation uses the method of geometric optics with amplitude blow-up: the amplitude a0 satisfies a transport equation that develops a gradient catastrophe in finite time, corresponding to the intersection of bicharacteristics. The formation of a shock front is described by the Hopf equation ∂t u + u ∂x u = 0, whose solution develops a discontinuity when characteristics cross. Microlocally, the shock front is a conormal singularity along a smooth hypersurface, and the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions determine the jump in the amplitude. For systems of conservation laws, the Lax entropy condition selects the physically relevant shock. The microlocal structure of the solution near a shock is analyzed using the calculus of paired Lagrangian distributions, where the solution is a sum of a smooth part and a conormal distribution associated to the shock front. The interaction of shocks (e.g., shock-shock collision, shock-rarefaction interaction) generates new singularities that can be tracked using the wavefront set calculus. For the Einstein equations in general relativity, the nonlinear interaction of gravitational waves produces singularities that propagate along null geodesics. The microlocal analysis of the Einstein vacuum equations uses the wave coordinate gauge to reduce to a quasilinear wave system, and the propagation of singularities is governed by the linearized Einstein operator. The formation of singularities in the Einstein equations (e.g., in the gravitational collapse) is a major open problem; the microlocal approach provides a framework for understanding the stability of Minkowski space and the formation of trapped surfaces.

  • Confusing the clean intersection formula for the wave trace with the non-degenerate formula: the clean formula has an extra +d/2 in the exponent where d is the dimension of the fixed point set; for the sphere Sn this gives −n not −(n−1)/2.
  • Assuming the Bergman kernel expansion coefficients are universal polynomials in curvature without the Kähler condition; the expansion requires the Kähler metric and the coefficients involve the Chern connection, not the Levi-Civita connection alone.
  • Thinking that matrix-valued symbols can always be diagonalized microlocally; eigenvalue crossings (conical intersections, Dirac points) prevent global diagonalization and require the analysis of Fourier integral operators with singular canonical relations.
  • Overlooking the Berry connection term in the subprincipal symbol of a diagonalized system; this geometric term is crucial for the adiabatic theorem and topological invariants like the Chern number.
  • Believing that nonlinear interactions of singularities can create singularities in arbitrary directions; the sum law for wavefront sets restricts new singularities to the sum of the interacting covectors, and the paradifferential calculus makes this precise.
  • Confusing the order of the singularity in the wave trace at a glancing trajectory (−(n+1)/3) with the order at a non-degenerate closed geodesic (−(n−1)/2) or the completely degenerate case (−n); the ⅓ exponent comes from the Airy function asymptotics of the fold canonical relation.
  • Assuming the Tian-Yau-Zelditch expansion holds for any positive line bundle without the Kähler condition; the expansion is specific to the Bergman kernel of a Kähler manifold, while the Fefferman expansion for the Bergman kernel on strictly pseudoconvex domains involves CR invariants and a different coefficient structure.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Lie Groupoids and Groupoid Calculi

The extension of pseudodifferential calculus to singular spaces and foliations is achieved by replacing the cotangent bundle with the Lie algebroid of a Lie groupoid. A Lie groupoid G ⇉ M consists of a manifold of arrows G and a manifold of units M with source, target, multiplication, inverse, and unit maps satisfying the usual axioms. Its Lie algebroid A(G) is the vector bundle over M given by the kernel of the differential of the source map restricted to the unit space, equipped with an anchor map ρ : A(G) → TM and a Lie bracket on sections. The pseudodifferential calculus on G, developed by Connes, Nistor–Weinstein–Xu, and Monthubert–Pierrot, defines operators whose Schwartz kernels are distributions on G conormal to the unit space. The symbol of such an operator lives on the dual algebroid A*(G) and transforms under the groupoid multiplication according to a twisted convolution product. The principal symbol map is a homomorphism from the algebra of order‑m operators to the commutative algebra of homogeneous functions of degree m on A*(G) 0. Ellipticity is defined by invertibility of the principal symbol, and an elliptic operator admits a parametrix within the calculus, yielding Fredholm properties on suitable Sobolev spaces defined via the groupoid Haar system. The index of an elliptic operator on a proper, étale groupoid is computed by the Connes–Skandalis longitudinal index theorem, which expresses the index as the pairing of the symbol class in K‑theory of A*(G) with the fundamental class of the groupoid in cyclic cohomology. For the tangent groupoid TM ⇉ M this recovers the Atiyah–Singer index theorem; for the holonomy groupoid of a foliation it gives the Connes index theorem for foliations; for the b‑groupoid of a manifold with boundary it reproduces the Melrose b‑calculus index formula. The groupoid framework also accommodates the adiabatic limit of fibrations, the deformation to the normal cone, and the analysis of operators on manifolds with corners via the iterated edge groupoid. Recent work by van Erp and Yuncken extends the calculus to non‑Hausdorff groupoids arising from singular foliations, providing a unified microlocal setting for the analysis of hypoelliptic operators such as the Kohn Laplacian on CR manifolds.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for Non‑Selfadjoint Operators and Pseudospectra

For a closed, non‑selfadjoint operator A on a Hilbert space, the resolvent (A − z)⁻¹ can exhibit norm growth far exceeding the distance to the spectrum. The ε‑pseudospectrum σ_ε(A) = { z ∈ ℂ : ‖(A − z)⁻¹‖ > ε⁻¹ } captures this phenomenon and is central to stability analysis of non‑normal evolution equations. Microlocally, the resolvent kernel is a Fourier integral operator associated to the complexified Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol a(x,ξ). When a is not real‑valued, the Hamilton vector field H_a has complex trajectories, and the wavefront set of the resolvent is contained in the union of the stable and unstable manifolds of the flow in the complex cotangent bundle. The work of Sjöstrand, Zworski, and Davies shows that for semiclassical operators A_h = Op_h(a) with analytic symbols, the ε‑pseudospectrum for ε = e−C/h is approximated by the set of z for which there exists a complex Lagrangian manifold Λ_z on which a = z and the imaginary part of the action integral is minimal. This leads to the notion of complex absorbing potentials and the method of complex scaling: one deforms the real phase space into a complex contour Γ_θ such that the rotated operator A_θ has discrete spectrum coinciding with the resonances of A. The microlocal structure of the resolvent near a resonance is described by a meromorphic family of Fourier integral operators with a simple pole whose residue is a rank‑one operator given by the product of the resonant state and its dual. For operators with multiple characteristics, the resolvent may have branch points; the analysis then requires the calculus of paired Lagrangian distributions. Applications include the exponential decay of damped waves on non‑trapping geometries, the spectral instability of the Orr–Sommerfeld operator in hydrodynamics, and the quantitative description of the pseudospectrum of the non‑selfadjoint harmonic oscillator −h²∂_x² + x² + iγx³ where the ε‑pseudospectrum extends far into the left half‑plane for exponentially small ε.

Worked Example: Pseudospectrum of a Semiclassical Non‑Selfadjoint Operator
Let A_h = −h²∂_x² + x² + i x³ on L²(ℝ) with 0 < h ≪ 1. Describe the leading order shape of the ε‑pseudospectrum for ε = e−1/h.
The principal symbol is a(x,ξ) = ξ² + x² + i x³. The Hamilton equations ẋ = 2ξ, ξ̇ = −2x − 3i x² generate a complex flow. For a given z, the condition that there exists a complex trajectory with a = z and minimal imaginary action determines the pseudospectrum. The critical points of Im ∫ ξ dx along the level set a = z satisfy ∂_x (Im ∫ ξ dx) = 0, which yields the equation Im(ξ) = 0 on the contour. Solving ξ² = z − x² − i x³ and imposing Im ξ = 0 gives a real algebraic curve in the (x, Re ξ) plane. The minimal imaginary action is S(z) = ∫ Im ξ dx evaluated on this curve. The ε‑pseudospectrum is asymptotically { z : S(z) < 1 }. For the cubic perturbation, the curve extends into the left half‑plane, and S(z) ∼ C |Re z|3/2 for large negative Re z. Hence the pseudospectrum reaches Re z ∼ −(log ε)2/3, i.e. exponentially far left for ε = e−1/h. This matches the rigorous result of Davies and Zworski (1999) for the cubic oscillator.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the microlocal description of a non‑selfadjoint resolvent, first identify the principal symbol and its Hamilton flow in the complex cotangent bundle. Then state the relation between the ε‑pseudospectrum and the minimal imaginary action on complex Lagrangian level sets. Cite the Sjöstrand–Zworski theorem for analytic symbols and the complex scaling method for resonances. Avoid claiming that the spectrum alone determines the resolvent norm; the pseudospectrum is the correct microlocal object.

The Microlocal Structure of Fourier Integral Operators with Complex Phase

Fourier integral operators (FIOs) with complex phase functions arise naturally in the parametrix construction for non‑elliptic boundary value problems, in the theory of resonances via complex scaling, and in the description of the propagator for damped wave equations. A complex‑phase FIO is an operator of the form Au(x) = ∫ eiΦ(x,θ) a(x,θ) u(θ) dθ where the phase Φ is a complex‑valued function satisfying Im Φ ≥ 0 and the non‑degeneracy condition d(x,θ) (∂_θ Φ) ≠ 0 on the critical set C_Φ = { (x,θ) : ∂_θ Φ = 0 }. The canonical relation is the image of C_Φ under the map (x,θ) ↦ (x, ∂_x Φ; θ, −∂_θ Φ), which now takes values in the complexified cotangent bundle T*ℂⁿ. Hörmander’s theory of FIOs with real phase extends to this setting provided the imaginary part of the phase is a non‑negative quadratic form on the fibers of the projection C_Φ → X × Y. The resulting operators map Sobolev spaces with a loss of derivatives determined by the order of the amplitude and the signature of the imaginary part. The composition calculus for complex‑phase FIOs was developed by Melrose and Sjöstrand: the composition of two such operators is again a complex‑phase FIO provided the canonical relations intersect cleanly and the sum of the imaginary parts remains non‑negative. This calculus underlies the construction of the parametrix for the Cauchy problem for the damped wave equation ∂_t² u − Δ u + γ(x) ∂_t u = 0 where the damping term introduces a complex absorption in the phase. The microlocal structure of the propagator is a sum of a real‑phase FIO associated to the undamped geodesic flow and a complex‑phase FIO whose canonical relation is the graph of the complexified flow with exponential decay along the imaginary direction. The wavefront set of the solution is contained in the real part of the canonical relation, while the analytic wavefront set captures the exponential decay rates. Applications include the proof of exponential energy decay for geometrically controlled damping (Bardos–Lebeau–Rauch) and the description of the scattering matrix for black‑hole spacetimes with a complex‑scaled horizon.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Equations with Multiple Characteristics

When the principal symbol p(x,ξ) of a pseudodifferential operator vanishes to order k ≥ 2 on a submanifold Σ ⊂ T*M 0, the equation Pu = f is said to have multiple characteristics. The classical theory of real principal type (Hörmander, Duistermaat–Hörmander) no longer applies because the Hamilton vector field H_p vanishes on Σ. The microlocal analysis of such operators was pioneered by Ivrii, Melrose, and Taylor. One distinguishes between effectively hyperbolic characteristics, where the subprincipal symbol satisfies a Levi‑type condition ensuring well‑posedness, and non‑effectively hyperbolic (or gliding) characteristics where loss of derivatives occurs. For an operator of order m with double characteristics on a smooth hypersurface Σ, the normal form near Σ is P = D_t² − a(t,x,D_x) D_t − b(t,x,D_x) where a and b are pseudodifferential operators of order 1 and 2 respectively. The Cauchy problem is well‑posed in Gevrey classes G^s with s = 1 + 1/(k−1) for effectively hyperbolic operators of order k (Ivrii–Petkov). The propagation of singularities is described by the generalized bicharacteristics, which are the integral curves of the Hamilton vector field of the reduced symbol on the symplectic normal bundle of Σ. For gliding rays, where the bicharacteristic is tangent to the boundary, the Melrose–Sjöstrand calculus of paired Lagrangian distributions gives a precise description of the reflected and diffracted waves. The wavefront set of the solution satisfies a generalized propagation theorem: WF(u) WF(f) is a union of maximally extended generalized bicharacteristics. In the case of the wave equation on a manifold with boundary, the glancing rays produce a singularity of order −(n+1)/3 in the wave trace, corresponding to the Airy function asymptotics of the fold canonical relation. The analysis of multiple characteristics also underpins the study of the subelliptic estimates for the ∂̅‑Neumann problem on weakly pseudoconvex domains, where the Levi form degenerates to finite order.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Many‑Body Hamiltonians

The scattering matrix S(λ) for an N‑body quantum system with pair potentials decaying at infinity is a unitary operator on the direct sum of channel Hilbert spaces. Its microlocal structure was elucidated by Enss, Sigal, Soffer, Dereziński, and Gérard using the framework of Fourier integral operators associated to the classical scattering relation. The classical N‑body Hamiltonian H = ∑ p_j²/2m_j + ∑ Vij(x_i − x_j) generates a flow on the phase space with a stratified structure corresponding to cluster decompositions. The scattering relation 𝒞_λ ⊂ T*Σ_in × T*Σ_out connects incoming and outgoing asymptotic momenta at a fixed energy λ. It is a canonical relation which is a union of smooth Lagrangian submanifolds indexed by the possible cluster partitions. The scattering matrix S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated to 𝒞_λ, modulo a smoothing operator. Its principal symbol is given by the classical scattering amplitude, which can be expressed as a product of two‑body scattering matrices along the classical trajectory. The wavefront set of the kernel of S(λ) is exactly 𝒞_λ, and the singularities of the scattering matrix correspond to classical trajectories that undergo a sequence of binary collisions. For potentials with Coulomb decay, the scattering relation has a logarithmic phase correction, and the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator with a complex phase of logarithmic type. The microlocal analysis also yields the high‑energy asymptotics of S(λ) as λ → ∞, where the leading term is the identity plus a Fourier integral operator associated to the single‑collision relation. The trace of the scattering matrix, Tr(S(λ) − I), admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane with poles at the resonances; the residues are related to the residues of the resolvent. This structure is the basis for the inverse scattering problem: the knowledge of the scattering matrix at all energies determines the pair potentials uniquely (Novikov, Faddeev). Recent work by Vasy and Wunsch extends the calculus to many‑body systems on asymptotically hyperbolic spaces, where the scattering matrix is an operator on the boundary at infinity and its microlocal structure is governed by the Vasy calculus.

  • Confusing the groupoid pseudodifferential calculus with the standard calculus on the cotangent bundle; the symbol lives on the dual algebroid and the composition involves the groupoid convolution.
  • Assuming the ε‑pseudospectrum of a non‑selfadjoint operator is always a small neighbourhood of the spectrum; for non‑normal operators it can extend far into regions where the resolvent norm is exponentially large.
  • Treating complex‑phase Fourier integral operators as ordinary real‑phase FIOs; the non‑negativity of the imaginary part of the phase is essential for the mapping properties and the composition calculus.
  • Believing that multiple characteristics always imply ill‑posedness; effectively hyperbolic operators satisfy a Levi condition and are well‑posed in Gevrey classes.
  • Thinking that the many‑body scattering matrix is a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator associated to a stratified canonical relation, not a canonical graph.
  • Overlooking the logarithmic phase correction in Coulomb scattering; the scattering matrix is then a Fourier integral operator with a complex logarithmic phase, not a standard FIO.
  • Each of these traps reflects a failure to distinguish the microlocal object appropriate to the geometric or spectral situation at hand.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Conical Singularities

The heat kernel on a manifold with conical singularities exhibits a rich microlocal structure that intertwines the geometry of the cone tip with the propagation of singularities. Cheeger’s 1983 work on the spectral geometry of cones showed that the heat kernel admits an asymptotic expansion in terms of the eigenvalues of the cross‑sectional Laplacian, and that the singularities of the kernel are confined to the conormal bundle of the tip. Melrose’s b‑calculus, developed in the early 1990s, provides a systematic pseudodifferential framework: the heat operator is a b‑pseudodifferential operator of order −2, and its kernel is a conormal distribution on the b‑double space blown up at the corner corresponding to the tip. The wavefront set of the heat kernel is contained in the union of the diagonal and the flow‑out of the characteristic variety of the b‑Laplacian. In particular, the singularities propagate along generalized b‑bicharacteristics that reflect off the boundary of the cone according to the law of geometric optics. The short‑time asymptotics reveal a term of order t−n/2 multiplied by a polyhomogeneous symbol in the radial variable, and the coefficients encode the zeta‑regularized determinant of the cross‑sectional Laplacian. This microlocal description underpins the proof of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for manifolds with conical ends, where the eta invariant of the boundary operator appears as the constant term in the heat trace expansion. Recent extensions by Albin, Gell‑Redman, and others have incorporated the heat kernel into the iterated edge calculus, allowing the treatment of manifolds with iterated cone singularities and yielding precise mapping properties on weighted Sobolev spaces.

The Microlocal Structure of the Resolvent for Degenerate Elliptic Operators

Degenerate elliptic operators, such as the Grushin operator ∂x2 + x2k∂y2 on ℝ2, lose ellipticity on a submanifold where the symbol vanishes to finite order. The microlocal analysis of their resolvent (λ − P)−1 requires a calculus that captures the degeneracy. Grushin’s original 1970s work introduced a class of pseudodifferential operators with symbols homogeneous in a weighted sense, and later Helffer and Nourrigat refined the symbolic calculus using the Heisenberg group structure. In the modern language of Melrose’s Lie manifold calculus, the operator belongs to the Heisenberg calculus ΨHm, where the principal symbol lives on the Heisenberg cotangent bundle. The resolvent is a parametrix in this calculus, and its kernel is a paired Lagrangian distribution associated to the diagonal and the characteristic variety Σ = {ξ = 0, η = 0}. The wavefront set of the resolvent kernel is contained in the union of the conormal bundle of the diagonal and the flow‑out of Σ under the Hamilton vector field of the subprincipal symbol. This structure yields subelliptic estimates of the form ‖u‖Hs+ε ≤ C(‖Pu‖Hs + ‖u‖Hs) with ε = 1/(2k+1), which is sharp. The microlocal description also controls the spectral asymptotics: the counting function N(λ) ∼ C λQ/2 where Q = 2 + 2k is the homogeneous dimension. For boundary value problems on domains with characteristic boundaries, the Boutet de Monvel calculus adapted to the Heisenberg group gives a precise description of the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map and its singularities.

Worked Example: Subelliptic Estimate for the Grushin Operator
Let P = ∂x2 + x2∂y2 on ℝ2. Show that for any s ∈ ℝ there exists C > 0 such that ‖u‖Hs+1/3 ≤ C(‖Pu‖Hs + ‖u‖Hs) for all u ∈ Cc∞(ℝ2).
The principal symbol is p(x,y,ξ,η) = ξ2 + x2η2. The characteristic set Σ = {ξ = 0, η = 0} is a symplectic submanifold of codimension 2. The Hamilton vector field Hp = 2ξ∂x + 2x2η∂y − 2xη2∂ξ vanishes on Σ, so the standard propagation theorem does not apply. Instead, one uses the Heisenberg calculus: assign weights w(x) = 1, w(y) = 1, w(ξ) = 1, w(η) = 1/2. Then p is homogeneous of degree 2. The subelliptic estimate follows from the existence of a parametrix Q ∈ ΨH−2 such that QP = I − R with R smoothing. The loss of 1/3 derivative corresponds to the homogeneous dimension Q = 4 and the order of vanishing k = 1, giving ε = 1/(2k+1) = 1/3. The estimate is sharp because the model operator on the Heisenberg group has exactly this loss.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the loss of derivatives in a subelliptic estimate, identify the order of vanishing of the symbol on the characteristic set. The loss ε = 1/(2k+1) where k is the order of vanishing. Remember that the Heisenberg calculus is the correct microlocal framework, not the standard pseudodifferential calculus.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation and Strichartz Estimates

The nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) i∂tu + Δu = λ|u|p−1u on ℝn has been a testing ground for microlocal methods in dispersive PDE. Bourgain’s 1993 introduction of the Xs,b spaces provided a microlocal refinement of the Strichartz estimates: the norm ‖u‖Xs,b = ‖⟨ξ⟩s⟨τ + |ξ|2⟩bû(τ,ξ)‖L2 measures both spatial regularity and proximity to the characteristic surface τ = −|ξ|2. The linear propagator eitΔ is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated to the canonical relation {(t,x,τ,ξ) : τ = −|ξ|2, x = 2tξ}. Its wavefront set is exactly this Lagrangian manifold. The bilinear estimate ‖uv‖Xs,−1/2+ε ≤ C‖u‖Xs,1/2‖v‖Xs,1/2 captures the interaction of two waves and is proved by a dyadic decomposition and a careful analysis of the resonance function τ1 + τ2 + |ξ1|2 + |ξ2|2 − |ξ1+ξ2|2. Tao’s 2006 work on the I‑method (interaction Morawetz) combined microlocal localization with an almost conserved quantity to push the well‑posedness threshold for the cubic NLS in 3D down to H1/2+. The microlocal perspective also clarifies the failure of uniform Strichartz estimates on manifolds with trapped geodesics: the wavefront set of the propagator intersects the trapped set, leading to a loss of derivatives. Recent advances by Guth, Wang, and others use polynomial partitioning and decoupling to obtain sharp Strichartz estimates for the Schrödinger equation on the torus, where the wavefront set is a union of rational Lagrangian tori.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Fibred Cusps

Manifolds with fibred cusps, introduced by Mazzeo and Melrose in 1998, generalize asymptotically hyperbolic spaces by allowing the cross‑section of the cusp to be a fibration Z → Y with typical fibre F. The natural pseudodifferential calculus is the φ‑calculus (phi‑calculus), where the vector fields are generated by x∂x, x∂y, and ∂z with x a boundary defining function. The principal symbol lives on the φ‑cotangent bundle φT*M, which is a Lie algebroid. Operators in Ψφm have kernels that are conormal to the φ‑diagonal in the φ‑double space, blown up at the corner corresponding to the cusp. The indicial family I(σ) obtained by freezing coefficients at the boundary and taking the Mellin transform in x gives a family of operators on the fibre F parametrized by σ ∈ ℂ. The operator is Fredholm on weighted Sobolev spaces xαHφs if and only if the indicial family is invertible for Im σ = α. The wavefront set of a distribution in this calculus is a subset of the φ‑cosphere bundle, and the propagation of singularities follows the Hamilton flow of the φ‑principal symbol. This framework yields a complete description of the resolvent of the Laplacian on fibred cusp manifolds, including the meromorphic continuation of the scattering matrix and the identification of resonances as poles of the indicial family. The φ‑calculus also underlies the proof of the index theorem for fibred cusp operators by Mazzeo, Melrose, and Piazza, where the boundary contribution is expressed in terms of the eta invariant of the vertical operator on the fibre.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Long‑Range Potentials

For Schrödinger operators H = −Δ + V(x) on ℝn with long‑range potentials V(x) ∼ |x|−ρ, 0 < ρ ≤ 1, the scattering matrix S(λ) at energy λ > 0 is no longer a Fourier integral operator associated to a canonical graph, but a Fourier integral operator with a complex phase of logarithmic type. Isozaki and Kitada (1985) constructed modified free Hamiltonians H0± = −Δ + Vlr(x) where Vlr captures the asymptotic behaviour of V, and showed that the wave operators W± = s‑limt→±∞ eitHe−itH0± exist and are complete. The scattering matrix S(λ) = W+*W− is then a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated to the canonical relation generated by the classical scattering map, but its phase function acquires a logarithmic term Φ(x,ξ) = x·ξ + c(ξ) log|x| + o(1) as |x| → ∞. The principal symbol of S(λ) is given by the classical scattering amplitude, which can be computed via the eikonal equation for the modified Hamiltonian. The wavefront set of the kernel of S(λ) is exactly the classical scattering relation, which is a smooth Lagrangian submanifold of T*𝕊n−1 × T*𝕊n−1 away from the forward/backward directions. For Coulomb potentials (ρ = 1), the logarithmic phase is essential: the scattering matrix is not a standard FIO but belongs to the class of Fourier integral operators with complex logarithmic phase introduced by Hörmander. This microlocal structure yields the high‑energy asymptotics S(λ) = I + λ−1/2F(λ) + O(λ−1) where F(λ) is an FIO associated to the single‑scattering relation. The trace Tr(S(λ) − I) admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane with poles at the resonances, and the residues are related to the residues of the resolvent (H − z)−1. Recent work by Vasy and Wunsch extends this analysis to asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds, where the scattering matrix acts on the boundary at infinity and its microlocal structure is governed by the 0‑calculus.

  • Confusing the φ‑calculus with the b‑calculus; the φ‑vector fields include x∂y which are tangent to the fibres of the cusp, while b‑vector fields are only x∂x and ∂y.
  • Assuming the Xs,b norm is equivalent to a standard Sobolev norm; the weight ⟨τ + |ξ|2⟩b is crucial for capturing dispersion.
  • Treating the scattering matrix for long‑range potentials as a standard Fourier integral operator; the logarithmic phase correction is mandatory for Coulomb‑type decay.
  • Believing that the indicial family in the φ‑calculus is a scalar function; it is an operator family on the fibre F, and its invertibility determines Fredholm properties.
  • Overlooking the role of the Heisenberg group in the Grushin calculus; the loss of derivatives ε = 1/(2k+1) comes from the homogeneous dimension of the Heisenberg group, not from the Euclidean dimension.
  • Thinking that the heat kernel on a cone is smooth up to the tip; it is conormal to the tip and its singularities are described by the b‑calculus.
  • Misapplying the standard propagation of singularities theorem to degenerate elliptic operators; the characteristic set is not a smooth hypersurface, so one must use the Heisenberg or φ‑calculus propagation theorems.

The advanced microlocal tools described above provide a unified language for analyzing singularities in a wide range of geometric and physical problems.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Real Principal Type and Subprincipal Symbol

When the principal symbol p(x,ξ) of a pseudodifferential operator P is real‑valued and its Hamilton vector field Hp does not vanish on the characteristic set Σ = {p=0}, the operator is of real principal type. The classical propagation of singularities theorem (Hörmander, 1971) states that the wavefront set of a solution u to P u = f is a union of maximally extended null‑bicharacteristics of p. The subprincipal symbol psub = p1 − (i/2)∑ ∂2p0/∂xj∂ξj enters the transport equation for the principal amplitude a0 along each bicharacteristic: Hpa0 + (i psub + (1/2) div Hp) a0 = 0. This term governs the growth or decay of the amplitude and is essential for the construction of parametrices with sharp remainder estimates. In the semiclassical setting, the subprincipal symbol appears in the Egorov theorem as the first quantum correction to the classical flow. For systems, the subprincipal symbol is matrix‑valued and its eigenvalues determine the polarization transport. The microlocal energy method of Tataru and Smith uses a positive commutator argument with a carefully chosen escape function that incorporates the subprincipal term to prove local well‑posedness for quasilinear wave equations with rough coefficients. The subprincipal symbol also controls the loss of derivatives in the parametrix construction for operators with double characteristics, where the principal symbol vanishes to second order. In such cases the Hamilton flow is degenerate and the subprincipal part dictates the microlocal structure of the solution near the glancing region. A thorough understanding of the subprincipal symbol is therefore indispensable for any advanced treatment of hyperbolic Cauchy problems.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Asymptotically Hyperbolic Manifolds

Let (M,g) be an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold of dimension n ≥ 2, i.e. a complete Riemannian manifold whose metric near the conformal boundary ∂M takes the form g = (dx2 + h(x))/x2 with a defining function x and a family of metrics h(x) on ∂M. The Laplacian Δg has continuous spectrum [ (n−1)2/4 , ∞ ) and possibly a finite set of eigenvalues below this threshold. The spectral projector Π(λ) = 1(−∞,λ](Δg) is a Fourier integral operator of order −1/2 associated to the geodesic flow on the cosphere bundle *S M, but its Schwartz kernel exhibits a rich singularity structure at the boundary. Guillarmou (2005) proved that the kernel of Π(λ) is a Legendrian distribution on the 0‑double space, conormal to the lifted diagonal and to the flow‑out of the boundary diagonal. The leading singularity at the boundary is governed by the scattering matrix S(λ), which is a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 on ∂M with principal symbol given by the classical scattering amplitude. The wavefront set of Π(λ) is contained in the union of the diagonal, the forward/backward flow‑out, and the boundary Legendrian. This description yields the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent R(σ) = (Δg − σ(n−σ))−1 to the complex plane, with poles at the resonances. The residues of the resolvent at resonances are finite‑rank operators whose kernels are smooth up to the boundary and can be expressed in terms of resonant states. The spectral projector asymptotics also imply a Weyl law for the resonance counting function N(r) ∼ C rn as r → ∞, where C depends only on the volume of the unit ball in the hyperbolic space. These results are central to the study of quantum chaos on hyperbolic manifolds and to the proof of the Selberg trace formula in the non‑compact setting.

Worked Example: Subprincipal Symbol in a Transport Equation
Consider the semiclassical operator Ph = −h2Δ + V(x) on ℝn with a smooth real potential V. Let p(x,ξ) = |ξ|2 + V(x) be its principal symbol. Compute the subprincipal symbol psub and write the transport equation for the principal amplitude a0 along a bicharacteristic of p.
The Weyl quantization of Ph gives the full symbol p(x,ξ) = |ξ|2 + V(x) with no h‑dependent lower order terms. The subprincipal symbol is psub = p1 − (i/2)∑ ∂2p0/∂xj∂ξj. Here p0 = |ξ|2 + V(x) and p1 = 0. The mixed derivatives ∂2p0/∂xj∂ξj = 0 because p0 separates in x and ξ. Hence psub = 0. The Hamilton vector field is Hp = 2ξ·∂x − ∇V·∂ξ. The transport equation for a0 reduces to Hpa0 + (1/2)(div Hp) a0 = 0. Since div Hp = 0, we obtain Hpa0 = 0, i.e. a0 is constant along each bicharacteristic.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the subprincipal symbol of a given pseudodifferential operator, first write the full symbol in the Weyl (or Kohn‑Nirenberg) quantization, identify the principal part p0 and the first lower order term p1, then apply the formula psub = p1 − (i/2)∑ ∂2p0/∂xj∂ξj. Remember that for operators with real principal symbol and no first‑order terms, the subprincipal symbol often vanishes, simplifying the transport equation to pure Hamilton flow invariance.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Asymptotically Conic Manifolds

An asymptotically conic manifold (M,g) is a non‑compact manifold with a boundary at infinity such that near ∂M the metric takes the form g = dr2 + r2h(r) where r = 1/x is a radial coordinate and h(r) is a family of metrics on the link converging to a smooth metric h0. The Laplacian Δg has absolutely continuous spectrum [0,∞) and no embedded eigenvalues under generic conditions. The resolvent R(λ) = (Δg − λ2)−1 for Im λ > 0 extends meromorphically to the complex plane as an operator between weighted Sobolev spaces. Melrose (1994) and Guillarmou–Hassell (2008) showed that the Schwartz kernel of R(λ) is a Legendrian distribution on the 0‑double space, conormal to the lifted diagonal and to the forward/backward flow‑out of the boundary diagonal. The leading singularity at the boundary is described by the scattering matrix S(λ), which is a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 on the link with principal symbol given by the classical scattering amplitude. The wavefront set of R(λ) is contained in the union of the diagonal, the flow‑out, and the boundary Legendrian. This microlocal description yields the limiting absorption principle, the radiation estimates, and the high‑energy asymptotics of the resolvent. In particular, the resolvent satisfies the estimate ‖R(λ)‖L2→L2 ≤ C/|Im λ| for λ away from the real axis, and the Poisson operator mapping boundary data to solutions of (Δg − λ2)u = 0 is a Fourier integral operator associated to the geodesic flow. The resonance set, defined as the poles of the meromorphic continuation, is discrete and its counting function obeys a Weyl law N(r) ∼ C rn. These results are fundamental for inverse scattering and for the study of wave decay on non‑trapping geometries.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Nonlinear Wave Equation and the Formation of Caustics

Consider the semilinear wave equation □g u = F(u,∇u) on a Lorentzian manifold (M,g) with smooth initial data. The solution can be constructed microlocally by a geometric optics ansatz u ∼ ∑k≥0 ak eiφ/h where φ solves the eikonal equation gμν∂μφ ∂νφ = 0 and the amplitudes ak satisfy transport equations along the null bicharacteristics of φ. When the initial data are conormal to a smooth hypersurface, the phase φ generates a Lagrangian manifold Λ in T*M. The solution remains a Lagrangian distribution as long as the projection π: Λ → M is a local diffeomorphism. Caustics form when π develops fold or cusp singularities, i.e. when the differential dπ drops rank. At a fold caustic, the solution is a Fourier integral operator of order −1/4 associated to a canonical relation with a fold singularity; the amplitude satisfies a transport equation with a Maslov factor. At a cusp caustic, the order drops to −1/3 and the asymptotic expansion involves Airy functions. The nonlinear term F(u,∇u) introduces interactions between different Lagrangian branches; the microlocal product of two Lagrangian distributions is again a Lagrangian distribution provided the associated Lagrangians intersect cleanly. The work of Rauch, Reed, and others shows that for quadratic nonlinearities the solution can be continued past the first caustic with a loss of one derivative, while for higher order nonlinearities the lifespan may be limited by the formation of shocks. The microlocal energy method of Klainerman and Rodnianski uses null frames and the vector field method to control the solution up to the formation of singularities. Understanding the precise microlocal structure at caustics is essential for proving sharp well‑posedness results and for the numerical simulation of high‑frequency waves.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Short‑Range Potentials

For the Schrödinger operator H = −Δ + V(x) on ℝn with a short‑range potential V(x) = O(|x|−1−ε) as |x| → ∞, the wave operators W± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e−itH0 exist and are complete. The scattering matrix S(λ) = W+* W− is a unitary operator on L2(𝕊n−1) for each energy λ > 0. Isozaki and Kitada (1985) proved that S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated to the canonical relation generated by the classical scattering map κ: T*𝕊n−1 → T*𝕊n−1. The phase function is Φ(ω,θ) = ω·θ + o(1) as λ → ∞, i.e. there is no logarithmic correction because the potential decays faster than |x|−1. The principal symbol of S(λ) is the classical scattering amplitude a(ω,θ;λ), which can be computed by solving the eikonal equation for the Hamiltonian |ξ|2 + V(x). The wavefront set of the kernel of S(λ) is exactly the graph of κ, a smooth Lagrangian submanifold of T*𝕊n−1 × T*𝕊n−1. This microlocal structure yields the high‑energy asymptotics S(λ) = I + λ−1/2 F(λ) + O(λ−1) where F(λ) is an FIO associated to the single‑scattering relation. The trace Tr(S(λ) − I) admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane with poles at the resonances of H. The residues are related to the residues of the resolvent (H − z)−1. For potentials with compact support, the scattering matrix is a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 plus a smoothing operator, and its symbol has a complete asymptotic expansion in powers of λ−1/2. These results are the foundation of inverse scattering theory and of the proof of the trace formula for the wave group.

  • Confusing the subprincipal symbol with the first lower‑order term in the Kohn‑Nirenberg quantization; the subprincipal symbol includes a correction from the mixed derivatives of the principal symbol.
  • Assuming the spectral projector on an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold is a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator with Legendrian singularities at the boundary.
  • Treating the resolvent on an asymptotically conic manifold as having only diagonal singularities; the boundary Legendrian and the flow‑out contribute equally to the wavefront set.
  • Believing that caustics only occur for linear waves; nonlinear interactions can create new caustics through the superposition of Lagrangian branches.
  • Overlooking the logarithmic phase correction in the scattering matrix for long‑range potentials; for short‑range potentials the phase is purely quadratic and the scattering matrix is a standard FIO.
  • Misapplying the standard propagation of singularities theorem to operators with double characteristics; the Hamilton flow is degenerate and one must use the subprincipal symbol to determine the microlocal behaviour.
  • Thinking that the scattering matrix for short‑range potentials is a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator associated to the classical scattering map, which is generally not the identity.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Non‑Involutive Characteristics

When the principal symbol of a pseudodifferential operator vanishes to second order on a submanifold Σ of the cotangent bundle, the Hamiltonian vector field is tangent to Σ and the characteristic set fails to be involutive. This situation, termed non‑involutive double characteristics, was systematically studied by Dencker, Sjöstrand and Zworski (2004). They proved that the wavefront set of a solution propagates along the generalized bicharacteristics defined by the Hamilton flow of the subprincipal symbol restricted to Σ. The subprincipal symbol enters the transport equations for the principal amplitude and determines whether singularities can cross Σ or are reflected.

A key tool is the FBI (Fourier–Bros–Iagolnitzer) transform, which provides a phase‑space resolution fine enough to capture the microlocal behaviour near Σ. Using the FBI transform one can construct a parametrix for the Cauchy problem as a Fourier integral operator associated to a canonical relation that folds along Σ. The loss of derivatives in the parametrix is precisely one half of the order of vanishing of the principal symbol, a result that sharpens the classical Hörmander theory for real principal type operators.

Propagation of singularities for non‑involutive operators exhibits new phenomena: singularities may split into several branches when the Hamiltonian flow leaves Σ, and the Maslov index acquires contributions from the folding of the canonical relation. These effects are quantified by the subprincipal symbol and by the curvature of Σ inside the characteristic variety. The theory has been applied to the analysis of the wave equation on Lorentzian manifolds with degenerate metrics and to the study of degenerate hyperbolic equations arising in fluid dynamics.

In the semiclassical setting, the non‑involutive condition leads to a refined Egorov theorem where the quantum evolution of observables is governed by a classical flow that lives on a singular Lagrangian. The work of Sjöstrand and Zworski on semiclassical resonances for non‑selfadjoint perturbations shows that the distribution of resonances near the real axis is controlled by the same subprincipal data that governs the Cauchy problem.

For exam purposes, remember that the loss of one derivative for non‑involutive double characteristics is a universal feature, independent of the dimension, and that the subprincipal symbol replaces the principal symbol in the transport equations on Σ. This distinguishes the non‑involutive case from the standard real principal type theory where the principal symbol alone dictates propagation.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Magnetic Schrödinger Operators with Long‑Range Potentials

Consider the magnetic Schrödinger operator H = (−i∇ − A(x))² + V(x) on ℝⁿ, where the magnetic potential A(x) and the electric potential V(x) satisfy long‑range decay conditions |∂^α A(x)| ≤ C_α ⟨x⟩−1−|α| and |∂^α V(x)| ≤ C_α ⟨x⟩−1−|α| for |α| ≤ 2. The presence of a magnetic field B = dA modifies the classical Hamiltonian to |ξ − A(x)|² + V(x). Isozaki and Kitada (1985) extended their short‑range construction to this setting, showing that the scattering matrix S(λ) at energy λ > 0 is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated to the canonical relation generated by the classical scattering map κ on the energy surface.

The phase function of S(λ) acquires a logarithmic correction Φ(ω,θ) = ω·θ + λ−1∫0^∞ (A(γ(s))·γ̇(s)) ds + o(1) as λ → ∞, where γ is the classical trajectory with asymptotic directions ω, θ ∈ 𝕊n−1. This correction reflects the Aharonov–Bohm effect and is absent in the purely electric case. The principal symbol of S(λ) is the magnetic scattering amplitude a(ω,θ;λ), which solves a transport equation along the Hamiltonian flow of the magnetic Hamiltonian. The amplitude can be expressed in terms of the solution of the eikonal equation for the magnetic Hamiltonian.

Nakamura (1995) and Umeda (1997) proved that the wavefront set of the kernel of S(λ) is exactly the graph of κ, a smooth Lagrangian submanifold of T*𝕊n−1 × T*𝕊n−1. Moreover, they established a high‑energy asymptotic expansion S(λ) = I + λ−1/2F₁(λ) + λ−1F₂(λ) + … where each F_j(λ) is an FIO associated to the iterated scattering relations. The trace Tr(S(λ) − I) admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane; its poles are the magnetic resonances, and the residues are linked to the residues of the magnetic resolvent (H − z)−1.

For potentials with compact support, the magnetic scattering matrix reduces to a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 plus a smoothing operator, and its symbol has a complete asymptotic expansion in powers of λ−1/2. This simplification underlies the inverse scattering results of Novikov and others, who recovered the magnetic field from the high‑energy asymptotics of S(λ). The microlocal structure also yields sharp Strichartz estimates for the magnetic Schrödinger evolution, as shown by Erdogan, Goldberg and Schlag (2009).

In examinations, a common twist is to ask for the order of the scattering matrix as an FIO and the nature of the phase correction. The correct answer: order 0, with a logarithmic phase term linear in the magnetic potential integrated along classical trajectories. Forgetting the logarithmic term or stating that the phase is purely quadratic is a frequent error.

Identifying the canonical relation of the magnetic scattering matrix
Let H = (−i∇ − A)² + V on ℝ³ with A(x) = (−x₂, x₁, 0)/(1+|x|²) and V ≡ 0. Determine the canonical relation C ⊂ T*𝕊² × T*𝕊² associated to the scattering matrix S(λ) at high energy λ → ∞.
The classical Hamiltonian is |ξ − A(x)|². The Hamiltonian flow preserves the magnetic momentum ξ − A(x). As |x| → ∞, A(x) = O(|x|−1), so the flow asymptotically coincides with the free flow but with a shifted momentum. The scattering map κ sends an incoming direction ω ∈ 𝕊² and impact parameter b ∈ ω^⊥ to an outgoing direction θ determined by the deflection angle produced by the magnetic field B = dA = (0,0,2)/(1+|x|²)². The canonical relation is C = {(θ, η; ω, ξ) ∈ T*𝕊² × T*𝕊² : (θ, η) = κ(ω, ξ)}. Because the magnetic field is rotationally symmetric about the x₃‑axis, κ preserves the angular momentum component L₃ = x₁ξ₂ − x₂ξ₁. Hence C is the graph of a symplectomorphism that twists the fibres by an amount equal to the total magnetic flux through the plane orthogonal to the trajectory. The phase function is Φ(ω,θ) = ω·θ + λ−1∫_ℝ A(γ(s))·γ̇(s) ds, where γ is the classical trajectory with asymptotics ω, θ.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the microlocal structure of a scattering matrix, first identify the classical Hamiltonian and its long‑range terms. Write down the eikonal equation for the phase, then read off the canonical relation as the graph of the classical scattering map. Remember that any long‑range magnetic potential contributes a logarithmic phase correction; electric long‑range potentials give a quadratic phase. The order of the FIO is always 0 for short‑range and long‑range magnetic Schrödinger operators. Practise deriving the phase correction for a simple A(x) = (−x₂, x₁, 0)/|x|² to internalise the Aharonov–Bohm term.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Lie Algebroids and the Tangent Groupoid

A Lie algebroid A → M generalises both the tangent bundle and the action Lie algebroid of a Lie group action. Its sections form a Lie algebra with an anchor map ρ: A → TM.
