The tangent groupoid 𝔾 = A × {0} ∪ M × M × (0,1] constructed by Connes (1994) provides a smooth deformation from the Lie algebroid to the pair groupoid. This deformation yields a continuous field of C*‑algebras whose fibre at t = 0 is the groupoid C*‑algebra C*(A) and at t > 0 is the algebra of compact operators on L²(M). The pseudodifferential calculus on A, developed by Nistor, Weinstein and Xu (1999), uses the tangent groupoid to define a symbol map σ: Ψ^m(A) → S^m(A*)/Sm−1(A*), where A* is the dual bundle.

The principal symbol of an A‑pseudodifferential operator is a function on A* homogeneous of degree m. The composition formula mirrors the standard calculus: σ(P∘Q) = σ(P)σ(Q) modulo lower order terms. Ellipticity is defined by invertibility of the principal symbol on A* 0. An elliptic A‑operator admits a parametrix in Ψ−m(A) and has finite‑dimensional kernel and cokernel on compact M. The index of an elliptic A‑operator is computed by the Connes–Skandalis longitudinal index theorem, which expresses the index as the pairing of the symbol class in K⁰(A*) with the fundamental class of the tangent groupoid in K‑homology.

When A = TM, the calculus reduces to the classical pseudodifferential calculus on M. For the action algebroid of a proper Lie group action, the A‑calculus coincides with the invariant pseudodifferential calculus on the quotient orbifold. The tangent groupoid also provides a geometric proof of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem for families: the family index is the K‑theory class of the symbol in the C*‑algebra of the tangent groupoid of the family.

Recent work by Debord and Skandalis (2015) extended the calculus to Lie algebroids with non‑Hausdorff base, enabling the analysis of pseudodifferential operators on foliations with singularities. The analytic wavefront set of a distribution on a Lie algebroid is defined using the FBI transform adapted to the anchor map, and the propagation of singularities theorem holds for operators of real principal type with respect to the Lie algebroid Hamiltonian flow.

For competitive examinations, the essential points are: the tangent groupoid gives a smooth deformation quantisation of the Lie algebroid; the symbol calculus is identical in form to the classical one but lives on the dual bundle A*; elliptic regularity and the index theorem follow from the exact sequence of the tangent groupoid C*‑algebras. Questions often ask to identify the principal symbol of an invariant differential operator on a homogeneous space as a function on the dual of the action algebroid.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for Non‑Selfadjoint Operators on Asymptotically Hyperbolic Manifolds

Let (M,g) be an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold of dimension n ≥ 2, i.e. near the conformal boundary ∂M the metric takes the form g = (dx² + h(x))/x² with a defining function x and a family of metrics h(x) on ∂M. Consider a non‑selfadjoint operator P = Δ_g + V(x) where V is a complex‑valued potential decaying as O(x^∞). The resolvent R(λ) = (P − λ²)−1 is initially defined for Im λ ≫ 0. Vasy (2013) proved that R(λ) admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane as a family of operators between weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,ℓ(M). The poles are the resonances of P.

The microlocal structure of the continued resolvent is described by the 0‑calculus of Mazzeo and Melrose. The Schwartz kernel of R(λ) is a Legendrian distribution on the 0‑double space M²0, associated to the union of the diagonal, the boundary diagonal, and the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field of the principal symbol p = |ξ|_g² − λ². Because P is non‑selfadjoint, the Hamilton flow is complexified; the flow‑out becomes a complex Legendrian submanifold. The principal symbol of R(λ) on the diagonal is (p)−1, while on the boundary Legendrian it satisfies a transport equation involving the subprincipal symbol of P.

Guillarmou (2005) showed that the resolvent kernel has a polyhomogeneous expansion at the boundary faces of M²0, with leading term determined by the scattering matrix S(λ) of the model hyperbolic space. The scattering matrix itself is a Fourier integral operator on ∂M associated to the geodesic flow on the boundary at infinity. For non‑selfadjoint perturbations, S(λ) is no longer unitary but remains an elliptic FIO of order 0. The resonances correspond to the poles of the meromorphic family S(λ).

The distribution of resonances in the complex plane is governed by a fractal Weyl law when the trapped set of the geodesic flow is a hyperbolic repeller. Dyatlov and Zworski (2015) proved that the number of resonances in a strip |Im λ| ≤ C grows like O(h−δ) where δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set. The proof uses a microlocal partition of unity adapted to the stable/unstable manifolds and a complex absorbing potential to localise the resolvent near the trapped set.

In examinations, a typical question asks for the nature of the singularities of the resolvent kernel on the 0‑double space. The correct description: a Legendrian distribution associated to the union of the diagonal, the boundary diagonal, and the flow‑out of the Hamilton field of the principal symbol, with polyhomogeneous expansions at all boundary faces. Forgetting the flow‑out or treating the boundary Legendrian as a simple conormal singularity is a common mistake.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Edge Singularities

Let M be a compact manifold with an edge singularity: near the edge Y ⊂ M the metric is a warped product g = dx² + x² g_Y + g_Z where x is the distance to Y, g_Y is a metric on the link Y, and g_Z is a metric on the base Z. The Laplacian Δ_g is an edge‑degenerate operator. Mazzeo (1991) developed the edge calculus Ψ^m_e(M) which captures the precise asymptotics of solutions near Y. The spectral projector Π_λ = 1[0,λ](√Δ_g) is not a pseudodifferential operator in the edge calculus; instead it is a Fourier integral operator associated to a Legendrian submanifold in the edge‑cotangent bundle ^eT*M.

The Legendrian is the union of the conormal bundle of the diagonal in M × M and the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field of the principal symbol |ξ|_g² from the boundary face corresponding to the edge. The order of Π_λ as an FIO is −(dim M)/2 + 1/4, reflecting the codimension of the edge. The principal symbol on the interior Legendrian is the standard spectral projector symbol (2π)−n times the volume form on the energy surface. On the boundary Legendrian, the symbol satisfies a transport equation that involves the indicial family of Δ_g, i.e. the family of operators on the link Y obtained by freezing the normal variable x and taking the Mellin transform.

The heat kernel e−tΔ_g has a similar microlocal structure: it is a Legendrian distribution on the edge‑heat space, with a polyhomogeneous expansion at the temporal boundary t = 0. The coefficients of the expansion are given by the heat kernel on the model cone over Y, which can be expressed in terms of Bessel functions. The trace of the heat kernel yields the edge‑Weyl law with a remainder term of order O(λn−1 log λ), where the logarithmic factor comes from the edge singularity.

Albin and Melrose (2010) used the edge calculus to prove an index theorem for Dirac operators on manifolds with edges. The spectral projector of the Dirac operator enters the definition of the η‑invariant, which appears as a boundary correction in the index formula. The microlocal analysis of the spectral projector is essential for defining the η‑invariant via the heat kernel regularisation.

For exam preparation, remember that the spectral projector on an edge manifold is an FIO, not a ΨDO, and its canonical relation is a Legendrian with a boundary component. The order shift of +1/4 relative to the interior order is a hallmark of edge geometry. Questions may ask to compute the indicial roots of the Laplacian on a model cone and to relate them to the asymptotics of the spectral projector kernel at the edge.

  • Confusing the subprincipal symbol with the first lower‑order term in the Kohn‑Nirenberg quantization; the subprincipal symbol includes a correction from the mixed derivatives of the principal symbol.
  • Assuming the spectral projector on an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold is a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator with Legendrian singularities at the boundary.
  • Treating the resolvent on an asymptotically conic manifold as having only diagonal singularities; the boundary Legendrian and the flow‑out contribute equally to the wavefront set.
  • Believing that caustics only occur for linear waves; nonlinear interactions can create new caustics through the superposition of Lagrangian branches.
  • Overlooking the logarithmic phase correction in the scattering matrix for long‑range potentials; for short‑range potentials the phase is purely quadratic and the scattering matrix is a standard FIO.
  • Misapplying the standard propagation of singularities theorem to operators with double characteristics; the Hamilton flow is degenerate and one must use the subprincipal symbol to determine the microlocal behaviour.
  • Thinking that the scattering matrix for short‑range potentials is a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator associated to the classical scattering map, which is generally not the identity.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Corners

Manifolds with corners arise naturally in geometric scattering, index theory, and the resolution of singularities. The standard b‑calculus of Melrose extends to the iterated edge calculus (also called the b‑calculus on manifolds with corners) by treating each boundary hypersurface as a fibration over the next deeper stratum. An operator in this calculus is a family of pseudodifferential operators on the interior, together with normal operators on each boundary face that are themselves pseudodifferential on the corresponding model cones. The symbol hierarchy consists of the interior principal symbol σ∂ on bT*M, the boundary symbols σH on the compressed cotangent bundles of the boundary hypersurfaces, and the corner symbols on the iterated normal bundles. Composition is governed by a push‑forward formula: the normal operator of a product is the product of the normal operators, provided the orders match and the Mellin transform in the defining functions converges. Ellipticity in this calculus requires invertibility of all symbols simultaneously; the parametrix construction proceeds by induction on the depth of the corner, solving a model problem on the deepest stratum and then lifting. The resulting parametrix is a polyhomogeneous Legendrian distribution on the b‑double space blown up at all corner diagonals. Index theorems for such operators (e.g. the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index for Dirac operators on manifolds with corners) involve eta invariants of the boundary families and a corner contribution expressed as an integral of a local density over the corner strata. In examinations, a typical question asks for the structure of the Schwartz kernel of a parametrix for an elliptic b‑operator on a manifold with corners. The correct answer: a sum of conormal distributions associated to the lifted diagonal and to the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field from each boundary face, with polyhomogeneous expansions at all boundary hypersurfaces and corners. Forgetting the corner blow‑ups or treating the corner symbols as mere restrictions of the boundary symbols is a frequent error.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Fibred Boundaries

A manifold with fibred boundary ∂M → B has a defining function x such that the metric near ∂M takes the form g = dx²/x² + gB + x² gF, where gB is a metric on the base and gF a family of metrics on the fibres. The Laplacian Δg is a 0‑operator in the sense of Mazzeo–Melrose. Its heat kernel e−tΔ is not a standard pseudodifferential operator on the 0‑double space; instead it is a Legendrian distribution associated to the union of the diagonal, the boundary diagonal, and the flow‑out of the Hamilton field of the principal symbol |ξ|g² from the boundary face. The construction uses the 0‑heat space, obtained by blowing up the temporal boundary t = 0 at the boundary diagonal and then performing a parabolic blow‑up at the corner. The kernel has a polyhomogeneous expansion at each boundary face: at the temporal face the coefficients are given by the heat kernel on the model cone over the fibre, expressed in terms of Bessel functions; at the spatial boundary faces the expansion involves the indicial family of Δg, i.e. the family of operators on the fibre obtained by freezing the normal variable and taking the Mellin transform. The trace of the heat kernel yields the 0‑Weyl law with a remainder O(λn−1 log λ), where the logarithmic factor comes from the fibred boundary geometry. For exam purposes, remember that the heat kernel on a fibred boundary manifold is a Legendrian distribution on the 0‑heat space, not a ΨDO, and its singularities propagate along the Hamilton flow of the principal symbol, reflecting off the boundary according to the indicial roots. A common mistake is to assume the heat kernel is smooth up to the boundary in the 0‑double space; the correct microlocal description includes Legendrian singularities at the boundary diagonal and the flow‑out.

Worked Example: Indicial Roots and Heat Kernel Asymptotics
Let M be a manifold with fibred boundary ∂M → S¹, fibre F = S¹, and metric g = dx²/x² + dθ² + x² dφ² near ∂M. Compute the indicial roots of Δg and describe the leading term of the heat kernel expansion at the boundary face x = 0.
The Laplacian in these coordinates is Δ = −x²∂x² − x∂x + ∂θ² + x⁻²∂φ². Freeze x and take the Mellin transform in x: replace x∂x by iσ. The indicial family is I(σ) = σ² + ∂θ² + ∂φ². Its spectrum on L²(S¹×S¹) consists of eigenvalues λk,l = σ² + k² + l² for k,l ∈ ℤ. The indicial roots are the values of σ for which I(σ) is not invertible, i.e. σ = ±i√(k²+l²). The leading term of the heat kernel at x = 0 comes from the zero mode k = l = 0, giving σ = 0. The model heat kernel on the cone over the torus is the Euclidean heat kernel on ℝ², which yields a term (4πt)⁻¹ exp(−(θ−θ')²+(φ−φ')²/4t). This term appears in the polyhomogeneous expansion at the boundary face with coefficient 1, reflecting the zero indicial root. Higher modes contribute terms with powers x|k,l| and are subleading.
Exam Strategy
When asked for the microlocal structure of a heat kernel on a singular space, first identify the appropriate blown‑up heat space (b‑heat, 0‑heat, edge‑heat). Then list the Legendrian components: diagonal, boundary diagonal, flow‑out. State the order of the Legendrian distribution (usually −n/2 for the diagonal, shifted by 1/4 at boundary faces). Finally, mention the polyhomogeneous expansion and the role of the indicial family. This checklist covers the points examiners award marks for.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Fibred Cusps

Fibred cusp metrics generalize both cusp and fibred boundary geometries: near the boundary ∂M → B the metric is g = dx²/x⁴ + gB/x² + gF. The Laplacian Δg belongs to the φ‑calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose. Its spectral projector Πλ = 1[0,λ](√Δg) is a Fourier integral operator associated to a Legendrian submanifold in the φ‑cotangent bundle φT*M. The Legendrian is the union of the conormal bundle of the diagonal in M × M and the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field of the principal symbol from the boundary face corresponding to the cusp. The order of Πλ as an FIO is −(dim M)/2 + 1/4, reflecting the codimension of the cusp. The principal symbol on the interior Legendrian is the standard spectral projector symbol (2π)−n times the volume form on the energy surface. On the boundary Legendrian, the symbol satisfies a transport equation involving the indicial family of Δg, i.e. the family of operators on the fibre obtained by freezing the normal variable and taking the Mellin transform in x. The heat kernel e−tΔ has a similar microlocal structure: it is a Legendrian distribution on the φ‑heat space, with a polyhomogeneous expansion at the temporal boundary t = 0 whose coefficients are given by the heat kernel on the model cusp, expressible in terms of Bessel functions of imaginary order. The trace of the heat kernel yields a Weyl law with remainder O(λn−1 log λ), where the logarithmic factor originates from the cusp geometry. In examinations, a typical question asks for the nature of the singularities of the spectral projector kernel on the φ‑double space. The correct description: a Legendrian distribution associated to the union of the diagonal, the boundary diagonal, and the flow‑out, with polyhomogeneous expansions at all boundary faces. Forgetting the flow‑out or treating the boundary Legendrian as a simple conormal singularity is a common mistake.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Asymptotically Complex Hyperbolic Manifolds

Asymptotically complex hyperbolic manifolds are complete Kähler manifolds whose metric approaches the Bergman metric on the unit ball in ℂⁿ at infinity. The Laplacian Δg is a 0‑operator with respect to a defining function ρ for the boundary at infinity. The resolvent R(σ) = (Δg − σ²)⁻¹ for Im σ > 0 extends meromorphically to ℂ as a family of operators between weighted 0‑Sobolev spaces. Its Schwartz kernel on the 0‑double space is a Legendrian distribution associated to the union of the diagonal, the boundary diagonal, and the flow‑out of the Hamilton field of the principal symbol |ξ|g² from the boundary face. The poles of the meromorphic continuation (the resonances) correspond to the eigenvalues of the indicial operator on the boundary, which in this setting is the Laplacian on the CR sphere S2n−1 with the sub‑Laplacian. The leading term of the resolvent kernel at the boundary face is given by the Poisson kernel of the model complex hyperbolic space, which can be expressed explicitly using the hypergeometric function 2F1. The scattering matrix S(σ) is a Fourier integral operator on the boundary associated to the geodesic flow on the CR sphere; its principal symbol is the classical scattering amplitude. For exam preparation, remember that the resolvent on an asymptotically complex hyperbolic manifold is not a pseudodifferential operator but a Legendrian distribution on the 0‑double space, and its meromorphic continuation is governed by the indicial roots of the boundary sub‑Laplacian. A frequent error is to treat the resolvent as having only diagonal singularities, ignoring the boundary Legendrian and the flow‑out.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Nonlinear Wave Equation with Quadratic Nonlinearities and Null Forms

Consider the nonlinear wave equation □u = Q(∂u, ∂u) on ℝ1+3, where Q is a quadratic form satisfying the null condition of Klainerman: Q(ξ, ξ) = 0 for all null vectors ξ. The null condition ensures that the worst nonlinear interactions occur only when waves travel in nearly the same direction, which allows global existence for small data. Microlocally, the solution u is a Lagrangian distribution associated to the union of the forward light cone and its iterated self‑interactions. The wavefront set WF(u) propagates along the Hamilton flow of the principal symbol τ² − |ξ|², but at points where two Lagrangian branches intersect, new singularities can be generated. The null form structure implies that the symbol of the nonlinearity vanishes on the diagonal of the characteristic set, which suppresses the formation of new singularities at transverse intersections. The geometric optics expansion u ∼ Σ ak eiλφk yields transport equations for the amplitudes ak along the bicharacteristics. The first correction a1 satisfies a linear equation with a source term given by the null form evaluated on the leading amplitude a0. Because of the null condition, this source term is smoother than a generic quadratic term, leading to improved decay estimates. In examinations, a typical question asks to explain why the null condition prevents blow‑up in the energy estimates. The answer: the null form Q(∂u, ∂u) can be written as a linear combination of ∂α(u ∂αu) − (∂αu)(∂αu) plus terms that vanish on the light cone; when integrated against the energy multiplier, the boundary terms cancel and the remaining terms are controllable by the Klainerman–Sobolev inequality. Forgetting the algebraic structure of the null form and treating it as a generic quadratic nonlinearity is a common mistake.

  • Confusing the iterated edge calculus with the standard b‑calculus; the former requires blow‑ups at all corner strata and separate corner symbols.
  • Assuming the heat kernel on a fibred boundary manifold is a pseudodifferential operator on the 0‑double space; it is a Legendrian distribution with flow‑out singularities.
  • Treating the spectral projector on a fibred cusp manifold as a ΨDO; it is an FIO with Legendrian singularities at the boundary diagonal and the Hamilton flow‑out.
  • Ignoring the indicial family when computing the boundary symbol of the resolvent on asymptotically complex hyperbolic spaces; the indicial roots determine the resonance set.
  • Overlooking the algebraic cancellation in null forms; the vanishing of Q on null vectors is essential for the improved decay in the geometric optics expansion.
  • Review these traps carefully before the exam.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Complex Characteristics

When the principal symbol of a pseudodifferential operator takes complex values, the characteristic set is no longer a real hypersurface in the cotangent bundle but a complex Lagrangian variety. The Cauchy problem for such operators is governed by the analytic wavefront set WFA rather than the C∞ wavefront set. The fundamental result of Sato–Kawai–Kashiwara and Hörmander states that if the operator is of real principal type after a complex change of variables, then the analytic singularities propagate along the complex bicharacteristic strips of the Hamilton field of the principal symbol. In the genuinely complex case, the propagation theorem fails in general; instead one has the Kashiwara–Schapira estimate: the analytic wavefront set of a solution is contained in the union of the analytic wavefront set of the data and the complex characteristic variety intersected with the forward flow of the imaginary part of the symbol. This leads to the notion of micro‑hyperbolicity: an operator is micro‑hyperbolic at a point if the imaginary part of its principal symbol does not change sign along the real bicharacteristics. For micro‑hyperbolic operators the Cauchy problem is well‑posed in Gevrey classes of order s > 1, and the loss of derivatives is precisely quantified by the order of vanishing of the imaginary part. A typical exam question asks to distinguish between the propagation of C∞ singularities (governed by the real principal type condition) and analytic singularities (governed by the sign condition on the imaginary part). The key trap is to apply the real principal type theorem to a complex‑valued symbol without checking the micro‑hyperbolicity condition; the correct answer must exhibit the sign condition and the resulting Gevrey regularity.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Relativistic Hamiltonians

For relativistic Hamiltonians such as the Dirac operator D = −iα·∇ + βm on ℝ3 with a short‑range potential V(x), the scattering matrix S(λ) at energy λ ∈ (−m, m) is a Fourier integral operator on the sphere S2 associated to the canonical relation given by the classical scattering map of the relativistic particle. The principal symbol of S(λ) is the classical scattering amplitude, which can be computed from the asymptotics of the Jost solutions of the Dirac equation. Microlocally, the wavefront set of the scattering matrix is contained in the graph of the scattering relation, and its singularities correspond precisely to the classical trajectories that connect incoming and outgoing directions. When the potential is long‑range (e.g. Coulomb), the scattering matrix is no longer a standard FIO but a paired Lagrangian distribution associated to the diagonal and the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field of the principal symbol from the boundary of the characteristic set. The modification of the phase by the Dollard phase factor restores the FIO structure after a suitable gauge transformation. In examinations, a frequent task is to derive the leading term of the scattering amplitude for the Dirac–Coulomb operator and to explain why the standard short‑range FIO calculus fails for the pure Coulomb potential. The correct response identifies the logarithmic phase correction and the resulting paired Lagrangian structure, and notes that the scattering matrix remains a Fourier integral operator after conjugation by the Dollard modifier.

Worked Example: Analytic Wavefront Set of a Complex‑Characteristic Operator
Consider the operator P = Dt + i Dx2 on ℝ2. Determine the analytic wavefront set of a solution u to Pu = 0 with initial data u(0,x) = δ(x). Explain why the C∞ wavefront set propagation theorem does not apply.
The principal symbol is p(t,x,τ,ξ) = τ + i ξ2. Its real part is τ, imaginary part is ξ2 ≥ 0. The operator is not of real principal type because the symbol is not real‑valued. The analytic wavefront set of the initial datum δ(x) is WFA(δ) = {(0,0;0,ξ) : ξ ≠ 0}. The Hamilton field of p is Hp = ∂τ + 2i ξ ∂ξ. The imaginary part ξ2 does not change sign along the real bicharacteristics (which are lines τ = const, ξ = const). Hence P is micro‑hyperbolic. By the Kashiwara–Schapira theorem, WFA(u) is contained in the forward flow of WFA(δ) under the real part of Hp, i.e. the set {(t,0;τ,ξ) : t>0, τ=0, ξ≠0}. The C∞ propagation theorem requires a real‑valued principal symbol of real principal type; here the symbol is complex, so the theorem is inapplicable.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem presents a pseudodifferential operator with a complex principal symbol, first check whether the imaginary part has a constant sign on the characteristic set. If it does, invoke micro‑hyperbolicity and Gevrey well‑posedness; if not, the Cauchy problem may be ill‑posed in any Gevrey class. Always distinguish between C∞ and analytic wavefront sets in your answer.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Edge Singularities and the Edge Calculus

Manifolds with edge singularities are spaces that locally look like a product of a cone over a closed manifold Y and a smooth manifold Z. The edge calculus developed by Mazzeo and Melrose provides a pseudodifferential calculus adapted to this geometry. The key idea is to blow up the edge Z in the double space, creating a front face that is a bundle of cones over Y. Operators in the edge calculus are those whose Schwartz kernels are conormal to the lifted diagonal and have polyhomogeneous expansions at the front face with exponents determined by the indicial family of the model operator on the infinite cone. The symbol hierarchy consists of an interior symbol (the usual principal symbol on the interior), an edge symbol (a family of operators on the model cone parametrized by the cotangent variables along the edge), and a normal operator (the translation‑invariant operator on the exact cone). Ellipticity in the edge calculus requires invertibility of all three symbols. The parametrix construction proceeds by first inverting the normal operator via Mellin transform, then solving a model problem on the front face, and finally correcting by a standard pseudodifferential parametrix on the interior. Index theorems for edge operators involve the eta invariant of the boundary family and a contribution from the edge symbol. A common examination question asks to describe the three‑level symbol structure and to explain why the normal operator must be invertible for a Fredholm property on weighted edge Sobolev spaces. The trap is to confuse the edge calculus with the b‑calculus; the edge calculus has an extra front face corresponding to the cone direction, and the normal operator lives on the infinite cone rather than on the cylinder.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Fibred Cusps

Fibred cusp metrics are complete metrics on the interior of a manifold with boundary M such that near the boundary the metric takes the form g = dx2/x4 + h(x)/x2 + k(x), where the fibres of a fibration ∂M → B are scaled by x. The heat kernel e−tΔ for the Laplacian on such a space is not a pseudodifferential operator on the 0‑double space but a Legendrian distribution on the fibred cusp double space obtained by a sequence of blow‑ups. Its singularities lie on the lifted diagonal, the boundary diagonal, and the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field of the principal symbol from the boundary face. The leading asymptotics as t → 0 are governed by the model heat kernel on the exact fibred cusp, which can be expressed in terms of the heat kernel on the fibre and the base. The small‑time expansion yields coefficients that are integrals of local curvature invariants over the fibres, leading to a fibred cusp version of the Minakshisundaram–Pleijel expansion. For the trace of the heat kernel, the asymptotic expansion contains terms of the form t−(dim B)/2 (log t)k reflecting the non‑product geometry of the cusp. In exams, a typical problem asks to identify the correct double space for the heat kernel and to explain why the standard 0‑calculus fails to capture the flow‑out singularities. The correct answer emphasizes the need for the fibred cusp blow‑up and the Legendrian nature of the kernel.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation with Derivative Nonlinearity

Consider the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation (DNLS) i∂tu + Δu = i∂x(|u|2u) on ℝ1+1. The nonlinearity contains a derivative, which destroys the standard gauge invariance and leads to a loss of derivatives in the energy estimates. Microlocally, the solution is a Lagrangian distribution associated to the characteristic variety τ = |ξ|2. The derivative nonlinearity generates a quadratic interaction of waves whose phases satisfy the resonance condition τ1 + τ2 = τ3, ξ1 + ξ2 = ξ3. The key observation is that the symbol of the nonlinearity vanishes on the diagonal of the characteristic set, a structure analogous to the null condition for wave equations. This vanishing allows one to apply a normal form transformation (the gauge transformation of Hayashi–Ozawa) that removes the derivative loss and yields a cubic nonlinearity with a non‑resonant phase. The transformed equation admits global well‑posedness for small data in Hs with s > 1/2. In the microlocal picture, the wavefront set of the solution propagates along the linear flow, and the normal form transformation corresponds to a conjugation by a Fourier integral operator that straightens the Lagrangian. A frequent exam question asks to perform the normal form reduction for DNLS and to explain why the derivative nonlinearity is critical in one dimension. The answer must exhibit the gauge transformation, compute the transformed nonlinearity, and note that the scaling critical Sobolev index is s = 1/2, making the problem energy‑critical.

  • Confusing the analytic wavefront set propagation for complex‑characteristic operators with the C∞ propagation theorem; the former requires micro‑hyperbolicity, the latter requires real principal type.
  • Treating the scattering matrix for a long‑range relativistic potential as a standard Fourier integral operator without the Dollard phase correction; the correct object is a paired Lagrangian distribution.
  • Mixing up the edge calculus and the b‑calculus; the edge calculus has a front face modelling the cone and a normal operator on the infinite cone, while the b‑calculus has a boundary face modelling the cylinder.
  • Assuming the heat kernel on a fibred cusp manifold is a pseudodifferential operator on the 0‑double space; it is a Legendrian distribution on the fibred cusp double space with flow‑out singularities.
  • Neglecting the gauge transformation in the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation; the derivative loss is removed only after the normal form reduction, and the critical Sobolev exponent must be identified.
  • Overlooking the three‑level symbol hierarchy (interior, edge, normal) when checking ellipticity in the edge calculus; all three must be invertible for Fredholmness.
  • Misidentifying the model operator for the heat kernel on a fibred cusp; the model is the exact fibred cusp heat kernel, not the product heat kernel on the cone.
The advanced sections above complete the required depth for microlocal analysis topics not previously covered.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Variable Multiplicity Characteristics

When the principal symbol of a pseudodifferential operator vanishes to different orders along intersecting submanifolds of the cotangent bundle, the characteristic set acquires a stratified structure. The Cauchy problem for such operators cannot be treated by the standard real‑principal‑type theory because the Hamilton vector field fails to be transversal to the characteristic variety on the higher‑multiplicity strata. The microlocal approach, developed by Hörmander, Ivrii and later by Dencker, replaces the single Hamiltonian flow by a family of bicharacteristic strips that may split or merge at the strata. One constructs a parametrix as a sum of Fourier integral operators associated to each stratum, matched by transmission conditions that encode the loss of derivatives. The key estimate is a subelliptic bound of the form ‖u‖Hs+1-δ ≤ C(‖Pu‖Hs + ‖u‖Hs) where δ depends on the maximal multiplicity. In examinations, a typical question asks to describe the geometry of the characteristic set for the operator P = D_t2 - t2 D_x2 on ℝ2 and to explain why the Cauchy problem is well‑posed in Gevrey classes but not in C^∞. The answer must identify the double characteristic at t = 0, compute the Levi form, and invoke the Ivrii–Petkov condition for Gevrey well‑posedness.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Non‑Selfadjoint Operators

For a non‑selfadjoint Hamiltonian H = H0 + V on ℝ^n with a long‑range potential V that is not symmetric, the scattering matrix S(λ) at energy λ is no longer unitary. Its microlocal structure is captured by a paired Lagrangian distribution on the product of the incoming and outgoing spherical cotangent bundles. The canonical relation is the graph of the classical scattering map, but the complex‑valued amplitude acquires an additional factor coming from the non‑selfadjoint part of V. The work of Zworski, Vasy and others shows that S(λ) can be written as I + A(λ) where A(λ) is a Fourier integral operator of order -1 associated to the scattering relation, plus a smoothing remainder that decays faster than any polynomial in λ. The poles of the meromorphic continuation of S(λ) correspond to resonances, and their distribution is governed by a fractal Weyl law when the trapped set is hyperbolic. In an exam setting, a common problem is to compute the principal symbol of A(λ) for the model operator H = -Δ + i x·∇ on ℝ^n and to verify that the imaginary part of the symbol yields the correct absorption rate. The solution requires writing the classical Hamiltonian flow, linearising it at the trapped set, and applying the stationary phase method to the oscillatory integral representation of the scattering amplitude.

Worked Example: Principal Symbol of the Non‑Selfadjoint Scattering Amplitude
Let H = -Δ + i x·∇ on ℝ3. Determine the principal symbol of the scattering amplitude A(λ) at high energy λ → +∞ and show that its imaginary part is negative definite on the trapped set.
The classical Hamiltonian is p(x,ξ) = |ξ|2 + i x·ξ. The real part gives the usual geodesic flow, while the imaginary part generates a radial contraction. The trapped set consists of the zero section ξ = 0. Linearising the flow at ξ = 0 yields the matrix (0 I; 0 I) whose eigenvalues have positive real part, indicating exponential decay. The scattering amplitude is an oscillatory integral ∫ eiΦ(x,ξ) a(x,ξ) dξ with phase Φ solving the eikonal equation. The principal symbol a0 satisfies the transport equation (H_p + i x·ξ) a0 = 0 along the flow. Solving this gives a0 = exp(-∫0^∞ x(s)·ξ(s) ds). Since x·ξ > 0 on the outgoing tail, the exponent is negative, so Im a0 < 0. This confirms the absorption property required by the optical theorem for non‑selfadjoint scattering.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the microlocal structure of a non‑selfadjoint scattering matrix, first identify the classical Hamiltonian and its trapped set. Then write the scattering amplitude as a Fourier integral operator, derive the transport equation for the principal symbol, and check the sign of the imaginary part. Remember that the paired Lagrangian calculus is needed only if the canonical relation has clean self‑intersection; otherwise a standard FIO calculus suffices.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Edge Singularities

An edge manifold M has a boundary ∂M that fibres over a base B with typical fibre Y, and the metric near the boundary takes the form g = dx2 + x2 g_Y(y,dy) + g_B(b,db) where x is a defining function for ∂M. The heat kernel e-tΔ for the Laplacian on such a space is not a pseudodifferential operator on the standard double space; it is a polyhomogeneous conormal distribution on the edge double space obtained by blowing up the corner ∂M × ∂M and then the lifted diagonal. The singularities lie on the lifted diagonal, the temporal boundary t = 0, and the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field from the edge face. The leading term as t → 0 is the model heat kernel on the exact edge ℝ_+ × Y × B, which factorises as the product of the Euclidean heat kernel on ℝ_+, the heat kernel on the fibre Y, and the heat kernel on the base B. The small‑time expansion yields coefficients that are integrals over the fibre of local curvature invariants, giving an edge analogue of the Minakshisundaram–Pleijel expansion. In examinations, a standard task is to describe the sequence of blow‑ups that produces the edge double space and to explain why the b‑calculus fails to capture the flow‑out singularities. The answer must emphasise that the b‑double space only resolves the boundary as a cylinder, whereas the edge geometry requires a front face modelling the cone over the fibre.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Conical Singularities

On a manifold with an isolated conical singularity, the Laplacian Δ admits a self‑adjoint extension with domain determined by a boundary condition at the cone tip. The spectral projector Π(λ) = 1(-∞,λ](Δ) is a Fourier integral operator with a singular canonical relation that wraps around the cone tip. Melrose and Wunsch showed that Π(λ) can be represented as a sum of a pseudodifferential part on the regular part of the manifold and a conormal distribution on the blown‑up space where the tip is replaced by its spherical link. The wavefront set of Π(λ) consists of the diagonal in the interior together with the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field from the link. The singularities propagate along broken geodesics that reflect off the cone tip according to the chosen self‑adjoint extension. A typical exam problem asks to compute the leading singularity of the Schwartz kernel of Π(λ) near the tip for the Friedrichs extension on a two‑dimensional cone of angle α. The solution uses separation of variables in polar coordinates, expresses the kernel in terms of Bessel functions Jν(r√λ) with ν = πk/α, and applies the Debye asymptotics to extract the conormal singularity of order -1 along the light cone r = r'.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Edge Singularities

The resolvent R(λ) = (Δ - λ)-1 on an edge manifold exhibits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane with poles at the resonances. Microlocally, R(λ) is a Legendrian distribution on the edge double space with respect to the Legendrian submanifold given by the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field from the edge face. The normal operator on the front face is the resolvent of the model operator on the exact edge ℝ_+ × Y × B, which can be inverted explicitly using the Mellin transform in the radial variable x. The indicial roots of the model operator determine the asymptotic expansion of the resolvent kernel as x → 0. When the edge is non‑product, the normal operator depends on the base variable b, and the full resolvent is constructed by a parametrix that glues the model resolvent to a pseudodifferential parametrix on the interior. In an exam, a frequent question is to derive the indicial equation for the model edge Laplacian Δ_edge = -(x∂_x)2 + Δ_Y + x2 Δ_B and to explain how the roots control the mapping properties of R(λ) between weighted edge Sobolev spaces. The answer must compute the Mellin symbol, find the roots γ satisfying γ2 = μ_k where μ_k are eigenvalues of Δ_Y, and state that the resolvent is Fredholm between x^δ H^sedge and xδ-2 Hs-2edge provided δ avoids the indicial roots.

  • Confusing the edge double space with the b‑double space; the edge blow‑up introduces a front face modelling the cone over the fibre, while the b‑blow‑up only creates a cylindrical face.
  • Assuming the heat kernel on an edge manifold is a pseudodifferential operator on the standard double space; it is a polyhomogeneous conormal distribution on the edge double space with flow‑out singularities.
  • Neglecting the paired Lagrangian structure of the non‑selfadjoint scattering matrix; the canonical relation self‑intersects cleanly, requiring the paired calculus to capture the correct amplitude.
  • Overlooking the dependence of the normal operator on the base variable in the edge calculus; this leads to an incorrect parametrix and wrong mapping properties.
  • Misidentifying the indicial roots for the model edge Laplacian; the roots are determined by the fibre spectrum, not by the base geometry.
  • Treating the spectral projector on a conical singularity as a standard pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator with a singular canonical relation wrapping the cone tip.
  • Forgetting that the Friedrichs extension on a cone imposes a specific boundary condition that selects the regular Bessel solution Jν and discards the singular Yν component.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Orbifolds and the Orbifold Calculus

Orbifolds are spaces locally modelled on quotients of Euclidean space by finite group actions. The microlocal analysis on orbifolds requires a calculus of pseudodifferential operators that respects the singular strata. Kawasaki introduced the orbifold pseudodifferential calculus by lifting operators to the frame bundle and using the groupoid of germs of local diffeomorphisms. The symbol of an orbifold operator lives on the inertia orbifold, which encodes the twisted sectors coming from non‑trivial isotropy groups. Composition of symbols involves a sum over conjugacy classes of the isotropy groups, leading to a non‑commutative symbolic calculus. The wavefront set of a distribution on an orbifold is defined as a subset of the cosphere bundle of the inertia orbifold, and the propagation of singularities theorem holds for real principal type operators with the Hamiltonian flow lifted to the inertia space. Index theory on orbifolds yields the Kawasaki index theorem, where the analytical index equals an integral over the inertia orbifold of the Chern character of the symbol twisted by the Todd class of the orbifold tangent bundle. In examinations, a typical problem asks to compute the subprincipal symbol of the orbifold Laplacian on a global quotient ℝⁿ/Γ and to verify that the heat kernel asymptotics reproduce the orbifold Euler characteristic.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Nonlinear Principal Symbol

When the principal symbol depends nonlinearly on the solution, the equation is a fully nonlinear pseudodifferential equation. The Cauchy problem for such equations was pioneered by Bony and Chemin using paradifferential calculus. The key idea is to paralinearize the nonlinear symbol: one writes the operator as a paradifferential operator with a symbol that is a smooth function of the solution and its derivatives, plus a smoothing remainder. The paralinearized equation is a linear pseudodifferential equation with a symbol in the exotic class S¹,¹ (symbols with limited regularity in the base variables). Energy estimates in Sobolev spaces with a loss of derivatives are obtained by constructing a symmetrizer that depends on the solution. The lifespan of the solution is controlled by the size of the initial data in a scale of Sobolev spaces Hˢ with s > n/2 + 1. A standard exam question asks to derive the paralinearization formula for a symbol p(x,ξ,u) = a(x,ξ) + b(x,ξ)u and to prove that the remainder belongs to OP S⁰,⁰. The answer must exhibit the Bony decomposition u = T_u + R_u, compute the commutator [T_a, T_u], and estimate the remainder using the Coifman–Meyer theorem.

Paralinearization of a Quadratic Nonlinearity
Let P(u) = Op(p(x,ξ,u)) with p(x,ξ,u) = ξ² + u ξ. Paralinarize P(u) around a given function u₀ ∈ Hˢ, s > n/2 + 1, and identify the principal symbol of the linearized operator.
Write u = u₀ + v. The symbol becomes p(x,ξ,u₀+v) = ξ² + (u₀+v)ξ = ξ² + u₀ξ + vξ. The paralinearization of the term vξ is Tv ξ + R where Tv is the paraproduct with symbol v(x) and R is a smoothing operator of order 0. The linearized operator acting on v is therefore Op(ξ² + u₀ξ) + Tv ξ. Its principal symbol is ξ² + u₀(x)ξ, which is real and of order 2. The subprincipal symbol involves the derivative of u₀ and the commutator [Op(ξ²), Tv]. The remainder R is in OP S⁰,⁰ by the Coifman–Meyer estimate because v ∈ Hˢ with s > n/2 ensures v is a multiplier on Sobolev spaces.
Exam Strategy for Nonlinear Microlocal Problems
When a question asks for paralinearization, first write the Bony decomposition of the unknown function. Then expand the symbol to first order in the remainder. Identify the paraproduct term as the principal part of the linearization and bound the remainder using the symbolic calculus for exotic classes. Always check the regularity threshold s > n/2 + 1 to guarantee that the paraproduct is a bounded operator on Hˢ⁻¹.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Nonlinear Wave Equations

For nonlinear wave equations of the form □u = Q(∂u,∂u) with quadratic null forms, the scattering matrix maps asymptotic data at past null infinity to data at future null infinity. Microlocally, the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator associated with the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow on the Minkowski cone. The null condition ensures that the nonlinear interaction does not create new singularities at the light cone; instead, the singularities propagate along null geodesics and the scattering matrix is a pseudodifferential operator of order zero on the sphere at infinity. The work of Klainerman and Christodoulou shows that the modified scattering operator, which incorporates a logarithmic phase correction, is a Fourier integral operator with a clean canonical relation. In an exam, one may be asked to compute the principal symbol of the scattering matrix for the model null form Q₀(∂u,∂v) = ∂ₜu ∂ₜv − ∇u·∇v. The answer uses the geometric optics ansatz u ∼ a eiλφ, v ∼ b eiλψ with φ,ψ solving the eikonal equation, and evaluates the oscillatory integral at the stationary phase points corresponding to intersecting null cones.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Stratified Spaces

Stratified spaces are unions of smooth manifolds of varying dimensions glued along lower‑dimensional strata. The Laplacian on a stratified space with iterated cone‑edge metrics admits a resolvent that is a polyhomogeneous distribution on the iterated blow‑up of the double space. The construction proceeds by induction on the depth of the stratification: at each step one blows up the diagonal of the current stratum and the product of the stratum with the normal cone. The normal operator on the front face is the resolvent of the model operator on the exact cone over the link, which is inverted using the Mellin transform. The indicial roots are determined by the spectrum of the Laplacian on the link. The resolvent is Fredholm between weighted Sobolev spaces x^δ H^sedge provided the weight δ avoids the indicial roots. A typical exam problem asks to describe the sequence of blow‑ups for a two‑stratum space M = Y ∪ C(L) where Y is a smooth manifold and C(L) is a cone over a link L. The solution must draw the iterated double space, identify the front faces, and write down the normal operator on the deepest front face as the resolvent of the model cone Laplacian.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Stratified Singularities

On a compact stratified pseudomanifold, the spectral projector Π(λ) = 1(-∞,λ](Δ) is a Fourier integral operator with a singular canonical relation that wraps around each stratum. The work of Albin, Leichtnam, Mazzeo, and Piazza shows that Π(λ) can be represented as a sum of a pseudodifferential part on the regular stratum and conormal distributions on the blown‑up space where each singular stratum is replaced by its spherical normal bundle. The wavefront set consists of the diagonal in the interior together with the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field from the conormal bundles of the strata. The singularities propagate along broken geodesics that reflect off the strata according to the self‑adjoint extension chosen at each singularity. For the Friedrichs extension on a two‑dimensional wedge of angle α, the kernel near the tip is expressed in terms of Bessel functions Jπk/α(r√λ) and the leading singularity is conormal of order −1 along the light cone r = r'. In examinations, a common task is to compute the indicial roots for the model wedge Laplacian Δ = −∂_r² − (1/r)∂_r − (1/r²)∂_θ² with Dirichlet conditions at θ = 0,α, and to deduce the mapping properties of Π(λ) between weighted Sobolev spaces.

  • Confusing the orbifold inertia space with the ordinary cotangent bundle; the inertia space includes twisted sectors that contribute to the index formula.
  • Neglecting the loss of derivatives in the paralinearization of fully nonlinear symbols; the exotic symbol class S¹,¹ requires a Nash–Moser iteration for well‑posedness.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix for nonlinear wave equations is a standard pseudodifferential operator without the logarithmic phase correction; the correction is essential for the clean intersection calculus.
  • Overlooking the iterative blow‑up structure in the stratified resolvent construction; each stratum requires its own front face and normal operator.
  • Misidentifying the indicial roots for the wedge Laplacian; the roots are πk/α, not the eigenvalues of the link Laplacian alone.
  • These traps highlight the subtle interplay between geometry and analysis in microlocal theory.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Double Characteristics

When the principal symbol of a pseudodifferential operator vanishes to second order on a submanifold of the cotangent bundle, the operator is said to have double characteristics. The Cauchy problem for such operators is substantially more delicate than for real principal type operators because the Hamilton vector field of the principal symbol vanishes on the characteristic set, so the standard propagation of singularities theorem does not apply. The foundational work of Hörmander, Ivrii, and Melrose shows that one must analyse the subprincipal symbol and the geometry of the characteristic manifold. If the subprincipal symbol satisfies a condition of non‑degeneracy (the so‑called condition (Ψ) of Ivrii), then the Cauchy problem is well‑posed in Gevrey classes of order s > 1, but not in C∞. The loss of derivatives is quantified by the order of the Gevrey class, which depends on the eigenvalues of the Hamilton map associated with the quadratic part of the symbol. In the case of effectively hyperbolic double characteristics, where the Hamilton map has purely imaginary eigenvalues, the loss is exactly one derivative and the solution can be constructed by a parametrix that is a Fourier integral operator with a complex phase. For non‑effectively hyperbolic double characteristics, the loss can be infinite, leading to non‑uniqueness or non‑existence. The microlocal structure of the solution is described by the wavefront set with respect to the appropriate Gevrey class, and the propagation of singularities follows the bicharacteristic flow of the quadratic approximation. A typical exam question asks to compute the Hamilton map for the operator P = D_t2 - t2 D_x2 on ℝ2 and to determine the Gevrey index for which the Cauchy problem is well‑posed. The answer uses the symbol p(t,x,τ,ξ) = τ2 - t2 ξ2, finds the characteristic set {τ = ± t ξ}, and evaluates the Hamilton map at a point on the characteristic set to obtain eigenvalues ± i |ξ|, giving Gevrey index 2.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically de Sitter Spaces

Asymptotically de Sitter spaces are Lorentzian manifolds whose metric approaches the de Sitter metric at conformal infinity. The wave equation □_g u = 0 on such spaces admits a well‑defined scattering matrix S(λ) that maps asymptotic data on past infinity to data on future infinity. Microlocally, S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator associated with the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow on the conformal boundary, which is a sphere bundle over the spatial sections. The work of Vasy, Zworski, and others shows that the scattering matrix is a meromorphic family of operators on the sphere, with poles corresponding to resonances. The principal symbol of S(λ) can be computed explicitly using the geometric optics construction: one solves the eikonal equation for the phase function and the transport equations for the amplitude. The result is that S(λ) is a pseudodifferential operator of order zero on the sphere, with principal symbol given by the exponential of the integral of the subprincipal symbol along the null geodesics. In the special case of exact de Sitter space, the scattering matrix reduces to a multiplication operator by a ratio of Gamma functions, reflecting the explicit solvability of the wave equation in terms of hypergeometric functions. For perturbations, the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator with a clean canonical relation, and its wavefront set is contained in the flow‑out of the diagonal. A common exam problem asks to derive the leading term of the scattering matrix for a metric g = -dt2 + cosh2(t) h where h is a metric on the sphere, and to show that the principal symbol is eiπ λ times the identity. The solution uses the change of variable s = tanh(t) to map the problem to a cylinder and then applies the standard formula for the scattering matrix on a product space.

Technique: Computing the Principal Symbol of a Scattering Matrix
Let (M,g) be an asymptotically de Sitter space with metric g = -dt2 + a(t)2 h where a(t) = cosh(t) and h is the round metric on S^n. Determine the principal symbol of the scattering matrix S(λ) for the wave equation □_g u = 0 at energy λ ∈ ℂ.
The wave equation separates as u(t,ω) = v(t) Y_k(ω) where Y_k are spherical harmonics with eigenvalue -k(k+n-1). The radial equation becomes v'' + n tanh(t) v' + (λ2 / cosh2(t) - k(k+n-1)) v = 0. Changing variable s = sinh(t) gives a hypergeometric equation. The Jost solutions behave as e± i λ t at t → ±∞. Matching the asymptotics yields the scattering matrix S(λ) acting on each spherical harmonic as multiplication by the ratio of Gamma functions Γ( (n/2) + i λ ) Γ( (n/2) - i λ + k ) / ( Γ( (n/2) - i λ ) Γ( (n/2) + i λ + k ) ). For large k, Stirling's formula gives the principal symbol σ(S(λ)) = eiπ λ (1 + O(k-1)). Hence the principal symbol is the scalar eiπ λ times the identity on the sphere.
Exam‑day strategy
When asked to compute a scattering matrix symbol, first separate variables using the symmetry of the background. Reduce to an ODE, identify the Jost solutions by their asymptotic behaviour, and match them using known special functions. The principal symbol is always the high‑frequency limit of the ratio of the Jost coefficients; use Stirling or stationary phase to extract it. Remember that the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator only when the canonical relation is clean; otherwise it is a pseudodifferential operator with a symbol that can be computed by the same method.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Foliated Manifolds and the Connes Calculus

On a foliated manifold (M,ℱ), the tangent bundle splits into the leafwise tangent bundle Tℱ and the normal bundle Nℱ. The Connes calculus, developed by Connes and Moscovici, provides a pseudodifferential calculus adapted to this splitting. Operators in this calculus act on sections of vector bundles over M and are defined by symbols that are functions on the leafwise cotangent bundle T*ℱ, with a parameter in the normal direction. The symbol classes S^mℱ consist of functions a(x,ξ,η) where (x,ξ) ∈ T*ℱ and η ∈ N*ℱ, satisfying estimates in ξ and η. The composition formula involves a twisted product that reflects the holonomy of the foliation. The principal symbol map is a homomorphism from the algebra of operators to the algebra of symbols modulo lower order, and the index theorem for elliptic operators in this calculus takes values in the cyclic cohomology of the holonomy groupoid. A key result is that the longitudinal index of an elliptic operator on a compact foliated manifold can be computed by the Connes–Chern character in cyclic cohomology. The wavefront set of a distribution on a foliated manifold is defined as a subset of the leafwise cosphere bundle S*ℱ, and the propagation of singularities for leafwise hyperbolic operators follows the leafwise Hamilton flow. In examinations, a typical question asks to define the Connes symbol of the leafwise Laplacian Δ_ℱ on a Riemannian foliation and to show that its principal symbol is the leafwise metric. The answer uses the fact that Δ_ℱ is a sum of squares of leafwise vector fields, and its symbol is the quadratic form on T*ℱ induced by the leafwise metric.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Non‑Product Type Singularities

Manifolds with non‑product type singularities are spaces where the metric near a singular stratum is not a product of a cone metric with a smooth metric on the link, but rather a warped product with a non‑constant warping function. Examples include the edge metrics of Mazzeo and the iterated edge metrics of Albin, Leichtnam, Mazzeo, and Piazza. The heat kernel on such spaces is constructed by a parametrix method on a resolution of the double space obtained by a sequence of blow‑ups. The leading term of the heat kernel is a polyhomogeneous conormal distribution on the resolved space, with asymptotic expansions at each boundary face determined by the indicial roots of the model operators on the normal cones. The indicial roots are the eigenvalues of the Laplacian on the link, shifted by the dimension of the fibre. The heat kernel is trace class for t > 0, and its trace has an asymptotic expansion as t → 0 whose coefficients are integrals of local invariants on the regular part plus contributions from the singular strata. The microlocal structure of the heat kernel is described by its wavefront set, which is the union of the diagonal in the interior and the flow‑out of the conormal bundles of the singular strata under the Hamilton vector field of the Laplacian. A standard exam problem asks to compute the first two terms of the heat trace asymptotics for a two‑dimensional cone with angle α. The solution uses the explicit heat kernel on a cone expressed in terms of Bessel functions, integrates over the cone, and extracts the coefficients: the leading term is (Area)/(4π t) and the next term is (π/α - 1)/12, reflecting the contribution of the conical singularity.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Cusp Singularities

Cusp singularities arise when a manifold has ends that are diffeomorphic to (0,ε)_x × Y with metric dx2/x4 + h(x)/x2, where Y is a compact manifold and h(x) is a family of metrics on Y. The Laplacian on such a space is essentially self‑adjoint on the Friedrichs domain, and its spectral projector Π(λ) = 1(-∞,λ](Δ) is a Fourier integral operator with a singular canonical relation that wraps around the cusp. The work of Melrose, Mazzeo, and Vasy shows that Π(λ) can be represented as a sum of a pseudodifferential part on the interior and a conormal distribution on the cusp‑resolved space obtained by blowing up the cusp at infinity. The wavefront set of Π(λ) consists of the diagonal in the interior together with the flow‑out of the Hamilton vector field from the conormal bundle of the cusp. The singularities propagate along geodesics that go into the cusp and return, creating a series of reflected singularities. The mapping properties of Π(λ) between weighted Sobolev spaces x^δ H^s are determined by the indicial roots of the model cusp Laplacian, which are the eigenvalues of the Laplacian on Y shifted by the dimension of Y. In an exam, one may be asked to describe the blow‑up construction for a cusp manifold and to write down the normal operator on the front face. The answer identifies the front face as the inward pointing spherical normal bundle of the cusp, and the normal operator as the spectral projector of the model cusp Laplacian on the exact cusp ℝ+_x × Y with metric dx2/x4 + h0/x2.

  • Confusing the Gevrey index for double characteristics with the loss of derivatives for real principal type operators; the former depends on the Hamilton map eigenvalues, the latter is zero.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on asymptotically de Sitter spaces is always a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator when the canonical relation is not the diagonal.
  • Neglecting the holonomy twisting in the Connes calculus composition formula; the twisted product is essential for the index theorem.
  • Overlooking the contribution of non‑product warping functions to the heat kernel asymptotics; the indicial roots depend on the full warped geometry, not just the link.
  • Misidentifying the front face in the cusp blow‑up; it is the spherical normal bundle of the cusp, not the cusp itself.
  • Treating the spectral projector on a cusp manifold as a standard pseudodifferential operator; its canonical relation is singular and requires the cusp‑resolved space.
  • Forgetting that the principal symbol of the leafwise Laplacian in the Connes calculus is the leafwise metric, not the full metric on M.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for Asymptotically Hyperbolic Spaces

Asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds are complete Riemannian manifolds whose metric near infinity can be written in the form g = (dx² + h(x))/x² where x is a boundary defining function and h(x) is a family of metrics on the conformal boundary converging to a metric h₀. The Laplacian Δ_g on such spaces has continuous spectrum [ (n−1)²/4 , ∞ ) and a discrete set of resonances in the complex plane. The scattering matrix S(σ) at spectral parameter σ ∈ ℂ is defined via the asymptotic expansion of generalized eigenfunctions: u ∼ xn/2−σ f₋ + xn/2+σ f₊, with S(σ) mapping f₋ to f₊. Vasy (2013) proved that S(σ) is a meromorphic family of Fourier integral operators associated with the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow on the boundary at infinity. The principal symbol of S(σ) is expressed in terms of the scattering metric and the volume form on the boundary. When the boundary is strictly convex, the canonical relation is a canonical graph and S(σ) is a pseudodifferential operator of order 2σ. The poles of S(σ) correspond precisely to resonances, and the residue at a resonance is a finite‑rank operator whose range consists of resonant states. In the setting of convex co‑compact hyperbolic quotients, the work of Guillarmou and Mazzeo shows that the scattering matrix extends holomorphically to ℂ { (n−1)/2 − ℕ } and satisfies a functional equation S(σ)S(n−1−σ) = Id. An examiner may ask to derive the leading term of the symbol of S(σ) for the hyperbolic space ℍⁿ⁺¹; the answer uses the explicit Poisson kernel and yields a constant multiple of |ξ|2σ.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Fibred Boundaries

A manifold with fibred boundary M has a boundary ∂M that is the total space of a fibration φ : ∂M → B with fibre Z. Near the boundary the metric takes the 0‑form g = dx²/x² + φ* g_B / x² + g_Z where x is a boundary defining function, g_B a metric on the base B, and g_Z a family of metrics on the fibres. The 0‑calculus of Mazzeo and Melrose provides a pseudodifferential calculus adapted to this geometry. The resolvent R(λ) = (Δ_g − λ)-1 for λ ∉ spec(Δ_g) is constructed as a 0‑pseudodifferential operator of order −2 on the 0‑double space M²0 obtained by blowing up the corner ∂M × ∂M. The normal operator on the front face is the family of model resolvents on the exact fibred boundary ℝ⁺_x × ∂M with metric dx²/x² + φ* g_B / x² + g_Z. Its indicial roots are determined by the spectrum of the vertical Laplacian on the fibres Z. The mapping properties R(λ) : x^δ H^s0 → x^δ Hs+20 hold for weights δ avoiding the indicial roots. When the fibration is a product, the 0‑calculus reduces to the b‑calculus; when the fibres are points it reduces to the scattering calculus. A typical exam problem asks to compute the indicial operator for the Laplacian on a manifold with fibred boundary where the fibre is a circle of length 2π; the answer identifies the indicial roots as ±√(λ + k²) for k ∈ ℤ.

Resolvent Construction on Fibred Boundaries
Describe the normal operator on the front face of the 0‑double space for the Laplacian on a manifold with fibred boundary.
The front face of M²0 is the inward pointing spherical normal bundle of the corner ∂M × ∂M. The normal operator N(R(λ)) is the resolvent of the model Laplacian on the exact fibred boundary ℝ⁺_x × ∂M with metric dx²/x² + φ* g_B / x² + g_Z. Concretely, after Fourier transform in the base variables and separation of variables in the fibre, N(R(λ)) acts as a family of ordinary differential operators in x whose coefficients are the vertical Laplacian on Z. Its indicial roots are the solutions ρ of ρ(ρ−1) + λ + μ_k = 0 where μ_k are the eigenvalues of the vertical Laplacian. The normal operator is invertible for λ away from the spectrum of the model operator, and its inverse gives the leading term of the resolvent parametrix.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the normal operator on a blown‑up space, first identify the corner being blown up, then write down the model metric on the front face. The normal operator is always the operator obtained by freezing coefficients at the boundary and replacing the defining function by a translation‑invariant variable. Memorise the indicial root equation for the three standard calculi: b, 0, and scattering.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Stratified Singularities and the Iterated Edge Calculus

Stratified pseudomanifolds are spaces with a filtration by closed subsets where each stratum is a smooth manifold and the link of each stratum is itself a stratified space. The iterated edge calculus of Albin, Leichtnam, Mazzeo, and Piazza (2013) builds a pseudodifferential calculus by resolving the double space through a sequence of blow‑ups indexed by the depth of the stratification. On a depth‑k stratified space, the iterated edge double space M²ie has boundary faces corresponding to each stratum and to each iterated edge. Operators in the calculus are conormal to the lifted diagonal and have polyhomogeneous expansions at each boundary face with index sets determined by the indicial families of the model operators on the iterated cones. The composition formula involves a push‑forward under a b‑fibration and a twisted product reflecting the holonomy of the fibrations linking strata. The principal symbol lives on the iterated edge cotangent bundle, which is a bundle over the interior together with a family of operator‑valued symbols on each boundary face. The index theorem for iterated edge operators expresses the Fredholm index as the integral of a characteristic form over the interior plus contributions from each stratum given by the eta invariants of the link operators. A standard exam question asks to describe the blow‑up order for a depth‑2 stratified space with strata Y ⊂ X; the answer is: first blow up Y × Y, then blow up the proper transform of X × X, and finally blow up the intersection of the two front faces.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Cubic Nonlinearities and the Null Condition

Consider a nonlinear wave equation □ u = Q(∂u, ∂u, ∂u) where Q is a cubic form satisfying the null condition: Q(ξ, ξ, ξ) = 0 whenever ξ is a null vector for the Minkowski metric. The null condition was introduced by Klainerman (1986) and Christodoulou (1986) to guarantee global existence for small data in 3+1 dimensions. Microlocally, the null condition implies that the worst resonant interactions occur only when the three input wavefront directions are pairwise distinct and not all collinear. Using the semiclassical wavefront set WF_h(u) and the propagation of singularities theorem for real principal type operators, one shows that the cubic term does not create new singularities at the same order as the linear part. The proof proceeds by a paradifferential reduction: write the equation as □ u = Tq (u, u, u) + lower order, where T_q is a paradifferential operator with symbol q(x, ξ) vanishing to second order on the null cone. Energy estimates in the semiclassical Sobolev spaces H^s_h yield a bootstrap that closes for s > 2. The key microlocal estimate is that the trilinear form satisfies |⟨T_q(u,u,u), v⟩| ≤ C h1/2 ‖u‖H1_h3 ‖v‖H1_h. An examiner may ask to verify the null condition for the cubic nonlinearity Q(∂u,∂u,∂u) = (∂_t u)3 − |∇u|2 ∂_t u; substituting ξ = (τ, ξ) with τ² = |ξ|² gives Q(ξ,ξ,ξ) = τ³ − |ξ|² τ = τ(τ² − |ξ|²) = 0.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Anti‑de Sitter Spaces

Asymptotically anti‑de Sitter (AdS) spacetimes are Lorentzian manifolds with conformal boundary ℝ × Y where Y is a compact Riemannian manifold. The metric near the boundary takes the form g = (dx² + h(x))/x² with x a boundary defining function and h(x) a family of Lorentzian metrics on ℝ × Y converging to a static metric. The wave operator □_g has a well‑posed Cauchy problem once boundary conditions are imposed at the conformal boundary; the Dirichlet condition corresponds to fixing the leading term of the field. The scattering matrix S(ω) maps Dirichlet data on the boundary to the Neumann data of the unique solution with prescribed frequency ω. Vasy (2010) and Holzegel‑Warnick (2014) proved that S(ω) is a meromorphic family of pseudodifferential operators on the boundary of order 1, with poles at the quasinormal modes. The principal symbol of S(ω) is given by the square root of the boundary Laplacian plus a potential term coming from the mass of the field. When the boundary metric is a product ℝ × Y, the canonical relation of S(ω) is the graph of the Hamiltonian flow of the boundary Hamiltonian. The residues at the poles are finite‑rank operators whose ranges are the resonant states, which are smooth up to the boundary. In the context of the AdS/CFT correspondence, the scattering matrix is identified with the two‑point function of the dual conformal field theory. A typical exam problem asks to compute the leading symbol of S(ω) for a massive scalar field on pure AdSd+1; the answer uses the explicit hypergeometric solution and yields σprin(S(ω)) = √(ω² + m² + Δ(Δ−d)) where Δ is the conformal dimension.

  • Confusing the indicial roots for the 0‑calculus with those for the b‑calculus; the 0‑roots involve the vertical Laplacian on the fibre, while b‑roots involve the normal operator on the boundary.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on asymptotically hyperbolic spaces is always a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator when the boundary is not strictly convex.
  • Neglecting the twisted product in the iterated edge composition formula; the holonomy of the link fibrations contributes a non‑trivial phase factor.
  • Overlooking that the null condition for cubic nonlinearities requires vanishing on the null cone to second order, not merely first order.
  • Treating the AdS scattering matrix as a Fourier integral operator for all boundary metrics; it is a pseudodifferential operator when the boundary is a product.
  • Misidentifying the front face in the iterated edge blow‑up; the order of blow‑ups follows the depth of the stratification from deepest to shallowest.
  • Forgetting that the resolvent on a fibred boundary manifold maps between weighted 0‑Sobolev spaces only for weights avoiding the indicial spectrum.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Polyhedral Singularities and the Polyhedral Calculus

A manifold with polyhedral singularities is a space locally modeled on a product of a smooth manifold with a polyhedral cone. The polyhedral calculus, developed by Mazzeo, Vertman, and others, extends the b-calculus and the edge calculus to handle the iterated singularities that arise at the corners of the polyhedron. The key geometric object is the polyhedral blow-up of the configuration space, which resolves the diagonal and all boundary faces in an order compatible with the face lattice of the polyhedron. The resulting space carries a family of vector fields tangent to all boundary faces, generating a Lie algebra that defines the polyhedral pseudodifferential operators. The symbol of such an operator is a collection of principal symbols on the cotangent bundles of the interior and each boundary face, together with normal operators that capture the behavior at the singular strata. These normal operators are themselves pseudodifferential operators on the link manifolds, which are again spaces with polyhedral singularities of lower depth, leading to an inductive structure. The composition formula involves a twisted product that accounts for the holonomy of the fibrations linking adjacent strata. Fredholm criteria are expressed in terms of the invertibility of the full symbol hierarchy: the interior principal symbol, the boundary principal symbols, and the normal operators at all depths. The index theorem for polyhedral operators, proved by Albin, Leichtnam, Mazzeo, and Piazza, expresses the index as a sum of a bulk term (an integral of a characteristic form over the interior) and boundary contributions localized at the singular strata, each given by an eta invariant of the corresponding normal operator. This calculus is essential for the analysis of the Hodge Laplacian on polyhedral domains, where the boundary conditions are encoded in the choice of domain for the normal operators.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Asymptotically Euclidean Manifolds with Conic Singularities

An asymptotically Euclidean manifold with conic singularities is a non-compact manifold whose ends are modeled on cones over compact link manifolds, with the metric approaching a conic metric at infinity. The resolvent R(λ) = (Δ − λ)−1 for λ in the complex plane away from the spectrum is studied using the scattering calculus with conic singularities, which combines the Melrose scattering calculus on the smooth part with the conic calculus near the singularities. The compactification of the manifold is achieved by a radial compactification of each end, producing a manifold with boundary where the boundary is a disjoint union of the link manifolds. The resolvent kernel lifts to a polyhomogeneous conormal distribution on the scattering double space blown up at the conic singularities. The leading asymptotics of the resolvent as λ approaches the real axis from the upper half-plane are governed by the scattering matrix S(λ), which is a pseudodifferential operator on the boundary of order zero. Its principal symbol is given by the absolute value of the Hamiltonian flow of the boundary Hamiltonian, which in the conic case is the geodesic flow on the link. When the link has no closed geodesics, the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator associated to a canonical relation that is a graph. The poles of the meromorphic continuation of R(λ) across the real axis correspond to resonances, and their distribution is controlled by the dynamics of the geodesic flow on the link. For a cone over a sphere with the standard metric, the resonances are explicitly known and lie on a lattice in the complex plane. The presence of conic singularities introduces additional resonances coming from the indicial roots of the Laplacian on the cone, which are determined by the eigenvalues of the Laplacian on the link. The resolvent satisfies weighted estimates of the form ‖⟨x⟩−s R(λ ± i0) ⟨x⟩−s‖ ≤ C|λ|−1 for s > 1/2, where x is a boundary defining function, provided λ avoids a neighborhood of the threshold energies corresponding to the indicial roots.

Worked Problem: Indicial Roots on a Conic End
Consider the Laplacian on the exact cone C(Y) = (0, ∞)r × Y with metric dr2 + r2h, where (Y, h) is a compact Riemannian manifold of dimension n−1. Let ΔY be the Laplacian on Y with eigenvalues μk ≥ 0. Find the indicial roots of the operator r2Δ on the cone and explain their role in the mapping properties of the resolvent between weighted Sobolev spaces.
The Laplacian on the cone is Δ = ∂r2 + (n−1)/r ∂r + r−2ΔY. Multiplying by r2 gives r2Δ = r2∂r2 + (n−1)r∂r + ΔY. Substituting the ansatz u = r−iσφ(y) yields the indicial equation: (−iσ)(−iσ−1) + (n−1)(−iσ) + μk = 0, which simplifies to σ2 − i(n−2)σ − μk = 0. The roots are σk,± = i(n−2)/2 ± √(μk − (n−2)2/4). For μk > (n−2)2/4 the roots are complex conjugates with imaginary part (n−2)/2; for μk = (n−2)2/4 there is a double root; for μk < (n−2)2/4 the roots are purely imaginary and distinct. The indicial roots determine the asymptotic behavior of solutions near r = 0 and r = ∞. The resolvent (Δ − λ)−1 maps the weighted Sobolev space Hs,α (with weight rα) to Hs+2,α provided α avoids the imaginary parts of the indicial roots. If α crosses an indicial root, the Fredholm index changes by the multiplicity of the corresponding eigenmode. This is the conic analogue of the b-calculus weight condition.
Exam Strategy: Indicial Roots and Weighted Spaces
When asked about mapping properties of the resolvent on a conic or asymptotically conic manifold, always compute the indicial roots first. The weight parameter α in the weighted Sobolev space Hs,α must not equal the imaginary part of any indicial root. If the problem gives a specific link Y (e.g., a sphere Sn−1), compute the eigenvalues of ΔY explicitly: μk = k(k+n−2) for k = 0, 1, 2, …. Then the indicial roots are σk,± = i(n−2)/2 ± √(k(k+n−2) − (n−2)2/4). The threshold weights are the imaginary parts of these roots.
