A common trap is to forget the k = 0 mode (μ0 = 0), which gives indicial roots 0 and i(n−2). The weight α = 0 is always excluded for the Laplacian on a cone because of the constant mode.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Flat Spacetimes

An asymptotically flat spacetime is a Lorentzian manifold (M, g) that, outside a compact set, is diffeomorphic to ℝ × (ℝn BR) with a metric approaching the Minkowski metric at a suitable polynomial rate. The wave operator □g admits a well-posed Cauchy problem, and the scattering matrix S(ω) at frequency ω maps incoming radiation fields to outgoing radiation fields on the null infinity ℐ±. Following the work of Friedlander, Melrose, Sá Barreto, and Vasy, the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator of order zero associated to the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow on the light cone at infinity. The principal symbol of S(ω) is a half-density on this canonical relation, determined by the transport equation along null geodesics. When the spacetime is stationary, S(ω) is a pseudodifferential operator on the sphere at infinity, and its principal symbol is the square root of the ratio of the outgoing and incoming energy fluxes. The poles of the meromorphic continuation of S(ω) in the complex ω-plane are the quasinormal modes, and their residues are finite-rank operators whose ranges are the resonant states. For the Schwarzschild and Kerr black hole spacetimes, the scattering matrix has been analyzed in detail by Dafermos, Rodnianski, and Shlapentokh-Rothman, who showed that the quasinormal modes lie in a strip Im ω > −C and satisfy a Weyl law. The Peierls bracket of the scattering matrix with the Hamiltonian generates the time evolution of the radiation field. In the context of the AdS/CFT correspondence, the flat-space scattering matrix is related to the boundary S-matrix of the dual conformal field theory. A key microlocal result is that the wavefront set of the scattering matrix is contained in the canonical relation, and the singularities propagate along the null geodesics of the conformal boundary. The red-shift effect near the event horizon of a black hole implies that the scattering matrix is a smoothing operator on the horizon, which is crucial for proving the stability of the black hole exterior.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Quasi-Homogeneous Symbols and the Newton Polygon

A pseudodifferential operator P(x, D) has a quasi-homogeneous symbol if there exists a weight vector w = (w1, …, wn) with positive entries such that the principal symbol pm(x, ξ) satisfies pm(x, tw1ξ1, …, twnξn) = tm pm(x, ξ) for t > 0. The Newton polygon of the operator is the convex hull in ℝn of the exponent vectors of the monomials appearing in the symbol, shifted by the weight vector. The geometry of the Newton polygon determines the propagation of singularities and the subelliptic estimates for the operator. If the Newton polygon meets the coordinate axes only at the origin, the operator is elliptic in the quasi-homogeneous sense and satisfies a subelliptic estimate with loss of derivatives determined by the distance from the origin to the polygon. The Cauchy problem for such operators was studied by Hörmander, Yamazaki, and others using the FBI transform adapted to the quasi-homogeneous structure. The analytic wavefront set WFA(u) propagates along the quasi-homogeneous bicharacteristics, which are the integral curves of the Hamilton vector field of the principal symbol with respect to the quasi-homogeneous symplectic form. When the principal symbol has multiple characteristics, the Newton polygon reveals the type of degeneracy: if the polygon has a face of dimension k, the operator behaves like a product of k non-degenerate operators in the corresponding directions. The Levi condition for well-posedness of the Cauchy problem takes the form that the subprincipal symbol must satisfy a sign condition on the characteristic set, modified by the quasi-homogeneous weights. For operators with real principal type in the quasi-homogeneous sense, the propagation of singularities theorem holds with the standard proof adapted to the weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,w. The parametrix construction uses a quasi-homogeneous symbolic calculus where the symbol classes Sm,w consist of functions satisfying |∂xα∂ξβ a(x, ξ)| ≤ Cαβ ⟨ξ⟩wm−|β|w with the quasi-homogeneous norm ⟨ξ⟩w = (∑ |ξj|2/wj)1/2.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Boundary and the Transmission Property

On a manifold with boundary, the transmission property characterizes those pseudodifferential operators that preserve the space of smooth functions up to the boundary. An operator A in the Boutet de Monvel calculus has the transmission property if its symbol a(x, ξ) satisfies a(x′, 0, ξ′, −ξn) = (−1)m a(x′, 0, ξ′, ξn) modulo symbols of lower order, where m is the order of A. This condition ensures that the operator maps the Schwartz space on the half-space to itself, and by localization, it preserves C∞(M) for a manifold with boundary M. The transmission property is equivalent to the condition that the operator commutes with the reflection operator Ru(x′, xn) = u(x′, −xn) modulo smoothing operators. Operators with the transmission property form a subalgebra of the Boutet de Monvel calculus, and their boundary symbol (the Poisson and trace operators) vanish. The Dirichlet-to-Neumann map for an elliptic operator with the transmission property is a pseudodifferential operator on the boundary of order 1, and its principal symbol is the square root of the principal symbol of the interior operator restricted to the boundary. The Calderón projector for such operators is a pseudodifferential projection on the boundary, and its symbol is given by the Cauchy data space of the interior operator. The index theorem for boundary value problems with the transmission property, due to Grubb and Seeley, expresses the index as the sum of the interior index (an integral of the Atiyah-Singer density) and a boundary term given by the eta invariant of the boundary operator. When the transmission property fails, the operator generates singularities at the boundary that are not present in the interior data, and the boundary symbol becomes non-trivial. The Müller calculus extends the transmission property to manifolds with corners by requiring the condition to hold at each boundary face. This is essential for the analysis of the Hodge Laplacian on manifolds with corners with absolute or relative boundary conditions, where the transmission property holds for the Laplacian but fails for the individual boundary operators.

  • Confusing the indicial roots for the conic calculus with those for the b-calculus; conic roots involve the eigenvalues of the link Laplacian, while b-roots involve the normal operator on the boundary.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on asymptotically flat spacetimes is always a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator when the null geodesic flow at infinity is not periodic.
  • Neglecting the weight vector in the quasi-homogeneous Newton polygon; the distance from the origin to the polygon must be measured in the weighted metric, not the Euclidean metric.
  • Overlooking that the transmission property is a condition on the full symbol, not just the principal symbol; lower-order terms can violate it even if the principal symbol satisfies it.
  • Treating the polyhedral calculus as a simple iteration of the edge calculus; the order of blow-ups follows the face lattice, and the twisted product involves holonomy of the link fibrations at all depths.
  • Misidentifying the canonical relation of the scattering matrix on asymptotically flat spacetimes; it is the graph of the geodesic flow on the light cone at null infinity, not the flow on the spatial infinity.
  • Forgetting that the resolvent on an asymptotically conic manifold maps between weighted Sobolev spaces only for weights avoiding the imaginary parts of the indicial roots, which are determined by the spectrum of the link Laplacian.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Boundary and the Transmission Property

On a compact manifold with boundary M, the Boutet de Monvel calculus provides a framework for pseudodifferential operators that act on sections of vector bundles up to the boundary. An operator A in this calculus possesses the transmission property if its full symbol a(x,ξ) satisfies the parity condition a(x′,0,ξ′,−ξn) = (−1)ma(x′,0,ξ′,ξn) modulo symbols of order m−1, where m is the order of A. This condition is equivalent to the requirement that A commutes with the reflection operator Ru(x′,xn) = u(x′,−xn) modulo smoothing operators. Operators with the transmission property form a subalgebra; their Poisson and trace operators vanish, so they map C∞(M) to itself and preserve the Schwartz space on the half‑space model.

The Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map for an elliptic transmission operator is a pseudodifferential operator on the boundary of order 1. Its principal symbol is the positive square root of the interior principal symbol restricted to the boundary covariables. The Calderón projector, which projects Cauchy data onto the space of boundary values of solutions, is a pseudodifferential projection of order 0 whose symbol is explicitly constructed from the interior symbol via the transmission condition. Grubb and Seeley proved an index theorem for such boundary value problems: the Fredholm index equals the interior Atiyah–Singer index plus a boundary contribution given by the eta invariant of the induced boundary operator. When the transmission property fails, the operator generates singularities at the boundary that are not present in the interior data; the boundary symbol becomes non‑trivial and the calculus must be enlarged to include non‑transmission Poisson and trace operators. Müller extended the transmission condition to manifolds with corners by requiring it on each boundary face, a requirement essential for the Hodge Laplacian with absolute or relative boundary conditions where the Laplacian itself has the transmission property but the individual boundary operators do not.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Nonlinear Lower Order Terms

Consider a Cauchy problem for a pseudodifferential operator P(x,D) of real principal type with a nonlinear lower order term F(u,∇u). The principal symbol pm(x,ξ) determines the characteristic set and the Hamiltonian flow. Well‑posedness in Sobolev spaces Hs requires an energy estimate that controls the nonlinear term. Bony’s paradifferential calculus decomposes the product into a paraproduct and a remainder, allowing one to replace the nonlinear term by a paradifferential operator TF with symbol in a suitable symbol class Smρ,δ. The Levi condition for the linearised operator demands that the subprincipal symbol satisfy a sign condition on the characteristic set; for nonlinear problems this condition is imposed on the linearisation around the approximate solution. Using a Nash–Moser iteration in weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,w with quasi‑homogeneous weights adapted to the Newton polygon of the operator, one constructs a parametrix for the linearised problem and obtains tame estimates. The propagation of singularities theorem then holds microlocally: the wavefront set of the solution is contained in the union of the forward Hamiltonian flowout of the initial wavefront set and the characteristic set where the nonlinear term may create new singularities. For semilinear wave equations with quadratic derivative nonlinearities, the null condition ensures that the worst interactions are cancelled, yielding global existence for small data. The microlocal analysis of such problems relies on the symbolic calculus of paradifferential operators, the construction of approximate solutions via geometric optics, and the use of microlocal defect measures to capture concentration effects.

Energy Estimate with Paradifferential Regularization
Show how to obtain an a priori estimate for the semilinear wave equation □u = (∂tu)2 on ℝ1+3 using paradifferential calculus.
1. Write the equation as (□ − T(∂tu))u = R(u) where T(∂tu) is the paradifferential operator with symbol ∂tu and R(u) is a smoother remainder. 2. The principal symbol of □ is τ2 − |ξ|2; the subprincipal symbol of T(∂tu) is i∂tu. 3. Apply the energy method to the linearised operator □ − T(∂tu) in the weighted space Hs,1 with weight ⟨ξ⟩1. 4. The Levi condition holds because the imaginary part of the subprincipal symbol is non‑negative on the characteristic set. 5. Gronwall’s inequality yields ‖u(t)‖Hs ≤ C‖u(0)‖Hs exp(C∫0t‖∂tu‖L∞ ds). 6. For small data, the null structure gives ‖∂tu‖L∞ ≤ Cε⟨t⟩−1, closing the estimate.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a nonlinear Cauchy problem appears, first identify the principal symbol and verify the real principal type condition. Then treat lower order nonlinearities as paradifferential perturbations; the key is to check the Levi sign condition on the subprincipal symbol of the linearised operator. Use weighted Sobolev spaces adapted to the Newton polygon to obtain tame estimates for the Nash–Moser scheme.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Flat Spacetimes with Nontrivial Topology

On an asymptotically flat spacetime (M,g) with nontrivial topology (e.g., a black hole exterior with a wormhole), the scattering matrix S(λ) for the wave equation □gu = 0 at frequency λ is a Fourier integral operator on the sphere at null infinity ℐ+. Its canonical relation is the graph of the geodesic flow on the light cone at null infinity, but trapped geodesics and topological obstructions create additional Lagrangian components. Melrose and Sá Barreto showed that S(λ) admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane; its poles are the quasinormal modes. The microlocal structure of S(λ) is encoded in the resolvent (□g − λ2)−1, which Vasy analysed using the b‑calculus on the radial compactification. The presence of trapped sets leads to a loss of derivatives in the estimate for the resolvent, reflected in the order of the Fourier integral operator. When the topology allows multiple asymptotic ends, the scattering matrix becomes a matrix‑valued Fourier integral operator coupling the ends. The high‑frequency asymptotics of S(λ) are governed by the geometry of the trapped set: if the trapped set is a normally hyperbolic symplectic submanifold, the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator associated to a clean canonical relation; if the trapped set is fractal, the scattering matrix belongs to a more exotic class of operators with non‑smooth canonical relations. These results are essential for understanding the decay of waves on black hole backgrounds and the distribution of resonances.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Fibred Cusps

A fibred cusp metric on a manifold M with boundary ∂M is a metric that near the boundary takes the form dx2/x4 + h(x)/x2 + gF(x), where x is a boundary defining function, h is a family of metrics on the base B of a fibration ∂M → B, and gF is a family of metrics on the fibres. The Laplacian Δg is an element of the fibred cusp calculus of Mazzeo and Melrose. Its indicial operator is obtained by Mellin transform in x and is a family of operators on the fibres parameterised by the dual variable σ. The resolvent (Δg − λ)−1 admits a meromorphic continuation from Im λ ≫ 0 to the complex plane as an operator between weighted Sobolev spaces xαHsfc(M). The poles correspond to eigenvalues of the indicial operator; the weights α must avoid the imaginary parts of the indicial roots, which are determined by the spectrum of the vertical Laplacian on the fibres. The scattering matrix at the continuous spectrum is a pseudodifferential operator on the base B of order 0, and its principal symbol is the identity. The index of the Dirac operator on such manifolds is computed by the fibred cusp index theorem of Vaillant, which expresses the index as an integral of the Atiyah–Singer density plus a boundary term involving the eta invariant of the family of fibre Dirac operators. The microlocal structure of the resolvent is captured by the fibred cusp double space, where the lifted Schwartz kernel is conormal to the lifted diagonal and Legendrian at the fibred cusp face.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Non‑Product Conical Singularities

Consider a manifold M with an isolated conical singularity where the link is a fibration L → B with fibre F, so the metric near the cone tip is dr2 + r2gL(r) with gL(r) a family of metrics on L that does not split as a product. The Laplacian Δ is an element of the iterated edge calculus of Albin, Gell‑Redman, and others. The spectral projector Π[a,b] = 1[a,b](Δ) is a pseudodifferential operator on the resolved space obtained by blowing up the cone tip and then the fibres of the link fibration. Its Schwartz kernel is polyhomogeneous conormal to the lifted diagonal and has a Legendrian singularity at the iterated edge face. The leading asymptotics of the heat kernel trace as t → 0 are given by the Tian–Yau–Zelditch expansion, where the coefficients involve integrals of curvature invariants on the regular part and contributions from the singular strata determined by the geometry of the fibration. The microlocal structure of Π[a,b] is encoded in its wavefront set, which is contained in the conormal bundle of the diagonal together with the flowout of the characteristic variety of the edge operator. The index of a Dirac operator on such a space is computed by the iterated edge index theorem, which expresses the index as a sum of an interior term, a boundary term from the link, and a corner term from the fibre. These results extend the classical conical calculus to non‑product cones and are crucial for the analysis of geometric operators on stratified spaces.

  • Confusing the transmission property with the condition that only the principal symbol satisfies the parity condition; lower order terms can violate transmission even when the principal symbol does not.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on asymptotically flat spacetimes is always a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator whenever the null geodesic flow at infinity is not periodic.
  • Neglecting the weight vector in the quasi‑homogeneous Newton polygon; the distance from the origin to the polygon must be measured in the weighted metric, not the Euclidean metric.
  • Overlooking that the resolvent on a fibred cusp manifold maps between weighted Sobolev spaces only for weights avoiding the imaginary parts of the indicial roots, which are determined by the spectrum of the vertical Laplacian on the fibres.
  • Treating the iterated edge calculus as a simple iteration of the edge calculus; the order of blow‑ups follows the face lattice, and the twisted product involves holonomy of the link fibrations at all depths.
  • Misidentifying the canonical relation of the scattering matrix on asymptotically flat spacetimes; it is the graph of the geodesic flow on the light cone at null infinity, not the flow on spatial infinity.
  • Forgetting that the spectral projector on a non‑product conical singularity is a pseudodifferential operator on the resolved space, not on the original singular space.

The Fibered Corners Calculus and the Resolution of Singularities for Manifolds with Fibered Corners

The fibered corners calculus, developed by Mazzeo, Melrose, and collaborators, extends the b‑calculus and the edge calculus to manifolds whose boundary admits a tower of fibrations. A manifold with fibered corners is a manifold with corners where each boundary hypersurface is the total space of a fibration over the adjacent lower‑dimensional boundary faces, and these fibrations are compatible in the sense of a category of fibrations. The resolution of singularities proceeds by iteratively blowing up the corners in an order dictated by the face lattice, producing a manifold with boundary on which the lifted vector fields are tangent to all boundary hypersurfaces. The resulting calculus of pseudodifferential operators, denoted Ψfcm(M), consists of operators whose Schwartz kernels are conormal to the lifted diagonal and polyhomogeneous conormal at the boundary faces created by the blow‑ups. The principal symbol lives on the fibered corners cotangent bundle fcT*M, a vector bundle whose sections are smooth up to the boundary and satisfy the fibered corners symbol calculus. Ellipticity in this calculus is defined by invertibility of the principal symbol together with invertibility of the normal operators (indicial families) at each boundary face. The normal operator at a face is obtained by Mellin transform in the defining function of that face and is a family of operators on the fiber of the corresponding fibration, parameterized by the dual variable. The Fredholm property for elliptic operators in Ψfcm(M) holds between weighted Sobolev spaces xαHsfc(M) provided the weight vector α avoids the imaginary parts of the indicial roots coming from all normal operators. The index of an elliptic fibered corners operator is computed by a generalization of the Atiyah–Singer index theorem, where the boundary contribution is a sum of eta invariants associated to the family of fiber Dirac operators at each level of the fibration tower. This calculus underlies the analysis of geometric operators on spaces with iterated fibrations, such as the moduli spaces of Riemann surfaces with nodes and the compactifications of locally symmetric spaces.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Non‑Smooth Symbols in Hörmander Classes

Classical microlocal analysis assumes symbols in the Hörmander classes Smρ,δ with ρ>δ≥0, which are smooth in both position and frequency. For many applications — such as hyperbolic equations with coefficients of limited regularity, or nonlinear problems where the symbol depends on a solution — one must work with symbols that are only Zygmund or Besov regular in the spatial variable. The appropriate framework is the paradifferential calculus of Bony and its microlocal refinement by Métivier, Taylor, and others. A symbol a(x,ξ) belonging to the class Γms (s>0) is measurable in x, smooth in ξ, and satisfies |∂ξαa(x,ξ)| ≤ Cα⟨ξ⟩m−|α| with a Zygmund condition of order s in x. The corresponding paradifferential operator Ta is defined by a Littlewood–Paley decomposition: Tau = Σj aj(x,D)Δju, where Δj are dyadic frequency cutoffs and aj is a smooth approximation of a at frequency 2j. The principal symbol of Ta is the equivalence class of a modulo symbols of order m−s. The wavefront set of a distribution u can be characterized by the decay of its paradifferential pieces: (x₀,ξ₀)∉WF(u) iff there exists a conic neighborhood Γ of ξ₀ such that ‖Δju‖L² ≤ CN2−jN for all N when ξ₀∈Γ. For a pseudodifferential operator P with symbol in Γms, the propagation of singularities theorem holds provided s>1: if Pu=f with f smoother than u by m−1 derivatives, then WF(u)WF(f) is invariant under the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol. The proof uses a commutator argument with a paradifferential escape function and requires a sharp Gårding inequality for non‑smooth symbols, which holds when s>1. When s≤1, counterexamples show that singularities can be created or destroyed in a non‑classical way. This theory is essential for the local well‑posedness of quasilinear hyperbolic systems such as the Einstein equations in wave coordinates, where the metric coefficients belong to Cs with s just above the critical regularity.

Worked Example: Paradifferential Energy Estimate
Let P = ∂t + Ta(t,x,Dx) where a(t,x,ξ) ∈ Γ1s with s>1, real principal symbol, and Ta the paradifferential quantization. Show that for any u ∈ C([0,T];Hσ) solving Pu = f, the energy estimate ‖u(t)‖Hσ ≤ C(‖u(0)‖Hσ + ∫0t‖f(s)‖Hσ ds) holds with a constant depending on the Zygmund norm of a.
Apply the paradifferential operator Λσ = (1−Δ)σ/2 to the equation: ∂tΛσu + ΛσTau = Λσf. Take the L² inner product with Λσu and take real parts. The commutator [Λσ,Ta] is a paradifferential operator of order σ with symbol in Γσs−1 (since s>1, s−1>0). By the sharp Gårding inequality for paradifferential operators with Zygmund symbols, Re⟨Tav,v⟩ ≥ −C‖v‖²L². The commutator term is bounded by C‖u‖²Hσ. Grönwall's inequality yields the stated estimate.
Exam Strategy: Non‑Smooth Symbol Classes
When a question asks about propagation of singularities for operators with limited regularity, identify the symbol class (Γms or Besov Bs∞,∞). The threshold s>1 is the standard condition for classical propagation; if s≤1, mention the breakdown and the need for alternative methods (e.g., FBI transform or semiclassical measures). Cite the paradifferential calculus of Bony–Métivier and the sharp Gårding inequality as the key tools.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Flat Spacetimes with Non‑Stationary Black Holes

On a stationary black hole spacetime such as Kerr, the scattering matrix at fixed energy is a pseudodifferential operator on the sphere at null infinity, because the null geodesic flow at infinity is periodic. For a non‑stationary black hole — for instance, a spacetime describing a black hole formed by gravitational collapse, or a binary black hole merger — the geometry at null infinity is still asymptotically flat, but the null geodesic flow is no longer periodic; it exhibits a chaotic scattering behavior. The scattering matrix S(λ) for the wave equation □gu=0 at frequency λ becomes a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation given by the graph of the null geodesic flow on the light cone at null infinity. The phase function is generated by the Busemann function of the spacetime, and the amplitude is a symbol of order 0. The wavefront set of the Schwartz kernel of S(λ) is contained in the conormal bundle of the diagonal together with the flowout of the characteristic variety under the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol of □g. In the non‑stationary case, the flowout is not a closed loop, so the scattering matrix is not a pseudodifferential operator. Its singularities propagate along null geodesics that may orbit the black hole multiple times before escaping to infinity, producing a fractal structure in the scattering kernel. The resonances (poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent) are related to the trapped set of the null geodesic flow; for non‑stationary black holes the trapped set is a normally hyperbolic invariant manifold with a fractal dimension, leading to a resonance free strip of size proportional to the topological pressure of the flow. The microlocal analysis of this setting uses the Vasy calculus on the radial compactification of the spacetime, combined with a positive commutator argument at the event horizon and at null infinity. The resulting resolvent estimates imply local energy decay for the wave equation with a loss of derivatives determined by the pressure of the trapped set.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Groupoids with Non‑Hausdorff Orbit Spaces

The convolution calculus of pseudodifferential operators on a Lie groupoid 𝒢 ⇉ M, developed by Connes, Nistor, Weinstein, and others, assumes that the orbit space M/𝒢 is Hausdorff (or at least that the groupoid is proper). Many geometric situations — such as the holonomy groupoid of a foliation with non‑compact leaves, the tangent groupoid of a manifold with corners, or the groupoid of a non‑proper action — yield non‑Hausdorff orbit spaces. The microlocal theory on such groupoids requires a refinement of the symbol calculus that takes into account the non‑Hausdorff nature of the unit space. One approach, due to Debord and Skandalis, replaces the groupoid by its Haefliger groupoid, which is a proper étale groupoid Morita equivalent to the original one, and defines pseudodifferential operators as operators on the Haefliger groupoid that are invariant under the Morita equivalence. The principal symbol then lives on the Lie algebroid A𝒢, but the composition formula involves a twisted product that incorporates the holonomy of the groupoid. The wavefront set of a distribution on a groupoid is defined as a subset of the cosphere bundle of the Lie algebroid, invariant under the groupoid action. For a pseudodifferential operator P in the groupoid calculus, the propagation of singularities theorem states that WF(Pu)WF(u) is contained in the characteristic variety of P and is invariant under the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol on the Lie algebroid. The index theory for elliptic operators on non‑Hausdorff groupoids is formulated in terms of the K‑theory of the groupoid C*‑algebra, which may be computed using the Baum–Connes assembly map for the groupoid. When the groupoid is the holonomy groupoid of a foliation, the index theorem reduces to the Connes–Skandalis longitudinal index theorem, and the non‑Hausdorffness of the leaf space is reflected in the fact that the analytic index takes values in the K‑theory of the foliation C*‑algebra, which is not commutative. This framework is essential for the analysis of hypoelliptic operators on foliated manifolds, such as the Kohn Laplacian on CR manifolds with non‑compact leaves.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Fibered Corners and the b‑Fibered Calculus

The b‑fibered calculus, introduced by Mazzeo and Vertman, combines the b‑calculus (for manifolds with boundary) with the fibered corners calculus to treat manifolds whose boundary has a fibration structure and where the metric is of b‑type near the boundary. A typical example is a manifold with fibered corners equipped with a metric that near a boundary hypersurface H takes the form dx²/x² + h(x)/x² + gF(x), where x is a boundary defining function for H, h is a family of metrics on the base of the fibration H → B, and gF is a family of metrics on the fibers. The heat kernel of the Laplacian Δg on such a space is constructed by lifting to the b‑fibered heat space, obtained by blowing up the diagonal at the boundary faces in an order compatible with the fibered corners structure. The lifted heat kernel is polyhomogeneous conormal at all boundary faces of the heat space, with leading asymptotics determined by the model heat kernels on the fibers and on the base. The small‑time asymptotics of the trace of the heat kernel yield a generalized McKean–Singer formula: Tr(e−tΔ) ∼ Σk≥0 ak t(k−n)/2 + Σj bj t−j/2 log t, where the coefficients ak are integrals of local curvature invariants on the interior, and the coefficients bj are integrals of invariants on the boundary strata involving the second fundamental form and the curvature of the fibration. The logarithmic terms appear precisely when the indicial roots of the normal operators differ by integers, a phenomenon captured by the b‑fibered calculus. The resolvent (Δ−λ)−1 admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane as an operator between weighted b‑fibered Sobolev spaces, with poles at the indicial roots. The scattering matrix at the continuous spectrum is a pseudodifferential operator on the base B of order 0, and its principal symbol is the identity. The index of a Dirac operator on a manifold with fibered corners is given by the b‑fibered index theorem, which expresses the index as an integral of the Atiyah–Singer density over the interior plus a sum of boundary contributions involving the eta invariants of the family of fiber Dirac operators at each level of the fibration tower. This calculus provides the microlocal foundation for the analysis of geometric evolution equations on moduli spaces with fibered corner compactifications.

  • Confusing the fibered corners calculus with a simple iteration of the edge calculus; the blow‑up order follows the face lattice and the twisted product involves holonomy at all depths.
  • Assuming that non‑smooth symbols in Γms with s≤1 still satisfy the classical propagation of singularities; counterexamples show singularities can be created or destroyed.
  • Treating the scattering matrix on a non‑stationary black hole spacetime as a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator with a canonical relation given by the non‑periodic null geodesic flow.
  • Overlooking that the groupoid C*‑algebra for a non‑Hausdorff orbit space is non‑commutative, so the analytic index lives in a non‑commutative K‑theory group, not in ℤ.
  • Neglecting the logarithmic terms in the heat trace asymptotics on fibered corners; they arise from integer differences of indicial roots and are essential for the index formula.
  • Misidentifying the normal operator in the b‑fibered calculus; it is obtained by Mellin transform in the b‑defining function and is a family on the fiber, not on the base.
  • Forgetting that the sharp Gårding inequality for paradifferential operators requires s>1; for s≤1 the inequality fails and energy estimates break down.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Conical Singularities

On a manifold M with an isolated conical singularity the metric near the tip takes the form dr² + r² h where h is a metric on the link Y. The Laplacian Δg is not essentially self‑adjoint on C₀^∞(M{tip}); its Friedrichs extension has domain described by weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,γ with weight γ measuring decay relative to r. The resolvent (Δ−λ)⁻¹ for λ∉[0,∞) is constructed by Mellin transform in the radial variable r. The Mellin symbol is a family of operators on the link Y depending on the complex Mellin parameter σ. Poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent correspond to indicial roots, i.e. values of σ for which the model operator on the infinite cone fails to be invertible. These roots are explicitly given by the eigenvalues of the Dirichlet Laplacian on Y. The resolvent kernel lifts to a polyhomogeneous conormal distribution on the resolved double space obtained by blowing up the diagonal at the tip and then the front face at the boundary. The leading asymptotics at the front face are governed by the model resolvent on the exact cone, while the side faces encode the propagation of singularities along the bicharacteristics of the principal symbol |ξ|²−λ. The microlocal structure is captured by the calculus of conormal distributions on manifolds with corners: the resolvent is a pseudodifferential operator of order −2 in the interior, a conormal distribution of order −2 at the lifted diagonal, and a Legendrian distribution associated to the flow‑out of the characteristic variety at the boundary faces. This description yields precise mapping properties between weighted Sobolev spaces and, via the limiting absorption principle, the radiation fields at infinity. The scattering matrix at energy λ is a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 on the link Y whose principal symbol is the identity; its subprincipal symbol encodes the geometry of the link and the choice of self‑adjoint extension. The meromorphic continuation of the resolvent to the whole complex plane as an operator between weighted spaces is a cornerstone for inverse scattering on conical manifolds and for the analysis of the wave equation with low regularity data.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Minkowski Spacetimes

Consider a globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold (M,g) which outside a compact set is isometric to Minkowski space. The wave operator □g has a well‑defined forward fundamental solution E⁺. The scattering matrix S(λ) at frequency λ>0 maps incoming radiation fields on past null infinity ℐ⁻ to outgoing fields on future null infinity ℐ⁺. Microlocally, S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 associated with the canonical relation given by the null geodesic flow from ℐ⁻ to ℐ⁺. Because the spacetime is asymptotically flat, the flow is a diffeomorphism between the cosphere bundles of ℐ⁻ and ℐ⁺ away from trapped null geodesics. The principal symbol of S(λ) is the square root of the Jacobian of this diffeomorphism, which equals 1 for the standard Minkowski metric. Trapped geodesics generate a Lagrangian submanifold in the characteristic variety; their contribution appears as a singular component of the scattering matrix, a Legendrian distribution associated to the trapped set. The high‑frequency asymptotics of S(λ) are obtained by a geometric optics construction: one solves the eikonal equation for the phase function and the transport equations for the amplitude along the null bicharacteristics. The resulting parametrix is a sum of a pseudodifferential part (the direct transmission) and a Fourier integral part (the diffracted waves). The diffracted part is microlocally supported on the flow‑out of the trapped set and its wavefront set is contained in the union of the canonical relation and the conormal bundle of the trapped set. This structure underlies the proof of the local energy decay estimate for the wave equation on asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes and the meromorphic continuation of the cut‑off resolvent. In the presence of a non‑degenerate trapped set (e.g. a photon sphere), the scattering matrix exhibits a resonance expansion with poles at the quasinormal modes; the residues are finite‑rank operators whose wavefront sets are precisely the trapped Lagrangian.

Worked Example: Resolvent on a Conical Manifold
Let M be the two‑dimensional cone with metric dr² + r² dθ², θ∈[0,2π). Compute the indicial roots of the Laplacian Δ = ∂r² + (1/r)∂r + (1/r²)∂θ² and describe the leading singularity of the resolvent kernel (Δ−λ)⁻¹ at the tip for λ∉[0,∞).
Separate variables: write u(r,θ) = riσ eikθ. Substituting into (Δ−λ)u = 0 gives the indicial equation σ² + k² = 0, so σ = ± i|k|. The indicial roots are purely imaginary and symmetric about 0. For each integer k the Mellin symbol is invertible except at σ = ± i|k|. The resolvent kernel lifts to the resolved double space; at the front face (the blown‑up tip) the leading term is the model resolvent on the exact cone, which is a conormal distribution of order −2 with respect to the lifted diagonal. Its singularity is of type |r−r'|⁻¹ (in two dimensions) multiplied by a smooth function of the angular variables. The polyhomogeneous expansion contains terms ri|k| and r−i|k|, reflecting the two indicial roots. No logarithmic terms appear because the roots never differ by an integer. Hence the resolvent is a pseudodifferential operator of order −2 in the interior and a conormal distribution of order −2 at the front face, with no logarithmic singularities.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When asked to identify the microlocal nature of a resolvent or scattering matrix, first locate the underlying geometry (cone, asymptotically flat, etc.), then recall the associated blow‑up space. The order of the operator is determined by the principal symbol (−2 for Laplacian resolvent, 0 for scattering matrix). The wavefront set is always contained in the characteristic variety together with the flow‑out of any trapped or boundary‑generated Lagrangians. Memorise the standard indicial root formula for cones: σ = ±√(λk − (n−2)²/4) where λk are eigenvalues of the link Laplacian.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Stratified Singularities

A stratified space X is a union of smooth manifolds (strata) satisfying the frontier condition. Near a stratum S of codimension k the metric is modeled on a product of a smooth factor and a cone over a link L which itself may be stratified. The heat kernel of the Laplacian Δ on X is constructed by iterating the edge‑calculus blow‑up procedure: one first blows up the diagonal at the deepest stratum, then proceeds outward along the stratification. The resulting heat space is a manifold with corners whose faces correspond to the various strata and their pairwise collisions. The lifted heat kernel is polyhomogeneous conormal on this space; its leading asymptotics at each face are given by the model heat kernels on the corresponding cones or products of cones. The small‑time expansion of the trace Tr(e−tΔ) contains powers t(d−j)/2 where d is the dimension of the top stratum and j runs over the depths of the stratification. Logarithmic terms t−m/2 log t appear precisely when the indicial roots of the normal operators on successive strata differ by integers; these are captured by the iterated edge calculus. The coefficients of the expansion are integrals of local curvature invariants on the regular part together with contributions from the links, which involve the eta invariants of the family of link operators. The resolvent (Δ−λ)⁻¹ admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane as an operator between weighted edge Sobolev spaces; its poles are the indicial roots. The scattering matrix at the continuous spectrum is a pseudodifferential operator on the space of boundary data of order 0, and its principal symbol is the identity. This framework provides the microlocal foundation for the index theory of Dirac operators on stratified spaces, where the analytic index is expressed as a sum of interior characteristic integrals and boundary eta invariants at each stratum.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Fibred Corners

Manifolds with fibred corners generalize manifolds with boundary by allowing the boundary hypersurfaces to be fibred over lower‑dimensional bases, with the fibres meeting in a compatible way at corners. A typical metric near a corner of codimension two takes the form dx²/x² + dy²/y² + h(x,y) where x,y are boundary defining functions and h is a family of metrics on the fibres. The b‑fibred calculus of Mazzeo–Vertman combines the b‑calculus (for the x direction) with the fibred‑boundary calculus (for the y direction). The resolvent (Δ−λ)⁻¹ is constructed by lifting to the b‑fibred double space, obtained by blowing up the diagonal at all boundary faces in an order dictated by the face lattice. The lifted resolvent is polyhomogeneous conormal at each face; its leading term at a face corresponding to a boundary hypersurface H is the model resolvent on the infinite b‑fibred cylinder associated to H. The normal operator is obtained by Mellin transform in the b‑defining functions and is a family of operators on the fibres parametrised by the Mellin variables. Poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent correspond to indicial roots, which are the eigenvalues of the model normal operators. The scattering matrix at the continuous spectrum is a pseudodifferential operator on the base of the fibration of order 0; its principal symbol is the identity. The index of a Dirac operator on a manifold with fibred corners is given by the b‑fibred index theorem: the index equals the integral of the Atiyah–Singer density over the interior plus a sum of boundary contributions involving the eta invariants of the family of fibre Dirac operators at each level of the fibration tower. This calculus is essential for the analysis of geometric evolution equations on moduli spaces with fibred‑corner compactifications, such as the Weil–Petersson metric on the Deligne–Mumford compactification of the moduli space of curves.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Quadratic Nonlinearities and the Null Condition

Consider a semilinear pseudodifferential equation ∂tu = P(D)u + Q(u,u) where P is a real‑principal‑type operator of order m and Q is a quadratic form in u and its derivatives. The null condition is a structural hypothesis on Q that guarantees cancellation of the worst resonant interactions along the characteristic variety of P. Microlocally, the solution is studied via the paradifferential calculus of Bony: one writes Q(u,u) = TQ'(u)u + R where T is the paraproduct and R is a smoother remainder. The principal symbol of the linearised operator at a high‑frequency approximate solution is P(ξ) + i{Q'(u),P}(ξ) where {·,·} denotes the Poisson bracket. The null condition implies that the quadratic symbol Q'(u) vanishes on the characteristic set of P, so the subprincipal correction does not create new singularities. Propagation of singularities then follows the Hamiltonian flow of P, and the wavefront set of the solution remains contained in the union of the forward flow‑out of the initial wavefront set. Energy estimates in semiclassical Sobolev spaces Hs_h are obtained by a positive‑commutator argument using a microlocal weight adapted to the flow. The lifespan of the solution is at least of order ε−1 for data of size ε in a suitable Sobolev space, and global existence holds for small data when the null condition is satisfied and the linear part satisfies the Strauss exponent condition. The microlocal structure of the solution is captured by the semiclassical defect measure, which satisfies a Liouville transport equation along the bicharacteristic flow. This framework extends to systems where the null condition is replaced by a matrix‑valued null form, and to higher‑order nonlinearities where the cubic null condition plays the analogous role.

  • Confusing the indicial roots for a cone with those for an edge; the cone roots come from the link Laplacian, while edge roots involve a family of operators on the fibre.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on an asymptotically Minkowski spacetime is a pseudodifferential operator everywhere; it is a Fourier integral operator with a canonical relation given by the null geodesic flow, and only its principal symbol is scalar.
  • Neglecting logarithmic terms in the heat trace on stratified spaces; they arise from integer differences of indicial roots across strata and are essential for the index formula.
  • Treating the normal operator in the b‑fibred calculus as a single operator on the base; it is a family on the fibres obtained by Mellin transform in all b‑defining functions simultaneously.
  • Overlooking that the null condition for quadratic nonlinearities requires the quadratic symbol to vanish on the characteristic variety of the linear operator, not merely on the zero set of the principal symbol.
  • Misidentifying the order of the resolvent on a conical manifold; it is a pseudodifferential operator of order −2 in the interior but a conormal distribution of order −2 at the front face, not a standard pseudodifferential operator up to the boundary.
  • Forgetting that the iterated edge calculus blow‑up order follows the depth of the stratification; reversing the order destroys the product‑type structure needed for polyhomogeneity.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Non-Effectively Hyperbolic Characteristics

When the principal symbol p(x,ξ) of a pseudodifferential operator P vanishes to order k ≥ 2 along a submanifold Σ of the characteristic variety, the operator is said to have non-effectively hyperbolic characteristics. The Hamilton vector field Hp then vanishes to order k−1 on Σ, and the standard propagation of singularities theorem fails because the bicharacteristic flow is not well-defined as a smooth vector field. The microlocal analysis of such operators requires a refinement of the wavefront set that captures the order of vanishing of the symbol and the subprincipal symbol. For operators with double characteristics (k=2), the Levi condition — that the subprincipal symbol be real on Σ — is necessary for local solvability in the C∞ category. When the Levi condition holds, one can construct a parametrix using a phase function that is homogeneous of degree 1 in ξ but has a degenerate critical manifold along Σ. The resulting Fourier integral operator has a canonical relation that is a Lagrangian submanifold with a fold singularity along Σ. The wavefront set of the solution propagates along the generalized bicharacteristics, which are the integral curves of the Hamiltonian vector field of the subprincipal symbol restricted to Σ. For higher-order vanishing (k≥3), the situation is more delicate: the operator may fail to be locally solvable even if the subprincipal symbol is real, and the loss of derivatives in the a priori estimates depends on the order of vanishing. The microlocal energy estimates are obtained by conjugating the operator with a weight function that is adapted to the geometry of Σ, typically of the form exp(λφ(x,ξ)) where φ is a defining function for Σ raised to a fractional power. The optimal loss of derivatives is given by the order of the operator minus the order of vanishing of the symbol, and this loss is sharp as shown by counterexamples of Hörmander and Ivrii. The theory extends to systems where the characteristic variety has multiple components intersecting non-transversally, and the microlocal structure of the solution is captured by a matrix-valued defect measure that satisfies a system of transport equations along the generalized bicharacteristic flow.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Flat Spacetimes

On an asymptotically flat spacetime (M,g) satisfying the Einstein vacuum equations, the scattering matrix S(λ) for the wave equation □gu=0 at frequency λ ∈ ℝ{0} is a Fourier integral operator on the sphere at null infinity S2 with canonical relation given by the null geodesic flow from past to future null infinity. Unlike the asymptotically de Sitter or anti-de Sitter cases, the canonical relation is not a graph but has caustics corresponding to conjugate points along null geodesics. The principal symbol of S(λ) is a half-density on the canonical relation that can be computed explicitly in terms of the Van Vleck determinant of the null geodesic flow. At low frequencies (λ→0), the scattering matrix has a logarithmic singularity arising from the zero-energy resonance of the Laplacian on the spatial slices; this is related to the ADM mass of the spacetime via the formula limλ→0 λ−1 log ‖S(λ)−I‖ = −4πmADM. At high frequencies (λ→∞), the scattering matrix is a semiclassical Fourier integral operator with parameter h=λ−1, and its wavefront set is the graph of the classical scattering map on the cotangent bundle of S2. The microlocal structure of S(λ) encodes the black hole quasi-normal modes as poles of the meromorphic continuation of S(λ) to the lower half-plane; these poles correspond to the eigenvalues of the generator of the null geodesic flow on the trapped set. The trapped set for the Schwarzschild or Kerr spacetime is a normally hyperbolic invariant manifold, and the quasi-normal modes are distributed according to a Weyl law with a fractal correction term determined by the topological pressure of the flow. The scattering matrix also satisfies a unitarity relation S(λ)*S(λ)=I for real λ, which microlocally implies that the canonical relation is a symplectomorphism on the elliptic set and a Lagrangian correspondence with a fold on the hyperbolic set. The inverse scattering problem — recovering the metric from the scattering matrix — is microlocally equivalent to the boundary rigidity problem for the null geodesic flow, and the linearized problem is a Fourier integral operator with a canonical relation given by the X-ray transform on null geodesics.

Worked Example: Non-Effectively Hyperbolic Operator
Consider the operator P = Dt2 − t2Dx2 on ℝ2. Its principal symbol is p(t,x,τ,ξ) = τ2 − t2ξ2. Show that P has double characteristics on {t=0} and determine the Levi condition.
The characteristic variety is Σ = {τ2 = t2ξ2}. On {t=0}, we have τ=0, so Σ contains {t=τ=0}. The differential dp = 2τdτ − 2tξ2dt − 2t2ξdξ vanishes on {t=τ=0}, so the characteristics are double. The Hamilton vector field is Hp = 2τ∂t − 2tξ2∂τ + 2t2ξ∂x, which vanishes to first order on {t=τ=0}. The subprincipal symbol is psub = 0 (since P has real coefficients and no first-order terms). The Levi condition requires Im psub = 0 on Σ, which holds trivially. However, the operator is not locally solvable at {t=τ=0} because the subprincipal symbol does not satisfy the strict Levi condition (the Hessian of p in the normal directions has signature (1,1)). The loss of derivatives for the Cauchy problem is 1/2, as shown by the change of variables s = t2/2 which transforms P into a weakly hyperbolic operator with symbol τ2 − sξ2.
Exam Strategy: Identifying Characteristic Types
When a question asks about the Cauchy problem for a pseudodifferential operator, first compute the principal symbol and its Hamilton vector field. If Hp vanishes on the characteristic set, check the order of vanishing: order 1 means effectively hyperbolic (standard propagation), order ≥2 means non-effectively hyperbolic. For double characteristics, write down the Levi condition explicitly — it is a necessary condition for C∞ solvability. If the operator has real coefficients and no lower-order terms, the Levi condition holds automatically, but local solvability may still fail due to the geometry of the characteristic variety. Always check whether the subprincipal symbol is real on the characteristic set; if not, the operator is not locally solvable in the C∞ category.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Incomplete Edge Singularities

An incomplete edge manifold is a manifold M with a stratified boundary where the singular strata are fibrations Z → Y with fibre F, and the metric near the singular stratum is asymptotic to a warped product dr2 + r2gF + gY with r a defining function for the stratum. Unlike the iterated edge calculus where the fibration is complete, the incomplete edge calculus allows the fibre F to have boundary, leading to a double stratification. The pseudodifferential operators in this calculus are defined by a blow-up of the product M × M that resolves the diagonal and the boundary faces corresponding to the incomplete edges. The symbol calculus involves three types of symbols: the interior symbol on T*M°\\0, the edge symbol on the compressed cotangent bundle of the edge, and the incomplete edge symbol on a further blow-up that captures the boundary of the fibre. The normal operator at an incomplete edge is a family of operators on the infinite cone over F parametrized by the Mellin variables in the base Y and the edge defining function. The indicial roots are the eigenvalues of the model operator on the cone, and they determine the asymptotic behavior of solutions near the incomplete edge. The Fredholm property for an elliptic operator in this calculus requires that the operator be invertible on the interior, that the edge symbol be invertible on the edge face, and that the incomplete edge symbol be invertible on the incomplete edge face. The index theorem for the incomplete edge calculus involves a sum of contributions from the interior (the Atiyah-Singer integrand), the edge strata (eta invariants of the family of fibre Dirac operators), and the incomplete edge strata (a new term involving the spectral flow of the family of operators on the cone over F as the base point moves in Y). This calculus is essential for the analysis of geometric evolution equations on moduli spaces with incomplete edge compactifications, such as the Weil-Petersson metric on the moduli space of Riemann surfaces with nodes where the fibres are punctured Riemann surfaces.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Conic Singularities and the Mazzeo-Melrose Calculus

The Mazzeo-Melrose calculus (or b-calculus on conic manifolds) provides a framework for analyzing the resolvent (Δ−λ2)−1 on a manifold M with conic singularities, where the metric near the singularity is dr2 + r2gY with Y a compact manifold (the link). The resolvent is constructed as a polyhomogeneous conormal distribution on the resolved space Mk2 obtained by blowing up the diagonal and the boundary faces. The leading singularity of the resolvent at the front face is a conormal distribution of order −2 associated to the lifted diagonal, while the behavior at the side faces is determined by the indicial roots of the model operator on the exact cone. The indicial roots are the solutions σ ∈ ℂ to the equation σ(σ+n−2) = μ where μ is an eigenvalue of the Laplacian on Y and n = dim M. For λ in the continuous spectrum [0,∞), the resolvent has a limit as Im λ → 0+ which is a pseudodifferential operator of order −2 on the interior but has a logarithmic singularity at the boundary if there are integer differences between indicial roots. The scattering matrix S(λ) is a pseudodifferential operator on Y of order 0 whose principal symbol is the identity; its subprincipal symbol encodes the scattering phase shift. The meromorphic continuation of the resolvent to the complex plane has poles at the eigenvalues of Δ (if any) and at the indicial roots shifted by integers; these poles correspond to the resonances of the Laplacian. The wave trace Tr cos(t√Δ) has singularities at the lengths of closed geodesics on M and at the lengths of geodesics that reflect off the cone point; the latter are captured by the Poisson formula for conic manifolds, which involves a sum over the indicial roots. The heat kernel e−tΔ has a polyhomogeneous expansion as t → 0 with coefficients that are integrals of local invariants on M° and boundary terms involving the heat kernel on the cone over Y. The index of a Dirac operator on a conic manifold is given by the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer formula with the eta invariant of the link operator DY appearing as the boundary correction.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Logarithmic Singularities in the Coefficients

Consider a pseudodifferential operator P = p(x,Dx) where the symbol p(x,ξ) has logarithmic singularities in the x-variable, for example p(x,ξ) = |ξ|m + a(x)|ξ|m−1 with a(x) = log|x| near x=0. Such operators arise in the linearization of nonlinear PDEs with rough coefficients and in the study of boundary value problems on domains with corners. The microlocal analysis requires a symbol class Smlog consisting of symbols p(x,ξ) such that |∂xα∂ξβp(x,ξ)| ≤ Cαβ|ξ|m−|β|(1+|log|x||)k for some k ≥ 0. The composition formula for such symbols involves logarithmic corrections to the usual asymptotic expansion: the symbol of P1∘P2 is p1#p2 ∼ ∑α (i|α|/α!) ∂ξαp1 ∂xαp2 + log terms. The wavefront set of a solution to Pu = f propagates along the bicharacteristics of the principal symbol, but the logarithmic singularities in the coefficients cause a loss of regularity that is measured by a refined wavefront set WFlog(u) which records the order of logarithmic growth of the Fourier transform. For the Cauchy problem with data in a logarithmic Sobolev space Hslog, the energy estimates take the form ‖u(t)‖Hslog ≤ C exp(Ct) ‖u(0)‖Hslog + ∫0t ‖f(τ)‖Hslog dτ. The loss of derivatives is at most logarithmic, and the solution remains in the same logarithmic Sobolev space as the data. If the logarithmic singularity is in the principal symbol (e.g., p(x,ξ) = |ξ|m log|x|), the operator is not pseudodifferential in the standard sense but belongs to a larger class of operators with operator-valued symbols. The propagation of singularities then follows a generalized Hamiltonian flow where the symbol is matrix-valued and the bicharacteristics are replaced by a flow on a bundle over the phase space. This theory is essential for the analysis of the Einstein equations in wave coordinates where the metric coefficients have logarithmic singularities at the boundary of the domain of dependence.

  • Confusing non-effectively hyperbolic characteristics with effectively hyperbolic ones; the key test is whether the Hamilton vector field vanishes on the characteristic set.
  • Assuming the Levi condition is sufficient for local solvability of double characteristic operators; it is necessary but not sufficient — the geometry of the characteristic variety matters.
  • Treating the scattering matrix on asymptotically flat spacetimes as a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator with caustics, and its principal symbol is a half-density, not a scalar.
  • Neglecting the incomplete edge symbol in the Mazzeo-Vertman calculus; the boundary of the fibre introduces a new symbol level that must be invertible for Fredholmness.
  • Misidentifying the indicial roots for a conic manifold; they come from the eigenvalues of the link Laplacian, not from the base manifold.
  • Overlooking logarithmic terms in the resolvent expansion on conic manifolds; they arise from integer differences of indicial roots and affect the scattering matrix.
  • Assuming logarithmic singularities in coefficients cause a polynomial loss of derivatives; the loss is at most logarithmic, and the solution stays in logarithmic Sobolev spaces.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Non‑Involutive Double Characteristics

When the principal symbol p(x,ξ) vanishes to second order on a submanifold Σ of the cotangent bundle and the Hamilton vector field Hp does not span the normal directions to Σ, the characteristic set is said to be non‑involutive. This situation occurs, for example, for operators of the form P = Dt2 − a(t,x)Dx2 with a changing sign, or for the Keldysh type operators P = Dt2 − t2kDx2. The microlocal analysis proceeds by constructing a parametrix in a refined symbol class Smρ,δ with ρ = 1/2, δ = 0, which captures the loss of one half derivative in the energy estimates. The key tool is the FBI (Fourier–Bros–Iagolnitzer) transform, which linearises the Hamiltonian flow near Σ and reduces the problem to a model operator with constant coefficients on the normal bundle. The propagation of singularities theorem then states that the wavefront set of a solution u to Pu = f is contained in the union of the bicharacteristic leaves of the Hamilton foliation generated by the subprincipal symbol on Σ, together with the forward flow from WF(f). In particular, singularities can cross Σ transversally but may also be trapped along the leaves, leading to a loss of regularity measured by the order of the subprincipal symbol. The Cauchy problem with data in a Gevrey class Gs is well‑posed for s < 2, while for s ≥ 2 one encounters the classical counter‑examples of Colombini–Jannelli–Spagnolo showing that uniqueness fails. The energy estimate takes the form ‖u(t)‖Hs ≤ C‖u(0)‖Hs+1/2 + C∫0t‖f(τ)‖Hs−1/2 dτ, reflecting the half‑derivative loss. This theory underpins the analysis of degenerate hyperbolic equations arising in fluid dynamics and in the study of the Einstein equations in harmonic coordinates where the metric degenerates on a hypersurface.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Minkowski Spacetimes

On a Lorentzian manifold (M,g) that is asymptotically Minkowski — i.e. outside a compact set it is diffeomorphic to ℝ × (R0,∞) × Sn−1 with metric g = −dt2 + dr2 + r2h(r,ω) where h tends to the round metric on the sphere — the scattering matrix S(λ) for the wave operator □g is a Fourier integral operator associated with the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow at infinity. Its principal symbol is a half‑density on the space of null directions at null infinity ℐ+, and it encodes the asymptotic phase shift of solutions. The microlocal structure is obtained by constructing a parametrix for the resolvent (□g − λ2)−1 in the 0‑calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose, then taking the boundary values at the radial sets corresponding to incoming and outgoing null geodesics. The scattering matrix is unitary on the energy space and its singularities are precisely the lengths of null geodesics that connect points on ℐ− to ℐ+. In particular, if the spacetime contains a trapped set (e.g. a photon sphere), the scattering matrix develops a fractal singularity structure reflected in the resonance spectrum. The high‑frequency asymptotics of S(λ) are governed by the so‑called “sojourn times” of null geodesics, and the trace of S(λ) admits a meromorphic continuation with poles at the quasinormal modes. This framework has been used to prove the integrated local energy decay for the wave equation on Kerr–de Sitter spacetimes and to derive the Hawking radiation spectrum from the scattering matrix of a collapsing star.

Worked Example: Energy Estimate for a Non‑Involutive Double Characteristic Operator
Consider the operator P = Dt2 − t2Dx2 on ℝ2. Show that the Cauchy problem with data in Hs loses exactly one half derivative, i.e. the solution satisfies ‖u(t)‖Hs ≤ C‖u(0)‖Hs+1/2.
The principal symbol is p(t,x,τ,ξ) = τ2 − t2ξ2. The characteristic set Σ = {τ = ±tξ} is a double characteristic because ∂τp = ∂ξp = 0 on Σ. The Hamilton vector field Hp = 2τ∂t − 2t2ξ∂x − 2tξ2∂τ + 2tξ∂ξ vanishes on Σ, so Σ is non‑involutive. One constructs a parametrix using the FBI transform with weight φ(t,x,τ,ξ) = |τ − tξ|2 + |τ + tξ|2. The resulting symbol belongs to Sm1/2,0. The energy estimate follows from the sharp Gårding inequality for this symbol class, giving the half‑derivative loss. The constant C depends only on the C∞ bounds of the coefficients.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the order of derivative loss for a double characteristic operator, first check whether the Hamilton vector field vanishes on the characteristic set. If it does, the loss is typically 1/2 derivative for non‑involutive cases and 1 derivative for effectively hyperbolic cases. Memorise the model operators: Dt2 − t2kDx2 (loss 1/2) versus Dt2 − t2k+1Dx2 (loss 1). This pattern appears repeatedly in past papers.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Conical Singularities and the Conic Calculus

A manifold with conical singularities is a space M that near each singular point is diffeomorphic to a cone C(Y) = (0,1)r × Y / {0} × Y where Y is a compact smooth manifold (the link). The natural pseudodifferential calculus on such spaces is the conic calculus of Schulze and Melrose, which uses symbols that are polyhomogeneous in the radial variable r and the dual variable ρ. An operator A in this calculus has a complete symbol σ(r,y,ρ,η) satisfying |∂ra∂yα∂ρb∂ηβσ| ≤ Caαbβr−a−b(1+|ρ|+|η|)m−b−|β|. The principal symbol lives on the compressed cotangent bundle cT*M, which at the cone tip is identified with the cotangent bundle of the link Y. Ellipticity in this calculus means invertibility of the principal symbol on cT*M 0 together with invertibility of the conormal symbol, which is a family of operators on Y obtained by Mellin transform in r. The indicial roots are the poles of the meromorphic inverse of the conormal symbol; they determine the asymptotic expansion of solutions near the tip. The calculus is closed under composition and adjoints, and it contains the parametrices for elliptic cone operators. The index of an elliptic cone operator is given by the Atiyah–Singer formula plus a contribution from the eta invariant of the link operator, exactly as in the APS theorem for manifolds with boundary. This calculus has been extended to the iterated cone calculus for spaces with higher depth singularities, and it underlies the heat kernel asymptotics and the resolvent construction on conic manifolds.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Asymptotically Flat Manifolds

Let (M,g) be a complete Riemannian manifold that is asymptotically Euclidean: outside a compact set it is diffeomorphic to ℝn BR with metric g = δ + h where h = O(r−1) and ∂αh = O(r−1−|α|). The resolvent R(λ) = (Δg − λ2)−1 for Im λ > 0 admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane as an operator between weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,δ → Hs+2,δ−2. The microlocal structure of the continued resolvent is captured by the 0‑calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose on the radial compactification of M. The poles of the continuation are the resonances; they correspond to eigenvalues of the non‑selfadjoint operator obtained by complex scaling. Near the radial sets at the sphere at infinity, the resolvent is a Legendrian distribution associated with the incoming/outgoing Legendrian submanifolds in the compressed cotangent bundle. The scattering matrix S(λ) is the boundary value of the resolvent on these Legendrians and is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 on the sphere Sn−1. Its principal symbol is the identity, and its subprincipal symbol gives the scattering phase. The Poisson formula relates the trace of the wave group cos(t√Δ) to a sum over resonances and over the lengths of closed geodesics. In odd dimensions the resonances are known to lie in a logarithmic neighbourhood of the real axis, while in even dimensions they can accumulate at 0. The high‑energy asymptotics of the resolvent are governed by the classical Hamiltonian flow on the cotangent bundle, and the semiclassical defect measures of resonant states concentrate on the trapped set. This analysis is the foundation for the proof of local energy decay for the wave equation on asymptotically flat spacetimes and for the study of the black hole stability problem.

  • Confusing the half‑derivative loss for non‑involutive double characteristics with the full derivative loss for effectively hyperbolic double characteristics.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes is a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator with a half‑density principal symbol.
  • Neglecting the conormal symbol in the conic calculus; invertibility of the conormal symbol is essential for Fredholmness.
  • Misidentifying the indicial roots as the eigenvalues of the base Laplacian rather than the eigenvalues of the link operator on the cross‑section Y.
  • Overlooking the logarithmic terms in the resolvent expansion on asymptotically flat manifolds; they arise from integer differences of indicial roots and affect the scattering phase.
  • Treating the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent as a holomorphic family on the whole plane; it is only meromorphic with poles at resonances.
  • Assuming that the wavefront set of a solution to a non‑involutive operator propagates only along the Hamilton flow of the principal symbol; the subprincipal symbol on the characteristic variety also governs propagation.

The advanced topics above illustrate how microlocal techniques adapt to increasingly singular geometries and nonlinear interactions, providing the precise tools needed for modern PDE research.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Asymptotically Hyperbolic Manifolds with Conical Singularities

Let (M,g) be a complete Riemannian manifold which is asymptotically hyperbolic: outside a compact set it is diffeomorphic to (0,ε)_x × Y with metric g = (dx2 + h(x))/x2, where h(x) is a family of metrics on the compact boundary Y converging to a metric h0 as x→0. When Y itself carries a conical singularity, the boundary is a stratified space with a cone tip. The Laplacian Δ_g acts on functions and its resolvent R(λ) = (Δ_g − λ2)−1, initially defined for Im λ > 0, admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane as an operator between weighted Sobolev spaces x^δ H^s → xδ−2 Hs+2. The microlocal structure of the continued resolvent is captured by the 0‑calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose on the radial compactification of M, extended to the iterated 0‑calculus to handle the conical singularity on Y. The principal symbol of Δ_g − λ2 is elliptic on the compressed 0‑cotangent bundle 0T^*M, while the conormal symbol obtained by Mellin transform in the defining function x is a family of operators on the link of the cone. The indicial roots are the poles of the meromorphic inverse of this conormal symbol; they determine the asymptotic expansion of resolvent kernels near the boundary and the cone tip. Ellipticity in the iterated 0‑calculus requires invertibility of the principal symbol on 0T^*M 0 and invertibility of the conormal symbol on the model cone. When these hold, a parametrix exists in the calculus and the resolvent is a 0‑pseudodifferential operator with a Legendrian singularity at the radial sets. The scattering matrix S(λ) is the boundary value of the resolvent on the incoming/outgoing Legendrians and is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 on the boundary Y, with principal symbol given by the identity and subprincipal symbol encoding the scattering phase. The Poisson formula relates the trace of the wave group cos(t√Δ_g) to a sum over resonances (poles of R(λ)) and over lengths of closed geodesics. In odd dimensions the resonances lie in a logarithmic neighbourhood of the real axis; in even dimensions they may accumulate at 0. High‑energy asymptotics are governed by the classical Hamiltonian flow on 0T^*M, and semiclassical defect measures of resonant states concentrate on the trapped set. This framework underlies local energy decay for the wave equation on asymptotically hyperbolic spacetimes with conical singularities and provides the analytic foundation for the study of quasinormal modes in black hole perturbations.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Flat Spacetimes with Black Hole Horizons

Consider a stationary, asymptotically flat spacetime (M,g) containing a non‑degenerate Killing horizon, such as the Schwarzschild or Kerr solution. The wave operator □_g acts on scalar fields. After separation of variables using the Killing field ∂_t, the problem reduces to a family of stationary equations (Δ_g − ω2)u = 0 on the spatial slice Σ, where ω ∈ ℂ is the spectral parameter. The resolvent R(ω) = (Δ_g − ω2)−1 for Im ω > 0 continues meromorphically across the real axis to a strip Im ω > −γ, with poles at the quasinormal frequencies. The microlocal structure of the continued resolvent is described by the 0‑calculus on the radial compactification of Σ together with a b‑calculus near the horizon. At the horizon the red‑shift effect yields a normally hyperbolic trapping, which is captured by a normally hyperbolic radial set in the compressed cotangent bundle. The scattering matrix S(ω) is defined as the map from incoming to outgoing radiation fields on the conformal boundary ℐ+. It is a Fourier integral operator of order 0 on the sphere S2, associated with the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow from past to future null infinity. Its principal symbol is the identity, while the subprincipal symbol contains the scattering phase and encodes the effect of the horizon. The principal symbol of S(ω) can be computed by propagating half‑densities along the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol of □_g; the red‑shift at the horizon contributes a factor e−π ω/κ where κ is the surface gravity. The Poisson relation for the wave trace on such spacetimes expresses the singularities of the trace of the wave group in terms of the lengths of closed null geodesics and the quasinormal frequencies. In the Kerr case the lack of spherical symmetry leads to a non‑trivial canonical relation with caustics, and the scattering matrix becomes a Fourier integral operator with a fold singularity. The microlocal analysis of S(ω) provides precise high‑frequency asymptotics for the scattering amplitude and underpins the proof of integrated local energy decay for the wave equation on Kerr backgrounds.

Identifying the principal symbol of the scattering matrix on a Kerr background
Let (M,g) be the Kerr spacetime with mass M and angular momentum a. The scattering matrix S(ω) for the scalar wave equation is a Fourier integral operator on S2. Determine its principal symbol and explain how the surface gravity κ of the event horizon enters the subprincipal symbol.
The principal symbol of S(ω) is the identity on the sphere because the canonical relation of S(ω) is the graph of the identity map from incoming to outgoing directions at null infinity. To see this, note that the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol of □_g maps each incoming null geodesic to a unique outgoing null geodesic preserving the angular coordinates on S2. The subprincipal symbol arises from the transport equation for half‑densities along this flow. Near the horizon the red‑shift effect gives an exponential damping factor e−π ω/κ for each crossing of the horizon. When the geodesic grazes the horizon, the accumulated phase shift contributes a term −iπ ω/κ to the subprincipal symbol. Thus the full symbol of S(ω) is σ(S) = Id + (subprincipal) where the subprincipal part contains the factor e−π ω/κ and the scattering phase.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the principal symbol of a scattering matrix on a black hole background, first identify the canonical relation (usually the identity on the sphere at infinity). Then recall that the principal symbol of a Fourier integral operator associated with a canonical graph is the identity half‑density. The subprincipal symbol is where the geometry of the horizon enters; remember the red‑shift factor e−π ω/κ and the surface gravity κ. Write the answer in two steps: state the principal symbol, then explain the origin of the subprincipal correction.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Fibred Boundaries and the 0‑Calculus with Non‑Product Metrics

A manifold with fibred boundary is a compact manifold M with boundary ∂M equipped with a fibration φ: ∂M → B whose fibres are compact manifolds Z. A 0‑metric with non‑product structure near the boundary takes the form g = (dx2)/x2 + φ^*g_B/x2 + g_Z(x)/x2, where x is a boundary defining function, g_B is a metric on the base B, and g_Z(x) is a family of metrics on the fibres Z depending smoothly on x. The 0‑calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose is built on the 0‑tangent bundle 0TM, whose sections are vector fields tangent to the fibres of φ and vanishing at the boundary. Symbols are polyhomogeneous in the dual variables (ξ, η) corresponding to the base and fibre directions, with homogeneity in the total covector. The principal symbol lives on the compressed 0‑cotangent bundle 0T^*M, which at the boundary identifies with T^*B ⊕ T^*Z. Ellipticity requires invertibility of the principal symbol on 0T^*M 0 and invertibility of the normal operator, a family of operators on the model space ℝ+_x × Z obtained by freezing coefficients at the boundary. The normal operator is a suspended family of operators on Z parametrised by the base covariable η; its invertibility for all η ≠ 0 is the boundary ellipticity condition. When both conditions hold, a parametrix exists in the 0‑calculus and the operator is Fredholm between weighted Sobolev spaces x^δ H^s0. The index is given by the Atiyah–Singer formula on the interior plus a contribution from the family index of the normal operator on the fibres, which can be expressed via the η‑invariant of the induced family on Z. This calculus has been extended to the iterated 0‑calculus for manifolds with higher depth fibred corners, and it underlies the heat kernel asymptotics and the resolvent construction for the Laplacian on asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds with fibred boundary.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Nonlinear Lower Order Terms and the Null Condition

Consider a quasilinear pseudodifferential equation ∂_t u = P(u, ∂_x)u + Q(u, ∂_x) where P is a first‑order pseudodifferential operator with real principal symbol p1(t,x,ξ) and Q contains lower order nonlinearities. The null condition, introduced by Klainerman, requires that the quadratic part of Q vanishes on the characteristic set of p1 when the interacting waves are parallel.
