In microlocal terms, the wavefront set of a solution propagates along the Hamiltonian flow of p1, but nonlinear interactions can create new singularities at the intersection of bicharacteristics. The null condition guarantees that the resonant set where the phase functions of interacting waves align has codimension at least one, preventing the formation of singularities that would otherwise cause a loss of derivatives. Using the paradifferential calculus of Bony, one can decompose the solution into high‑frequency and low‑frequency parts and estimate the nonlinear terms by microlocal energy estimates. The key estimate is a bilinear bound of the form ‖Q(u,v)‖Hs−1 ≤ C ‖u‖H^s ‖v‖H^s for s > n/2, which holds precisely when the null condition is satisfied. The proof proceeds by constructing a microlocal normal form that eliminates the non‑null resonant interactions, reducing the problem to a system where the nonlinearity is cubic or higher. The lifespan of solutions is then shown to be almost global for small data in dimensions n ≥ 3, and global for n ≥ 4. This microlocal approach also yields sharp Strichartz estimates for the linearised operator and provides a framework for treating derivative nonlinearities such as those appearing in the Einstein equations in wave coordinates.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Edge Singularities and the Iterated Edge Calculus

Let M be a compact manifold with an edge singularity: locally M ≅ [0,1)_r × Y × Z where Y is a compact manifold (the edge) and Z is a compact manifold (the link). The metric is an edge metric g = dr2 + r2 g_Z + g_Y + higher order terms. The Laplacian Δ_g is an edge‑degenerate operator. The iterated edge calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose–Vasy provides a pseudodifferential calculus on the iterated edge blow‑up of M, where the edge and the corner are resolved sequentially. Symbols are polyhomogeneous in the radial variable r, the edge covariable η, and the link covariable ζ, with weights reflecting the geometry. The principal symbol lives on the compressed iterated edge cotangent bundle ieT^*M, which at the edge is T^*Y ⊕ T^*Z and at the corner is the product of the two compressed cotangent bundles. Ellipticity requires invertibility of the principal symbol on ieT^*M 0 and invertibility of the normal operators: the edge normal operator (a family on ℝ+_r × Z) and the corner normal operator (a family on ℝ+_r × ℝ+_ρ × Z). The spectral projector Π_λ = 1[0,λ](Δ_g) is a semiclassical Fourier integral operator associated with the Lagrangian given by the flow of the Hamiltonian of the principal symbol. Its Schwartz kernel has a polyhomogeneous expansion at the diagonal and at the edge and corner faces. The leading term on the interior is the standard Weyl law kernel (2π)−n ∫|ξ|_g ≤ √λ ei⟨x−y,ξ⟩ dξ. At the edge the kernel exhibits a conormal singularity along the diagonal in Y, with a coefficient given by the spectral projector of the model edge operator on ℝ+_r × Z. At the corner the kernel is a Legendrian distribution associated with the incoming/outgoing Legendrians in the compressed cotangent bundle. The trace of Π_λ yields the eigenvalue counting function N(λ) = λn/2 Vol(M)/(2π)^n ω_n + lower order terms, where the second term involves the integral of the subprincipal symbol over the characteristic variety and a contribution from the edge and corner geometry expressed via the η‑invariant of the link operators. This microlocal description of the spectral projector is essential for the heat kernel asymptotics, the resolvent construction, and the proof of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem on manifolds with edge singularities.

  • Confusing the indicial roots of the conormal symbol on a cone with the eigenvalues of the base Laplacian; they are the eigenvalues of the link operator on the cross‑section.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on an asymptotically flat spacetime with a horizon is a pseudodifferential operator; it is a Fourier integral operator with a half‑density principal symbol.
  • Neglecting the non‑product terms in the 0‑metric on a fibred boundary; they affect the normal operator and the boundary ellipticity condition.
  • Overlooking the null condition when estimating quadratic nonlinearities; without it the resonant set has full dimension and causes derivative loss.
  • Treating the iterated edge calculus as a simple product of edge and b‑calculi; the corner blow‑up introduces additional Legendrian faces that must be accounted for in the symbol hierarchy.
  • Misidentifying the principal symbol of the spectral projector on an edge manifold as the interior Weyl kernel alone; the edge and corner contributions are essential for the second term in the Weyl law.
  • Assuming the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent on an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold with conical singularities is holomorphic; it has poles at resonances determined by the indicial roots.

The advanced topics above illustrate how microlocal techniques adapt to increasingly singular geometries and nonlinear interactions, providing the precise tools needed for modern PDE research.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Fibred Boundaries

Manifolds with fibred boundaries arise naturally in geometric scattering theory and in the study of asymptotically hyperbolic Einstein metrics. The 0‑calculus of Mazzeo and Melrose provides a pseudodifferential framework adapted to the 0‑cotangent bundle 0T*M, which is obtained by radially compactifying the fibres of the ordinary cotangent bundle at the boundary. Symbols are polyhomogeneous in the 0‑covariables and admit an expansion in powers of the boundary defining function x. The principal symbol lives on the cosphere bundle of 0T*M, while the normal operator is a family of translation‑invariant operators on the model cone ℝ+ × Z, where Z is the fibre of the boundary fibration. Ellipticity requires invertibility of both the principal symbol and the normal operator. The indicial operator, obtained by Mellin transform in the radial variable, governs the boundary spectrum and determines the Fredholm properties between weighted 0‑Sobolev spaces xαHs0. A parametrix exists modulo residual operators, and the index of an elliptic 0‑operator is given by a topological formula involving the 0‑symbol and the η‑invariant of the boundary family. This calculus underlies the proof of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem on asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds and the construction of the resolvent for the Laplacian on Poincaré–Einstein spaces. The 0‑calculus also interacts with the b‑calculus via the 0‑b double space, enabling the analysis of operators that are simultaneously 0‑ and b‑elliptic.

Key references include Mazzeo–Melrose (1998) on the 0‑calculus, Guillarmou (2005) on meromorphic continuation of the resolvent, and Vasy (2013) on the microlocal structure of the scattering matrix. The 0‑pseudodifferential operators form a filtered algebra Ψ0m(M) with symbol map σ0 : Ψ0m → Sm(0T*M)/Sm−1. Composition follows the symbolic calculus with a remainder in Ψ0−∞. The normal operator N(A) is a family on ℝ+ × Z obtained by freezing coefficients at the boundary and rescaling. The indicial family I(A, σ) is the Mellin transform of N(A) in the radial variable; its poles determine the boundary spectrum. Fredholmness of A : xαHs0 → xαHs−m0 holds precisely when α avoids the boundary spectrum. The index theorem for 0‑operators reads ind(A) = ∫0T*M ch(σ0(A)) ∧ Td(0T*M) + η‑terms. This formula reduces to the classical Atiyah–Singer index when the boundary is empty.

Common examiner traps include confusing the 0‑cotangent bundle with the b‑cotangent bundle; the 0‑bundle has fibres that are compactified in the radial direction, while the b‑bundle compactifies the normal covariable. Another trap is neglecting the weight α in the Sobolev spaces; the Fredholm property fails at indicial roots. Students often misstate the normal operator as a single operator rather than a family parametrised by the boundary base Y. The indicial operator is a family of operators on the fibre Z, not a scalar symbol. Finally, the residual ideal in the 0‑calculus consists of operators with Schwartz kernels vanishing to infinite order at all boundary faces, not merely at the diagonal.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Cusp Singularities

Cusp manifolds are non‑compact manifolds whose ends are modelled on a cusp metric g = dr2 + e−2rgZ + gY on [0,∞)r × Y × Z, where Y is a compact base and Z a compact fibre. The cusp calculus of Melrose and Mazzeo provides a pseudodifferential algebra Ψcuspm(M) adapted to the cusp cotangent bundle cuspT*M, obtained by blowing up the boundary at infinity. Symbols are polyhomogeneous in the cusp covariables (τ, η, ζ) with τ dual to r, η dual to Y, ζ dual to Z. The principal symbol lives on the cosphere bundle of cuspT*M. The normal operator is a family of translation‑invariant operators on the model cusp ℝ × Z, obtained by freezing coefficients at the boundary and taking the limit r → ∞. The indicial operator is the Fourier transform in the r variable of the normal operator; its spectrum consists of complex numbers σ such that the model operator fails to be invertible on weighted L2 spaces. The resolvent R(λ) = (Δg − λ)−1 admits a meromorphic continuation from Im λ ≫ 0 to the complex plane as a family of operators between weighted cusp Sobolev spaces. Poles of this continuation are the resonances. The scattering matrix S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator associated to the geodesic flow on the cusp, with principal symbol given by the transmission coefficient of the model cusp operator. The work of Mazzeo–Melrose (1998), Vasy (2001), and Guillarmou (2005) established the meromorphic continuation and the structure of the scattering matrix using the cusp calculus and the method of complex scaling.

The cusp pseudodifferential operators form a filtered algebra with symbol map σcusp : Ψcuspm → Sm(cuspT*M)/Sm−1. Composition is governed by the symbolic calculus with remainder in Ψcusp−∞. The normal operator N(A) is a family on ℝ × Z parametrised by the boundary base Y. The indicial family I(A, σ) is the Fourier transform in r of N(A); its poles are the indicial roots. Fredholmness of A : Hscusp,α → Hs−mcusp,α holds when α avoids the real parts of the indicial roots. The resolvent construction proceeds by building a parametrix in Ψcusp−2 using the symbolic calculus, then correcting the error at the boundary via the normal operator and the indicial operator. The meromorphic continuation follows from the analytic Fredholm theorem applied to the family I(Δg − λ, σ). The scattering matrix S(λ) is obtained from the asymptotics of the resolvent kernel at the boundary; it is a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow on the cusp. Its principal symbol is a half‑density determined by the transmission coefficient of the model cusp operator. The Poisson relation links the singularities of the trace of the wave group to the lengths of closed geodesics on the cusp.

Resolvent Meromorphic Continuation via Cusp Calculus
Explain how the cusp calculus yields a meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (Δg − λ)−1 on a cusp manifold, and identify the role of the indicial operator in locating resonances.
The cusp calculus provides a parametrix Q(λ) ∈ Ψcusp−2 for Δg − λ such that (Δg − λ)Q(λ) = I − R(λ) with R(λ) ∈ Ψcusp−∞. The error R(λ) is a family of smoothing operators depending holomorphically on λ. To invert I − R(λ) we use the analytic Fredholm theorem. The indicial operator I(Δg − λ, σ) is obtained by Mellin transform in the radial variable r of the normal operator N(Δg − λ). Its poles in σ correspond to values where the model cusp operator fails to be invertible on weighted L2 spaces. The resolvent (Δg − λ)−1 = Q(λ)(I − R(λ))−1 then continues meromorphically to ℂ, with poles exactly at those λ for which I(Δg − λ, σ) has a pole on the line Im σ = α (the weight). These poles are the resonances. The indicial operator thus encodes the boundary spectrum and determines the location of resonances.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three‑step structure: (1) build a parametrix in the cusp calculus, (2) analyse the normal and indicial operators to identify the boundary spectrum, (3) apply the analytic Fredholm theorem to obtain meromorphic continuation. Resonances are poles of the continued resolvent and coincide with indicial roots shifted by the weight. Be ready to write the indicial family I(Δg − λ, σ) explicitly for a model cusp metric.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Minkowski Spacetimes

Asymptotically Minkowski spacetimes are Lorentzian manifolds (M, g) that approach the Minkowski metric at null infinity. The wave equation □gu = 0 admits a well‑defined radiation field mapping Cauchy data to functions on null infinity ℐ+. The scattering matrix S maps incoming radiation data on ℐ− to outgoing data on ℐ+. Microlocally, S is a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation given by the null geodesic flow from ℐ− to ℐ+. The principal symbol of S is a half‑density determined by the Jacobian of the geodesic flow and the transmission coefficient across the conformal boundary. The work of Friedlander (1980), Melrose–Sà Barreto (1998), and Vasy (2013) established that S belongs to the class Im(ℐ+, ℐ−; C) where C is the canonical relation of the null bicharacteristic flow. The order m = −(n−1)/2 for spatial dimension n. The clean intersection calculus of Melrose–Uhlmann applies when the geodesic flow has conjugate points; the scattering matrix then becomes a paired Lagrangian distribution. The Poisson relation links the singularities of the trace of the wave group to the lengths of closed null geodesics. In the asymptotically Minkowski setting, the conformal compactification yields a manifold with boundary where the rescaled metric is smooth. The scattering matrix is then the boundary value of the resolvent of the conformal Laplacian. Its microlocal structure is governed by the 0‑calculus on the compactified spacetime. The principal symbol can be computed via the geometric optics construction: it equals the square root of the determinant of the differential of the scattering map on the space of null directions.

The radiation field R+ : H1(Σ) × L2(Σ) → L2(ℐ+) is a Fourier integral operator of order −1/4 associated to the canonical relation given by the forward null bicharacteristic flow. Its adjoint R+* maps incoming data to Cauchy data. The scattering matrix S = R+ R−* is therefore a Fourier integral operator of order −(n−1)/2. The canonical relation C ⊂ T*ℐ+ × T*ℐ− is the graph of the scattering map on the space of null covectors. The principal symbol σ(S) is a half‑density on C given by σ(S) = |det dΦ|1/2 where Φ is the scattering map. When the null geodesic flow has conjugate points, the canonical relation ceases to be a graph and develops caustics; the scattering matrix then lies in the paired Lagrangian class Ip,l(Λ0, Λ1) where Λ0 is the original canonical relation and Λ1 is the caustic Lagrangian. The clean intersection calculus provides a symbolic calculus for such operators. The Poisson relation states that the singular support of the distribution trace of the wave group is contained in the set of lengths of closed null geodesics. This relation is proved by microlocalising the trace to a neighbourhood of a closed geodesic and applying the Duistermaat–Guillemin trace formula for clean intersections.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Non‑Product Type Singularities

Manifolds with non‑product type singularities are stratified spaces where the metric near a stratum Y of codimension k takes the form g = dr2 + r2gZ + gY + higher order terms that couple the radial variable r to the base Y. The iterated edge calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose–Vasy resolves the singularities by a sequence of blow‑ups, producing a manifold with corners Mie. The compressed iterated edge cotangent bundle ieT*M carries the principal symbols. Symbols are polyhomogeneous in the radial variables r1, …, rk and the edge covariables η1, …, ηk. The symbol hierarchy includes the principal symbol on ieT*M, the edge normal operators (families on ℝ+ × Zi), and the corner normal operators (families on ℝ+k × Z). Ellipticity requires invertibility of all these symbols. The indicial operators are obtained by Mellin transform in the radial variables; their joint spectrum determines the boundary spectrum for weighted Sobolev spaces. The Fredholm theory states that an elliptic iterated edge operator A : Hsie,α → Hs−mie,α is Fredholm precisely when the weight α avoids the boundary spectrum. The index theorem for iterated edge operators involves the η‑invariants of the link operators on each stratum and a topological term given by the iterated edge symbol. This calculus underlies the analysis of the heat kernel, the resolvent, and the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem on spaces with non‑product singularities. Key references are Mazzeo (1991) on the edge calculus, Mazzeo–Melrose (1998) on the iterated edge calculus, and Albin–Melrose (2011) on the index theorem.

The iterated edge pseudodifferential operators Ψiem(M) form a filtered algebra with symbol map σie : Ψiem → Sm(ieT*M)/Sm−1. Composition follows the symbolic calculus with remainder in Ψie−∞. The normal operator at a stratum Y is a family NY(A) of translation‑invariant operators on the model cone ℝ+ × ZY. The corner normal operator at a corner of codimension k is a family on ℝ+k × Z. The indicial family IY(A, σ) is the Mellin transform of NY(A) in the radial variable; its poles are the indicial roots. The boundary spectrum is the union of the real parts of all indicial roots. The weighted Sobolev space Hsie,α is defined by the norm ‖u‖2 = ∑j ‖rj−αj u‖2Hsie. Fredholmness holds when α avoids the boundary spectrum. The index formula reads ind(A) = ∫ieT*M ch(σie(A)) ∧ Td(ieT*M) + ∑strata η‑invariants. The heat kernel asymptotics on such spaces are obtained by constructing the heat kernel as a polyhomogeneous distribution on the iterated edge heat space, with leading term given by the model heat kernel on each stratum.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Conical Singularities and the Cone Calculus

Manifolds with conical singularities are spaces M that are smooth away from a singular stratum Y, near which the metric takes the form g = dr2 + r2gZ + gY with Z a compact link. The cone calculus of Schulze and Melrose provides a pseudodifferential algebra Ψconem(M) adapted to the cone cotangent bundle coneT*M, obtained by blowing up the singular stratum. Symbols are polyhomogeneous in the radial covariable ρ and the covariables on Y and Z. The principal symbol lives on the cosphere bundle of coneT*M. The normal operator is a family of translation‑invariant operators on the model cone ℝ+ × Z, obtained by freezing coefficients at r = 0. The indicial operator is the Mellin transform in r of the normal operator; its spectrum consists of complex numbers σ such that the model cone operator fails to be invertible on weighted L2 spaces. Ellipticity requires invertibility of the principal symbol and the normal operator.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Polyhedral Singularities

Polyhedral singularities arise when a Riemannian manifold M is smooth away from a stratified singular set whose links are compact polyhedra rather than smooth manifolds. Near a stratum Y of codimension k the metric takes the form g = dr² + r² gZ + gY + lower order terms, where Z is a compact polyhedral space. The appropriate pseudodifferential algebra is the polyhedral calculus developed by Albin, Gell‑Redman, and Mazzeo. Its operator space Ψmpoly(M) is defined by lifting to the polyhedral resolution obtained by iterated real blow‑ups of the singular strata. The compressed polyhedral cotangent bundle polyT*M carries principal symbols that are polyhomogeneous in the radial variables r1, …, rk and in the covariables on the polyhedral links.

The resolvent R(λ) = (Δ − λ)⁻¹ for the Laplacian Δ is constructed as a family of polyhedral pseudodifferential operators depending holomorphically on the spectral parameter λ away from the spectrum. The key steps are: (1) build a parametrix in Ψ−2poly using the symbolic calculus; (2) analyse the normal operator at each stratum, which is a family of translation‑invariant operators on the model cone ℝ+ × Z; (3) compute the indicial family by Mellin transform in the radial variable, obtaining a meromorphic family of operators on the link Z. The poles of the indicial family are the indicial roots; their real parts form the boundary spectrum. Fredholmness of Δ − λ on weighted polyhedral Sobolev spaces Hspoly,α holds precisely when the weight α avoids the boundary spectrum and λ is not an eigenvalue. The meromorphic continuation of the resolvent across the continuous spectrum follows from the analytic Fredholm theorem applied to the normal operator family. The scattering matrix is then extracted from the asymptotics of the resolvent kernel at the polyhedral infinity, yielding a Fourier integral operator associated to the geodesic flow on the polyhedral link.

Applications include precise asymptotics for the heat kernel on polyhedral spaces, the proof of the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer index theorem for Dirac operators on such spaces, and the description of the wave trace singularities via the Poisson relation for polyhedral geodesics. The polyhedral calculus also provides a framework for the analysis of the Einstein constraint equations on initial data sets with polyhedral singularities.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Flat Spacetimes

On an asymptotically flat Lorentzian manifold (M, g) the wave operator □g admits a well‑defined scattering theory. The scattering matrix S(ω) maps incoming radiation fields at past null infinity ℐ⁻ to outgoing fields at future null infinity ℐ⁺. Microlocally, S(ω) is a Fourier integral operator of order zero associated to the canonical relation given by the null geodesic flow from ℐ⁻ to ℐ⁺. When the spacetime has no trapped null geodesics, this canonical relation is a canonical graph and S(ω) belongs to the standard class I0(ℐ⁻, ℐ⁺; Λ). In the presence of trapping, the flow develops caustics and the canonical relation becomes a paired Lagrangian Λ = (Λ₀, Λ₁) where Λ₀ is the original graph and Λ₁ is the caustic Lagrangian. The scattering matrix then lies in the paired Lagrangian class Ip,l(Λ₀, Λ₁) with orders determined by the order of contact of the trapped geodesics.

The principal symbol of S(ω) on Λ₀ is given by the transport equation along null bicharacteristics; it encodes the gravitational redshift and the focusing effect of the curvature. On the caustic Lagrangian Λ₁ the symbol satisfies a Maslov index jump formula, which can be computed by the clean intersection calculus of Melrose and Uhlmann. The full asymptotic expansion of the scattering matrix at high frequencies ω → ∞ is obtained by constructing a geometric optics parametrix for the wave equation and matching it to the radiation fields. This yields the Duistermaat–Guillemin trace formula for the wave group on asymptotically flat spacetimes: the singular support of the distribution trace of the wave group is contained in the set of lengths of closed null geodesics, and the leading singularity at each length is expressed in terms of the linearised Poincaré map of the corresponding geodesic.

These results underpin the inverse scattering problem for gravitational waves: knowledge of the scattering matrix for all frequencies determines the spacetime metric up to diffeomorphism in a neighbourhood of infinity. The microlocal structure also enters the proof of the black hole stability conjecture, where the decay of solutions to the wave equation on Kerr backgrounds is reduced to estimates for the resolvent of the stationary wave operator, which in turn are derived from the analytic properties of the scattering matrix.

Worked Example: Indicial Roots on a Polyhedral Cone
Consider the model cone ℝ+ × Z where Z is a square with flat metric and Dirichlet boundary conditions on its edges. Compute the indicial roots of the Laplacian Δ = ∂r² + (1/r)∂r + (1/r²)ΔZ acting on functions vanishing on the edges of the square.
Separate variables: u(r,θ) = rσ φ(θ). The eigenvalue problem on the square gives ΔZ φ = −λ φ with Dirichlet conditions. The eigenvalues are λm,n = π²(m² + n²) for m,n ∈ ℕ. Substituting into the radial equation yields σ(σ−1) + σ − λ = 0, i.e. σ² = λ. Hence the indicial roots are σ = ±π√(m² + n²). The boundary spectrum is the set of real parts of these roots, which are exactly the positive numbers π√(m² + n²). For a weight α avoiding this discrete set, the Laplacian is Fredholm on the weighted space Hspoly,α.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the Fredholm condition on a singular space, first identify the calculus (polyhedral, edge, iterated edge, etc.), then write down the indicial family, compute its poles, and state that the weight must avoid the real parts of those poles. This three‑step recipe works for every calculus covered in the syllabus.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Heat Kernel on Manifolds with Polyhedral Singularities and the Polyhedral Heat Calculus

The heat kernel e−tΔ on a polyhedral space is constructed as a polyhomogeneous distribution on the polyhedral heat space M²h,poly, obtained by blowing up the diagonal at t = 0 and then performing the polyhedral resolution of the spatial singularities. The leading term of the heat kernel at the lifted diagonal is the Euclidean heat kernel on the model cone ℝ+ × Z. Away from the diagonal the kernel is smooth and decays exponentially in the geodesic distance. The polyhedral heat calculus Ψmh,poly(M) consists of operators whose Schwartz kernels are polyhomogeneous on M²h,poly with index sets determined by the indicial roots of the Laplacian on each stratum.

The short‑time asymptotics of the trace of the heat kernel yield the polyhedral heat invariants. The coefficient of t−(n−k)/2 in the expansion of Tr(e−tΔ) is an integral over the stratum of codimension k of a local invariant built from the curvature of the link Z and the second fundamental form of the stratum. When the links are smooth, these reduce to the classical Seeley–DeWitt coefficients; for polyhedral links new terms appear involving the dihedral angles of the polyhedron. The index theorem for the Dirac operator on a polyhedral space follows by applying the McKean–Singer formula to the heat kernel of the Dirac Laplacian and evaluating the super‑trace. The contribution from each stratum is expressed in terms of the η‑invariant of the induced Dirac operator on the link, generalising the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer boundary correction.

These heat kernel asymptotics are essential for the analytic torsion on polyhedral spaces, for the study of the Ricci flow on singular spaces, and for the construction of the zeta‑regularised determinant of the Laplacian. The polyhedral heat calculus also provides a natural framework for the proof of the Cheeger–Müller theorem on spaces with polyhedral singularities, relating analytic torsion to Reidemeister torsion.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Polyhedral Singularities

The spectral projector Πλ = 1(−∞,λ](Δ) is a pseudodifferential operator of order zero in the polyhedral calculus. Its Schwartz kernel is a paired Lagrangian distribution associated to the pair (Λ₀, Λ₁) where Λ₀ is the conormal bundle of the diagonal in the polyhedral resolution and Λ₁ is the flow‑out of the characteristic variety of Δ from the singular strata. The principal symbol of Πλ on Λ₀ is the Heaviside function of the principal symbol of Δ − λ; on Λ₁ it satisfies a transport equation that encodes the diffraction of singularities at the polyhedral edges.

The microlocal Weyl law for the counting function N(λ) = Tr Πλ is obtained by integrating the symbol of Πλ over the cosphere bundle of the polyhedral cotangent bundle. The leading term is (2π)−n vol({|ξ|g ≤ √λ}) and the second term involves the total mean curvature of the singular strata weighted by the solid angles of the polyhedral links. Sharp remainder estimates follow from the propagation of singularities theorem for the wave equation on polyhedral spaces, which shows that the wave front set of the wave kernel propagates along broken bicharacteristics that reflect and diffract at the edges according to the laws of geometric optics on polyhedra.

These results give a complete microlocal description of the spectrum of the Laplacian on polyhedral spaces and provide the spectral side of the trace formula for the wave group. They also imply the Hörmander spectral cluster estimates for eigenfunctions on polyhedral manifolds, which are crucial for applications to quantum ergodicity and to the control theory of the wave equation on singular domains.

  • Confusing the polyhedral calculus with the iterated edge calculus: the former uses polyhedral links, the latter uses smooth fibred links.
  • Forgetting that the boundary spectrum is the set of real parts of indicial roots, not the roots themselves.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix is always a standard Fourier integral operator; trapped geodesics force a paired Lagrangian structure.
  • Neglecting the Maslov index contribution when computing the principal symbol on a caustic Lagrangian.
  • Applying the smooth heat kernel asymptotics to polyhedral spaces without adding the dihedral angle terms.
  • Misidentifying the canonical relation of the spectral projector as only the conormal of the diagonal; the flow‑out from the singular strata must be included.
  • Overlooking that the Fredholm weight condition must be checked on every stratum simultaneously, not just on the deepest one.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Polyhedral Singularities

The spectral projector Πλ = 1(−∞,λ](Δ) on a compact polyhedral space M is a pseudodifferential operator of order zero in the polyhedral calculus of Mazzeo–Melrose. Its Schwartz kernel is a paired Lagrangian distribution associated to the pair (Λ₀, Λ₁) where Λ₀ is the conormal bundle of the lifted diagonal in the polyhedral resolution M²h,poly and Λ₁ is the flow‑out of the characteristic variety of Δ from the singular strata. On Λ₀ the principal symbol equals the Heaviside function of the principal symbol of Δ − λ; on Λ₁ it satisfies a transport equation that encodes diffraction of singularities at the polyhedral edges. The microlocal Weyl law for the counting function N(λ) = Tr Πλ follows by integrating this symbol over the cosphere bundle of the polyhedral cotangent bundle. The leading term is (2π)−n vol({|ξ|g ≤ √λ}) and the second term involves the total mean curvature of the singular strata weighted by the solid angles of the polyhedral links. Sharp remainder estimates are obtained from the propagation of singularities theorem for the wave equation on polyhedral spaces, which shows that the wave front set of the wave kernel propagates along broken bicharacteristics that reflect and diffract at the edges according to the laws of geometric optics on polyhedra. These results give a complete microlocal description of the spectrum of the Laplacian on polyhedral spaces and provide the spectral side of the trace formula for the wave group. They also imply the Hörmander spectral cluster estimates for eigenfunctions on polyhedral manifolds, which are crucial for applications to quantum ergodicity and to the control theory of the wave equation on singular domains.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Non‑Smooth Symbols

When the symbol a(x,ξ) of a pseudodifferential operator belongs to a Hölder–Zygmund class CsSm with s > 0 but not necessarily smooth, the standard symbolic calculus must be replaced by a paradifferential calculus à la Bony. The key tool is the Bony decomposition a = Ta + R where Ta is the paraproduct operator with symbol a and R is a smoothing remainder of order m − s. For a Cauchy problem ∂tu = Au, u(0)=u₀ with A = Op(a) of real principal type, one constructs a parametrix as a Fourier integral operator with a non‑smooth phase solving the eikonal equation in the sense of viscosity solutions. The loss of derivatives is precisely s derivatives: if u₀ ∈ Hσ then the solution satisfies u ∈ C([0,T]; Hσ−s). This result, due to Tataru and later refined by Smith–Tataru, is sharp; counterexamples show that any smaller loss fails. The proof uses a microlocal energy estimate with a weight adapted to the non‑smooth Hamiltonian flow, together with a commutator argument that exploits the almost orthogonality of the paradifferential pieces. An important corollary is the well‑posedness of the Cauchy problem for quasilinear wave equations with rough coefficients, where the principal symbol depends on the solution itself and inherits only the regularity of the solution.

Worked Example: Loss of Derivatives for a Non‑Smooth Principal Symbol
Consider the operator A = a(x,Dx) on ℝⁿ with symbol a(x,ξ) = |ξ| + b(x)·ξ, where b ∈ C0,α(ℝⁿ) for some 0 < α < 1. Determine the optimal loss of derivatives for the Cauchy problem ∂tu = Au, u(0)=u₀ ∈ Hσ.
The principal symbol is a₁(x,ξ) = |ξ| + b(x)·ξ. Since b is only Hölder continuous of exponent α, the symbol belongs to CαS1. By the Tataru–Smith theorem the loss of derivatives equals the Hölder exponent α. Hence the solution satisfies u ∈ C([0,T]; Hσ−α) and this loss is optimal. The proof proceeds by writing a = Ta + R with R ∈ Op(S1−α), constructing a parametrix for the paradifferential part Ta using a non‑smooth phase, and estimating the remainder by standard energy methods. The sharpness follows from a counterexample of Colombini–De Giorgi where a symbol in CαS1 yields exactly α derivatives loss.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the loss of derivatives in a Cauchy problem with a non‑smooth symbol, first identify the regularity class of the symbol (e.g. CsSm). The loss is exactly s derivatives for real principal type operators. Remember to mention the paradifferential reduction and the role of the Bony decomposition; examiners award marks for citing Tataru (1998) and Smith–Tataru (2002).

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Flat Spacetimes

On an asymptotically flat Lorentzian manifold (M,g) satisfying the Einstein vacuum equations, the scattering matrix S(λ) for the wave operator □g at frequency λ > 0 is a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow on the null cone at infinity. The work of Vasy, Baskin–Vasy, and Hintz shows that S(λ) admits a polyhomogeneous expansion at the radial sets corresponding to the event horizon and null infinity. The principal symbol of S(λ) encodes the classical scattering map: it is the exponential of the integral of the subprincipal symbol along null bicharacteristics. Trapped null geodesics (e.g. photon spheres) produce a paired Lagrangian structure in the kernel of S(λ), leading to a loss of regularity in the resonance expansion. The resonances of □g are the poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (□g − λ²)⁻¹; their distribution is governed by the fractal Weyl law with exponent determined by the dimension of the trapped set. In the Kerr family, the presence of a degenerate trapped set at the ergosphere modifies the exponent and introduces logarithmic corrections. These microlocal results underpin the proof of Price’s law for linear waves on black hole backgrounds and provide the linear input for the nonlinear stability of Minkowski space.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Non‑Compact Boundary

Let M be a manifold with boundary ∂M that is non‑compact but of bounded geometry. The Boutet de Monvel calculus extends to this setting by replacing the model half‑space ℝⁿ₊ with a uniform half‑space bundle over ∂M. Operators are matrices (A₊, K, T, Q) where A₊ is a pseudodifferential operator on the interior, K a Poisson operator, T a trace operator, and Q a pseudodifferential operator on the boundary. The symbol hierarchy now includes an interior principal symbol σψ(A₊), a boundary principal symbol σ∂(A₊,K,T,Q) living on the compressed cotangent bundle of ∂M, and a corner symbol capturing the behaviour at infinity of the boundary. Fredholm criteria require ellipticity of all three symbols and the Shapiro–Lopatinskii condition uniformly in the boundary variables. The index of such an operator is given by a relative index formula involving the η‑invariant of the induced boundary operator and a bulk term expressed as an integral of a characteristic form over M. This framework, developed by Schrohe, Schulze, and Seiler, applies to the Dirac operator on asymptotically cylindrical manifolds and to the ∂̅‑Neumann problem on non‑compact CR manifolds.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Non‑Product Type Singularities

Consider a stratified space X whose singular strata are modelled on cones C(Z) where the link Z is a compact manifold without boundary but the metric near the stratum is not a product; instead it is a warped product dr² + r² gZ(r) with gZ(r) depending smoothly on r. The resolvent R(λ) = (Δ − λ²)⁻¹ for the Laplacian on X is analysed using the iterated edge calculus of Mazzeo. The kernel of R(λ) is a polyhomogeneous distribution on the iterated edge double space X²ie with index sets determined by the indicial roots of the model operator on each stratum. The leading singularity at the lifted diagonal is the Euclidean resolvent kernel on the model cone; away from the diagonal the kernel is conormal to the lifted boundary faces. The meromorphic continuation of R(λ) to the complex plane is obtained by a contour deformation argument that uses the absence of embedded eigenvalues for non‑product type metrics. The scattering matrix S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator associated to the geodesic flow on the link Z, but its canonical relation acquires a paired Lagrangian component when the warping function creates trapped geodesics in the link. The resonance counting function satisfies a Weyl law with a remainder estimate that reflects the fractal dimension of the trapped set. These results, due to Guillarmou, Hassell, and Vasy, provide the microlocal foundation for the spectral theory of the Laplacian on spaces with non‑product singularities such as asymptotically complex hyperbolic manifolds.

  • Confusing the polyhedral calculus with the iterated edge calculus: the former uses polyhedral links, the latter uses smooth fibred links.
  • Forgetting that the boundary spectrum is the set of real parts of indicial roots, not the roots themselves.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix is always a standard Fourier integral operator; trapped geodesics force a paired Lagrangian structure.
  • Neglecting the Maslov index contribution when computing the principal symbol on a caustic Lagrangian.
  • Applying the smooth heat kernel asymptotics to polyhedral spaces without adding the dihedral angle terms.
  • Misidentifying the canonical relation of the spectral projector as only the conormal of the diagonal; the flow‑out from the singular strata must be included.
  • Overlooking that the Fredholm weight condition must be checked on every stratum simultaneously, not just on the deepest one.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Cauchy Problem for Pseudodifferential Operators with Non‑Smooth Coefficients and the Paradifferential Approach

When the coefficients of a pseudodifferential operator belong to a Hölder or Besov space rather than C∞, the standard symbolic calculus breaks down because the composition formula requires derivatives of the symbol. The paradifferential calculus of Bony replaces the operator by a paradifferential operator Ta whose symbol a(x,ξ) depends on x only through a Littlewood–Paley projection, making it locally constant in x at each frequency scale. For a symbol a ∈ Γmρ with ρ > 1/2, the commutator [Ta, Tb] gains one derivative in the sense of Sobolev spaces, and the remainder is of order m1 + m2 − ρ. This gain is the key to proving well‑posedness for nonlinear equations with rough coefficients. In the Cauchy problem for ∂tu = Ta(t,x,ξ)u + f with a ∈ C0([0,T]; Γ1ρ), ρ > 1/2, one constructs a parametrix as a paradifferential Fourier integral operator associated to the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol. The flow is Lipschitz in x and C1 in ξ, so the canonical relation is a Lipschitz Lagrangian manifold. The error terms are controlled by the paradifferential remainder estimates, yielding existence and uniqueness in C0([0,T]; Hs) for s > 1/2 + 1/(2ρ). The wavefront set of the solution propagates along the bicharacteristic flow in the sense of Filippov, and the loss of derivatives is exactly 1 − ρ. This framework, developed by Métivier, Taylor, and Smith, extends to quasilinear symmetric hyperbolic systems where the principal symbol depends on the solution itself, provided the solution remains in a Besov space Bs2,∞ with s > n/2 + 1.

The Microlocal Structure of the Scattering Matrix for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Minkowski Spacetimes

Let (M,g) be a Lorentzian manifold that coincides with Minkowski space outside a compact set. The wave operator □g admits a well‑defined scattering matrix S(λ) mapping incoming radiation fields on past null infinity ℐ− to outgoing fields on future null infinity ℐ+. Following the work of Friedlander, Melrose, and Sá Barreto, S(λ) is a Fourier integral operator of order zero associated to the canonical relation given by the geodesic flow from ℐ− to ℐ+. When the trapped set is normally hyperbolic, the canonical relation is a smooth Lagrangian submanifold of T*ℐ− × T*ℐ+ and S(λ) is a standard FIO with a principal symbol given by the square root of the Jacobian of the scattering map. If the trapped set contains a hyperbolic trapped orbit, the canonical relation develops a fold singularity and S(λ) becomes a paired Lagrangian distribution associated to the pair (Λ0, Λ1) where Λ0 is the flow‑out of the diagonal and Λ1 is the conormal bundle of the caustic. The principal symbol on Λ1 carries a Maslov factor eiπμ/4 where μ is the Maslov index of the trapped orbit. The resonances of □g are the poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (□g − λ2)−1 from Im λ > 0 to ℂ; they coincide with the poles of S(λ). The resonance counting function N(r) satisfies a fractal Weyl law N(r) ≤ C rδ+1 where δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set intersected with the energy surface. In the non‑trapping case, S(λ) is a pseudodifferential operator on the sphere at infinity and the resonances lie in a logarithmic neighbourhood of the real axis. The high‑energy asymptotics of S(λ) are governed by the geometric optics construction: the principal symbol is the product of the transmission and reflection coefficients along each broken geodesic, and the subprincipal symbol encodes the Maslov index and the integral of the subprincipal symbol of □g along the geodesic.

Worked Example: Principal Symbol of the Scattering Matrix on a Simple Asymptotically Minkowski Space
Consider a metric g = η + h where η is the Minkowski metric and h is a smooth, compactly supported perturbation. Let γ be a null geodesic of g that enters the perturbation region at past null infinity with direction ω− ∈ S2 and exits at future null infinity with direction ω+ ∈ S2. Compute the principal symbol of the scattering matrix S(λ) at the point (ω−, ω+) in terms of the linearised scattering map.
The canonical relation C ⊂ T*ℐ− × T*ℐ+ is the graph of the scattering map χ: T*ℐ− → T*ℐ+. Since the perturbation is compactly supported, χ is a symplectomorphism. The principal symbol σpr(S(λ)) is a half‑density on C given by |det dχ|1/2 times a Maslov factor. In coordinates (ω, η) on T*S2, the scattering map is χ(ω−, η−) = (ω+, η+). The linearised map dχ is a 4×4 symplectic matrix. The principal symbol is σpr(S(λ))(ω−, η−) = |det(∂ω+/∂ω−)|1/2 eiπμ(γ)/4, where μ(γ) is the Maslov index of the geodesic γ. For a simple perturbation without conjugate points, μ(γ) = 0 and the symbol reduces to the square root of the Jacobian of the angular scattering map.
Exam Strategy: Identifying the Canonical Relation of a Scattering Matrix
When asked to describe the microlocal structure of a scattering matrix, first determine whether the trapped set is empty, normally hyperbolic, or contains degenerate orbits. Empty trapped set → S(λ) is a pseudodifferential operator. Normally hyperbolic trapped set → S(λ) is a standard Fourier integral operator associated to a smooth canonical relation. Degenerate trapped orbits (glancing, hyperbolic with tangencies) → S(λ) is a paired Lagrangian distribution. Always check for fold or cusp singularities in the projection of the canonical relation to the base; these dictate the Maslov index and the order of the paired Lagrangian components.

The Microlocal Theory of Pseudodifferential Operators on Manifolds with Boundary and the Transmission Property

The Boutet de Monvel calculus handles boundary value problems by adjoining trace and Poisson operators, but a more intrinsic approach characterises those pseudodifferential operators on a manifold with boundary that preserve the space of smooth functions up to the boundary. An operator A ∈ Ψm(M) has the transmission property if its symbol a(x,ξ) in boundary normal coordinates (xn, x′, ξn, ξ′) satisfies the symmetry condition ∂αξn∂βxna(x′,0,ξ′,−ξn) = (−1)|α|+|β|∂αξn∂βxna(x′,0,ξ′,ξn) for all α,β. Equivalently, the symbol is even in ξn when xn = 0 after subtracting the Taylor series in xn at xn = 0. Operators with the transmission property map C∞(M) to C∞(M) and Hs(M) to Hs−m(M) for all s ∈ ℝ. The calculus of such operators, developed by Hörmander and Grubb, is closed under composition and adjoints. The principal symbol is the restriction of the interior symbol to the boundary, and the subprincipal symbol acquires a boundary correction term involving the normal derivative of the principal symbol. For the Dirichlet realisation of an elliptic operator A with the transmission property, the Calderón projector is a pseudodifferential projection on the boundary whose symbol is the projection onto the Cauchy data space of solutions of (A − λ)u = 0 that are smooth up to the boundary. The Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map is then a pseudodifferential operator of order 1 on the boundary with principal symbol given by the square root of the principal symbol of A in the normal direction. This framework avoids the matrix structure of the Boutet de Monvel calculus and is particularly suited for the analysis of the heat kernel and the η‑invariant on manifolds with boundary.

The Microlocal Analysis of the Resolvent for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Conic Singularities and the Friedrichs Extension

Let X be a manifold with an isolated conic singularity, modelled on the cone C(Z) = (0,∞)r × Z with metric dr2 + r2gZ. The Laplacian Δ on X is essentially self‑adjoint on C∞c(X∘) if and only if the link Z has dimension ≥ 2 and the first eigenvalue of the spherical Laplacian on Z satisfies λ1 ≥ (n−2)2/4. When this fails, one must choose a self‑adjoint extension; the Friedrichs extension corresponds to the quadratic form closure of the Dirichlet integral. The resolvent R(λ) = (ΔF − λ2)−1 for the Friedrichs extension is analysed using the b‑calculus of Melrose. The kernel of R(λ) lifts to a polyhomogeneous distribution on the b‑double space X2b with index sets determined by the indicial roots of the model operator Δcone − λ2 on the cone. The indicial roots are γ = −(n−2)/2 ± √((n−2)2/4 + λk) where λk are the eigenvalues of the spherical Laplacian. The Friedrichs condition selects the root with the larger real part at each eigenvalue. The leading singularity at the lifted diagonal is the Euclidean resolvent kernel on the cone; away from the diagonal the kernel is conormal to the lifted boundary faces. The scattering matrix S(λ) is a pseudodifferential operator on Z of order zero whose principal symbol is the identity, but its subprincipal symbol encodes the choice of self‑adjoint extension. The meromorphic continuation of R(λ) to the complex plane has poles at the resonances, which are the eigenvalues of the non‑self‑adjoint operator obtained by complex scaling the radial variable r → r eiθ. The resonance counting function satisfies a Weyl law with exponent n, but the remainder estimate reflects the presence of the conic singularity through the contribution of the indicial roots. When the cone angle is rational, the resonances accumulate on rays in the complex plane; for irrational angles they are distributed more uniformly. These results, due to Cheeger, Melrose, and Wunsch, provide the microlocal foundation for the spectral theory of the Laplacian on spaces with conic singularities and underpin the proof of the Hodge theorem for the Friedrichs extension.

The Microlocal Structure of the Spectral Projector for the Laplacian on Manifolds with Boundary and the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann Map

Let M be a compact manifold with smooth boundary ∂M and let Δ be the Laplacian with Dirichlet boundary conditions. The spectral projector Π(λ) = 1(−∞,λ](Δ) is a Fourier integral operator whose canonical relation is the union of the conormal bundle of the diagonal in M∘ × M∘ and the flow‑out of the conormal bundle of the boundary diagonal in ∂M × ∂M under the billiard flow. The billiard flow is the Hamiltonian flow of the principal symbol |ξ|g reflected at the boundary according to the law of geometric optics: the tangential component of ξ is preserved and the normal component changes sign. The kernel of Π(λ) has a singularity along the diagonal of order n, and along the reflected diagonal of order n−1/2. The Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map N(λ) = ∂νGD(λ)|∂M, where GD(λ) is the Dirichlet Green’s function, is a pseudodifferential operator on ∂M of order 1 with principal symbol √(λ − |ξ′|2). Its subprincipal symbol involves the mean curvature of ∂M and the second fundamental form. The wave trace Tr cos(t√Δ) has singularities at the lengths of periodic billiard trajectories. The Duistermaat‑Guillemin trace formula for manifolds with boundary includes a boundary contribution given by the integral over the boundary of the symbol of the billiard flow. The spectral counting function N(λ) satisfies Weyl’s law N(λ) ∼ (2π)−n vol(B*M) λn/2 with a second term of order λ(n−1)/2 proportional to the volume of the boundary. The remainder estimate is O(λ(n−1)/2) under the non‑periodicity condition on the billiard flow. When the billiard flow has a clean periodic orbit, the remainder exhibits a logarithmic singularity and the spectral projector acquires a paired Lagrangian structure along the flow‑out of the periodic orbit. The microlocal analysis of the spectral projector on manifolds with boundary, developed by Ivrii, Melrose, and Taylor, is the foundation for the proof of the Weyl law with sharp remainder and for the study of quantum ergodicity for billiards.

  • Confusing the transmission property with the Boutet de Monvel calculus: the former is a condition on the symbol of a single operator, the latter is a matrix calculus for boundary value problems.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on an asymptotically Minkowski space is always a standard FIO; hyperbolic trapped orbits force a paired Lagrangian structure with a Maslov index contribution.
  • Forgetting that the Friedrichs extension on a conic manifold selects the larger indicial root, not the smaller one; the smaller root corresponds to the Krein–von Neumann extension.
  • Neglecting the boundary contribution in the Duistermaat‑Guillemin trace formula for manifolds with boundary; the billiard flow reflection law must be used, not the geodesic flow on the double.
  • Misidentifying the canonical relation of the spectral projector on a manifold with boundary as only the conormal of the diagonal; the flow‑out from the boundary under the billiard flow is essential.
  • Overlooking that the paradifferential gain of derivatives requires ρ > 1/2; for ρ ≤ 1/2 the commutator estimate fails and well‑posedness may be lost.
  • Assuming the resonances on a conic manifold always satisfy a standard Weyl law; rational cone angles produce ray accumulation and irrational angles produce uniform distribution, affecting the remainder estimate.
